I, like other children, having no notions as to the multiplication of the human race, firmly believed that babies were imported packed up in boxes like a cargo of hardware. Thus, gazing for the first time at my master’s daughter, I argued that so lovely a being could not have come from the same factory as the rest of us, that is to say from Paris or from England, and I remained convinced that there must be some enchanted region where heaven-sent workmen were employed in making these choicer and lovelier specimens of humanity. Both of us being children, though in different ranks of life, we were soon on those terms of mutual confidence which were natural to our years, and my greatest joy was in playing with her, submitting to all her vagaries and insolence, which is not saying a little, for our relative position was never lost sight of in our games; she was always the young lady and I always the servant, so that I got the worst of it when slaps were going, and I need not say who was the sufferer.

My highest dream of happiness was to be allowed to fetch her from school, and when, by some unforeseen accident, someone else was entrusted with this delightful duty I was so deeply distressed that I honestly thought there could be no greater grief in life, and would say to myself: “It is impossible that I should ever be more miserable when I am a man grown.” My greatest delight was to climb the orange-tree in the courtyard to pick the topmost sprays of blossom; I felt myself at a height far above the greatest king on earth when seated on his throne, and I can remember no pleasure to be compared to that of being obliged to capture her in that divinely rapturous game known as hide and seek. If she ran like a gazelle I flew like a bird to catch her as soon as possible, seizing her by the first part of her dress or person that I could lay my hand on. When we changed parts, when she was the pursuer and I was to be caught, the innocent delight of the blissful game was doubled, and the darkest and dingiest hole in which I might hide, breathlessly awaiting the grasp of her imprisoning hands, was to me a perfect paradise. And I may honestly say that during these happy games I never had a thought or a feeling that did not emanate from the purest and most loyal idealism.

===Chinese===

罗西塔出落得异常标致。她当年有多标致，我记得清清楚楚，可要描摹她的眉眼，我却觉得为难。我仿佛此刻还看见她朝我微笑；她面容上那副奇特的表情，我在旁人脸上从未见过，就那样驻留在我的心间——它在我眼前浮现得那样清晰分明——就像那些与生俱来的观念，仿佛是随我们从前生带来的，或是我们还在摇篮里时，被某种神秘的力量印上心头的。然而我描不出它来，因为当年真实可触的东西，如今在我脑中只剩一段朦胧的理想；而世上再没有什么比心爱的理想更迷人，也再没有什么能像它这样，全然躲开一切分门别类的描摹。

我刚进这个家门的时候，以为罗西塔属于某种更高一等的人；我把我的感想说得再透彻些，好让您掂量掂量我是何等懵懂。我们小时候，家里添了小娃娃，大人们总爱哄我们说，娃娃是从法国、巴黎或是英国来的。我跟别的孩子一样，对人类如何繁衍毫无概念，便坚信娃娃都是装在箱子里运来的，同一批五金货没什么两样。于是，我头一回端详主人的女儿时，便暗自论定：这么可爱的一个人儿，决不可能跟我们大家出自同一家工厂——也就是说，决不是巴黎或英国出品的——而且我始终坚信，世上必定有个着了魔法的国度，那里的工匠承天之命，专造这些更精选、更可爱的人间样本。我们两个都是孩子，虽说身份悬殊，很快便亲热起来，好到我们那个年纪自然会有的那种无话不谈的地步；我最大的乐事就是陪她玩，由着她任性胡闹、作威作福——这可不是小事，因为我们玩游戏时从不曾忘掉各自的身份：她永远是小姐，我永远是仆人，所以一旦动起巴掌来，吃亏的总是我；而挨打的又是谁，就不必我说了。

我最高的幸福之梦，就是获准去学校接她回家；一旦碰上什么意外，这桩美差交到了别人手里，我便伤心透顶，老老实实地以为人生再没有比这更大的悲痛，还常对自己说：“等我长成了大人，也不可能比这更苦了。”我最大的快活，是爬上院子里的橘子树，去摘树梢最高处带花的枝条；那时我觉得自己所在的高处，远远高过世上最伟大的君王坐上宝座；而人间还有哪种快乐，比得上在那场名叫捉迷藏、令人如醉如痴的游戏里，不得不由我把她捉住？她若像瞪羚般飞跑，我便像鸟儿般飞扑过去，尽快追上她，一把抓住我够得着的她的衣裙或身上的什么地方。轮换角色的时候，她做追捕者，我做被追的，这场幸福游戏的纯真乐趣便加倍了；哪怕是最黑最脏的窟窿，只要我能在里头屏住呼吸，等候她那双囚人的手把我一把擒住，在我看来也是十足的天堂。我可以老老实实地说，在这些幸福的游戏里，我心里没有一个念头、一丝情绪，不是发源于最纯粹、最忠贞的理想主义。

===Original===

Then her singing! From the time when she was quite little she used to sing the popular airs of Andalusia with the ease of a nightingale, which knows all the secrets of song without having been taught. All the neighbors admired her wonderful facility and would come to listen to her, but to me their applause and admiration were an offence; I could have wished her to sing to no one but me. Her singing was a sort of melancholy warbling, qualified by her fresh childlike voice. The air, which repeated itself with complicated little turns and trills like a thread of sound, seemed to be lost in distant heights and then to come back to earth again on the low notes. It was like the song of the lark as it rises towards heaven and suddenly comes down to sing close in our ears; the spirit of the hearer seemed to expand as it followed the voice, and then to contract again, but always following the swing of the melody and feeling the music to be inseparable from the sweet little singer. The effect was so singular that to me it was almost painful to hear her, particularly in the presence of others.

We were, as I have said, of about the same age, she being eight or nine months older than I was. But I was stunted and puny while she was well grown and vigorous, and at the end of my three years’ residence in the house she looked much the elder of the two. These three years slipped by without our either of us suspecting that we were growing up; our games went on without interruption, for she was much livelier by nature than I, though her mother would scold her, trying to keep her in order and make her study﻿—in which, however, she did not always succeed. At the end of these three years, however, my adored young mistress was a woman grown; her figure was round and well formed, giving the finishing touch to her beauty; her face had a tenderer blush, a softer form, a gentler look; her large eyes were brighter but their glance was less restless and eager; her gait was more sober; her movements were, I cannot say lighter nor less light, but certainly different, though I could not, either then or now, define in what the difference lay. But no change struck me so much as that in her voice, which acquired a gravity and depth very unlike the shrill gay tones in which she had been wont to call me, bewildering my common sense and making me leave my various duties to join in her games. The bud, in short, had become a rose, the chrysalis was transformed into a butterfly.

Then, one day﻿—one dreadful, dismal day﻿—my young mistress appeared before me in a long dress. This alteration made such an impression on me that I could not speak a word the whole day. I felt like a man who has been cruelly imposed upon, and I was so vexed with her that in my secret soul I found fifty reasons for seriously resenting her rapid development. A perfect fever of argumentativeness was fired in my brain, and I debated the matter with myself in the most fervent manner during my sleepless nights. The thing that utterly confounded me was that the addition of a few yards of stuff to her skirts seemed altogether to have altered her character. That day﻿—a thousand times unblessed﻿—she spoke to me with the greatest formality, ordering me coldly and even repellently to do all the things I least liked doing﻿—and she, who had so often been my accomplice and screen in idleness, now reproved me for it! and all this without a smile, or a skip, or a glance!﻿—No more running, no more songs, no more hiding for me to find her, no making believe to be cross ending in a laugh﻿—not a squabble, not even a slap from her sweet little hand! It was a terrible crisis in my life﻿—she was a woman and I was still a child!

===Chinese===

还有她的歌喉！她还在很小的时候，就能唱安达卢西亚（Andalusia）的流行小调，轻松自如得像一只夜莺——夜莺未经谁教，便懂得歌唱的全部奥秘。左邻右舍都赞叹她这份天生的本事，常常来听她唱；可他们的掌声和赞美在我听来却像冒犯；我巴不得她只唱给我一个人听。她的歌是一种带忧郁的啭鸣，又让那副清亮的孩子嗓音添了几分柔和。那曲调带着繁复的小回旋和颤音，像一根细线似的绕来绕去，仿佛在高远的天际消散，又顺着低音落回地面。它像云雀的歌，一路升上天去，又忽然俯冲下来，贴着我们的耳朵吟唱；听歌人的心神随着歌声舒张开来，继而又收拢，却始终追着旋律的起伏，觉得那音乐同这位可爱的小歌者是分不开的。那效果如此奇特，以至于我听她唱歌几乎感到痛苦，尤其当着旁人的面。

正如我说过的，我们年纪相仿，她比我大八九个月。可我生得矮小孱弱，她却发育得高大健壮；我在这个家里住满三年的时候，她看上去已俨然是两人中的姐姐了。这三年不知不觉地溜了过去，我们谁也没有察觉自己正在长大；游戏照旧玩个不停，因为她天性比我活泼得多，尽管她母亲常骂她，想管束她、逼她用功——不过并不总能如愿。然而三年期满，我敬爱的小姐已是长成的女子了：她身姿圆润匀称，为她的美貌添上点睛的一笔；她的脸泛出更柔嫩的红晕，轮廓更柔和，眼神更温存；她那双大眼睛更亮了，目光却少了往日的急切躁动；步态更端庄了；她的举止，我说不上是更轻快还是更持重了，反正肯定是变了，虽然不管当时还是如今，我都说不清变在哪里。但最叫我震动的是她嗓音的变化：那嗓音添了一种庄重和深沉，同她从前唤我时那种尖亮欢快的调子大不一样——从前那嗓音搅得我心神发昏，丢下各样活计去跟她游戏。总之，花苞开成了玫瑰，蛹化成了蝴蝶。

后来有一天——一个可怕而阴惨的日子——我的小姐穿着长裙出现在我面前。这一变故给我的刺激这样深，害得我一整天说不出一句话。我觉得自己像一个被人狠狠哄骗了的人，心里对她气恼极了，暗地里找出了五十条理由，要认真怨恨她这飞快的长大。一场争辩的热病在我脑子里烧起来，在那些失眠的夜里，我用最热烈的方式独自把这件事辩了个遍。最叫我张皇失措的是：她裙子上不过添了几码料子，竟仿佛把她的性子整个儿改掉了。那一天——一千次可诅咒的一天啊——她同我说话一板一眼、客气到极点，冷冰冰甚至带着嫌弃地支使我去做那些我最不爱做的差事——而她，从前不知多少回同我串通一气、替我打掩护一起偷懒的，如今反倒为此责备我！而且这一切不带一丝笑，不蹦一下，不瞟一眼！——再也没有奔跑，再也没有歌声，再也没有她藏起来让我去找，再也没有假装怄气、末了一笑收场——连拌嘴都没有了，甚至连她那只可爱小手的巴掌也再挨不着了！这是我生平一场可怕的危机——她是女人了，而我还是个孩子！

===Original===

I need not say that this was an end to our pranks and games; I never again climbed the orange-tree, which henceforth blossomed unmolested by my greedy devotion, and unfolded its leaves and shed its luscious perfume at its own sweet will; we never again scampered across the courtyard, nor trotted too and from school﻿—I, so proud of my responsibility, that I would have defended her against an army if they had tried to carry her off. From that day Rosita always walked with the greatest dignity and circumspection. I often observed that as she went upstairs in front of me she took care not to show an inch, not a line, of her pretty ankles, and this systematic concealment I felt to be an insult to my dignity, for I had till lately seen a great deal more than her ankles! Bless me! I can laugh now when I remember how my heart was ready to burst over these things.

But worse misfortunes were in store. One day in the same year as that of this transformation old “Aunt” Martina, Rosario the cook, Marcial, and other members of the kitchen society were discussing something very important. I made the best use of my ears and presently gathered the most alarming hints: My young mistress was to be married. The thing seemed incredible for I had never heard of a lover. However, the parents used to arrange all these matters and the strange thing is that sometimes they did not turn out badly. A young man of good family had asked her hand, and her parents had consented. He came to the house accompanied by his relatives, who were some kind of counts or marquises with a high-sounding title. The suitor wore a naval uniform, for he served his country as a sailor, but in spite of his elegant costume he was by no means attractive. This no doubt was the impression he made on my young mistress, for from the first she manifested a great dislike to the marriage. Her mother tried to persuade her, but all in vain though she drew the most flattering picture of the young man’s excellent talents, ancient lineage and splendid wealth. The young girl was not to be convinced, and answered all these arguments with others no less cogent.

However, the sly baggage never said a word about the real reason, which was that she had another lover whom she really loved. This was a young artillery officer, Don Rafael Malespina, a fine-looking young fellow with a pleasing face. My young mistress had made his acquaintance in church, and the traitor Love had taken advantage of her while she was saying her prayers; but indeed a church has always seemed the fittest place, with its poetical and mysterious influences, for the doors of the soul to be opened for the admission of love. Malespina took to lurking round the house, in which I detected him on various occasions, and this love-affair became so much talked of in Vejer that the young naval officer came to know of it and challenged his rival. My master and mistress heard the whole story when news was brought to the house that Malespina had wounded his antagonist severely.

===Chinese===

不必说，我们的胡闹和游戏就此完结；我再没有爬过那棵橘子树，从此它开花不再受我贪婪的爱慕的侵扰，自在地舒展枝叶，随心所欲地吐着甜香；我们再没有在院子里追逐奔跑过，再没有一路小跑着往返学校——想当年，我对肩上的这份责任何等自豪，倘若真有一支军队要来把她抢走，我拼了命也要护住她。从那一天起，罗西塔走路总是端端正正、步步留意。我常注意到，她走在前面登楼时，总留神不让自己好看的脚踝露出一寸、一丝；她这样一以贯之的遮掩，在我看来是对我尊严的侮辱，因为直到不久之前，我见过的可远不止她的脚踝！天哪！如今想起我曾为这些事憋得心都要炸了，我不禁要好笑。

可是更倒霉的事还在后头。发生这场变化的同一年，有一天，老“姑妈”马蒂娜（Martina）、厨娘罗萨里奥（Rosario）、马西亚尔，还有厨房那伙人里的其他几位，正议论着一件顶要紧的事。我竖起耳朵听了个仔细，很快便听出了最叫人心惊的口风：我的小姐要嫁人了。这事听着简直难以置信，因为我从没听说她有什么情人。不过，这类事情向来都由父母做主，而奇怪的是，有时安排下来竟也不坏。一位好人家的青年向她求了亲，她父母也应允了。他由亲戚陪着登门，那些亲戚是什么伯爵侯爵之类，头衔响亮得很。这位求婚人身穿海军军服，因为他当水手为国效力；可任凭那身漂亮的制服，人却丝毫引不起人的好感。无疑，我的小姐对他也是这个印象，因为打从一开始，她就对这桩婚事显出极大的嫌恶。她母亲百般劝她，全然无用，尽管把那年轻人的出色才干、古老门第和万贯家私描画得天花乱坠。姑娘偏不信服，对这一切说辞都拿旗鼓相当的理由顶了回去。

然而，那个狡猾的小妮子对真正的缘由却只字不提——原来她另有一个情人，而且是真心爱着的。那是一位年轻的炮兵军官，堂拉斐尔·马莱斯皮纳（Don Rafael Malespina），一个英俊讨喜、面孔惹人喜爱的年轻人。我的小姐是在教堂里同他相识的，那叛徒爱情趁她祷告的当儿偷袭了她；不过说实在的，教堂凭着它诗意而神秘的氛围，向来就被视为最相宜的地方，让灵魂的门扉在那里开启，放爱情进来。马莱斯皮纳开始在宅子四周徘徊窥伺，我不止一回撞见了他；这段情事在韦赫尔传得沸沸扬扬，那位年轻的海军军官终于有了耳闻，便向情敌下了战书。等到马莱斯皮纳将对手重伤的消息传到家里，我的主人和主母才听说了全部原委。

===Original===

The scandal caused an immense commotion. My mistress’s religious feelings were so much shocked by this deed that neither she nor my master could conceal their wrath, and Rosita was their first victim. However, months went by; the wounded man got well again, and as Malespina himself was a man of birth and wealth, there were evident indications in the political atmosphere of the house that Don Rafael was about to be admitted. The parents of the wounded man gave up the suit, and those of the conqueror appeared in their place to ask the hand of my sweet young mistress. After some discussion and demur the match was agreed upon.

I remember the first time old Malespina came. He was a very tall, dry-looking man with a gaudily-colored waistcoat, a quantity of seals and ornaments hanging to his watch, and a very large sharp nose with which he seemed to be smelling everyone he talked to. He was terribly voluble and never allowed anyone else to get a word in; he contradicted everything, and it was impossible to praise anything without his saying that he had something far better. From the first I felt sure he was a vain man and utterly untruthful, and my opinion was amply justified later. My master received him with friendly politeness, as well as his son who came with him. From that time the lover came to the house every day, sometimes alone and sometimes with his father.

Now a new phase came over my young mistress. Her coolness to me was so marked that it verged on utter contempt. It made me understand clearly, for the first time, the humbleness of my condition, and I cursed it bitterly; I tried to argue with myself as to the claims to superiority of those who really were my superiors, asking myself, with real anguish of mind, how far it was right and just that others should be rich and noble and learned, while my ancestry were of such low origin; my sole fortune was my skin, and I hardly knew how to read. Seeing what the reward of my devotion was, I fully believed that there was no ambition in this wide world that I dared aspire to; and it was not till long after that I acquired a rational conviction that, by a steady and vigorous use of my own powers, I might gain almost everything I was deficient in. Under the scorn with which she treated me I lost all confidence in myself; I never dared open my lips in her presence, and she inspired me with far greater awe than her parents. Meanwhile I attentively watched all the signs of the love that possessed her; I saw her sad and impatient when her lover was late; at every sound of an approaching footstep her pretty face flushed and her black eyes sparkled with anxiety and hope. If it was he who came in she could not conceal her rapture, and then they would sit and talk for hours together; but always under the eye of Doña Francisca, for she would not have allowed the young lady to have a tête-à-tête meeting with anyone, even through iron bars.

===Chinese===

这件丑闻掀起了一场轩然大波。我主母的宗教情感被这桩事深深刺痛，她和我主人都掩不住心头的怒火，罗西塔首当其冲，成了他们泄愤的第一个牺牲品。然而几个月过去，受伤的那位养好了伤；马莱斯皮纳本人既有出身又有家财，这个家里的政治气候已明显透出风声：堂拉斐尔快要被接纳了。受伤那位的父母退了亲，得胜这边的父母便顶了上来，向我那可爱的小姐求亲。经过一番商议和踌躇，这门亲事算是定下了。

我还记得老马莱斯皮纳头一回登门的情形。他是个瘦长干瘪的高个子男人，穿着俗艳的花马甲，怀表上挂着一嘟噜图章和坠饰，还长着一只又大又尖的鼻子，同谁说话，那鼻子仿佛就要把谁嗅上一嗅。他话多得吓人，从不让旁人插进一个字；样样都要抬杠，你无论夸什么，他必定说自己有件不知好多少的。我从一开始就断定他是个虚荣透顶、极不老实的人，后来事实也充分证明了我的眼力。主人以友善的礼数接待了他，也这样接待了陪他同来的儿子。从那时起，那位情人天天上门，有时独自一人，有时跟着他父亲。

这时，我的小姐又进入了一个新阶段。她对我的冷淡那样明显，几乎到了全然轻蔑的地步。这使我头一回清清楚楚地明白了自己身份的低微，我把这身份狠狠地诅咒了一番；我在心里同自己争辩，那些真正高我一等的人究竟凭什么居上；我怀着真正的痛楚扪心自问：别人富有、高贵、有学问，而我的祖先出身这般微贱——我全部的家当就是这副皮囊，连大字也识不了几个——世道凭什么说这是正当公道的？眼见我一片忠心换来的是这般报偿，我深信在这茫茫世界上，再没有任何抱负是我敢企求的了；直到很久以后，我才理性地确信：只要执著而有力地运用自己的力量，我欠缺的东西几乎样样都能挣得来。在她投向我的轻蔑之下，我丧失了全部的自信；当着她的面我从来不敢张口，她比她的父母更叫我敬畏。与此同时，我留心观察着占据她心房的那份爱情的种种迹象；情人来迟了，我便见她愁眉不展、焦躁不安；每听到一阵走近的脚步声，她那漂亮的脸便涨红起来，黑眼睛里闪烁着焦灼与期盼。若进来的正是他，她便掩不住满心的欢喜，随后两人便坐下来说上几个钟头的话；但始终在堂娜·弗朗西斯卡的眼皮底下，因为她绝不容许小姐同什么人单独会面，哪怕隔着一道铁栏杆也不行。

===Original===

However, they carried on an extensive correspondence, and the worst of it all was that I had to be the go-between and courier. That drove me mad!﻿—The regular thing was that I should go out and meet the young gentleman at a certain place, as punctually as a clock, and he would give me a note to carry to my young mistress; having discharged this commission, she would give me one to take to him. How often have I felt tempted to burn those letters instead of delivering them. However, luckily for me, I always kept cool enough to resist this base temptation. I need hardly add that I hated Malespina; I no sooner saw him come into the house than my blood boiled, and whenever he desired me to do anything I did it as badly and sulkily as possible, wishing to betray my extreme disgust. This disgust, which to them seemed simply bad service, while to me it was a display of honest wrath worthy of a proud and noble heart, earned me many reprimands, and above all it once led my young lady to make a speech that pierced me to the heart like the thrust of an arrow. On one occasion I heard her say: “That boy is getting so troublesome that we shall have to get rid of him.”

At last the day was fixed for the wedding, and it was only a short while before that event that all I have already related took place with reference to my master’s project. It may therefore be easily understood that Doña Francisca had excellent reasons for objecting to her husband’s joining the fleet, besides her regard for his safety.

===Chinese===

不过，他们两人倒书信往来得十分频繁，而最糟的是，这传书递笺的信差非我莫属。这简直要把我逼疯了！——例行的是：我出门到某个地方去会那位年轻先生，准时得像座钟；他交给我一张便条，让我带给我的小姐；这项差事交割完毕，她又给我一封，让我捎给他。有多少回，我真想把那些信付之一炬，而不是送去。不过算我运气好，我每回都能保持足够的冷静，抵住了这个卑劣的诱惑。不必说，我恨马莱斯皮纳；一看见他走进家门，我的血就往头上涌；每逢他要我做什么事，我总做得尽量糟糕、尽量别别扭扭，好让人看出我满心的厌恶。这份厌恶，在他们眼里不过是差事办得不像样，在我心里却是正直的义愤，是一颗高傲尊贵的心应有的发作；它给我招来了许多顿训斥，尤其是有一天，害得我的小姐说出一句像利箭穿心般刺痛我的话。我听见她说道：“这孩子越来越碍事了，咱们得把他打发走。”

婚期终于定了下来；而我在前面讲的有关我主人那个计划的一切，就发生在那桩大事前不久。因此不难理解，除了挂念丈夫的安危之外，堂娜·弗朗西斯卡还有十足的理由，反对丈夫去参加舰队。

## VI / 第六章

===Original===

I remember very well that the day after the cuffing bestowed on me by Doña Francisca in her wrath at my irreverent conduct and her intense aversion to all naval warfare, I went out to attend my master in his daily walk. He leaned upon my arm, and on the other side of him walked Marcial; we went slowly to suit Don Alonso’s feeble pace and the awkwardness of the old sailor’s wooden leg. It was like one of those processions in which a group of tottering and worm-eaten saints are carried along on a shaky litter, threatening to fall if the pace of the bearers is in the least accelerated. The two old men had no energy or motive power left but their brave hearts, which still acted as truly as a machine just turned out of a workshop; or like the needle of a ship’s compass which, notwithstanding its unerring accuracy, could do nothing to work the crazy craft it served to guide! During our walk my master﻿—after having asserted, as usual, that if Admiral Córdova had only tacked to port instead of starboard the battle of “the 14th” would never have been lost﻿—turned the conversation once more on their grand project, and though they did not put their scheme into plain words, no doubt because I was present, I gathered from what they said that they intended to effect their purpose by stealth, quietly walking out of the house one morning without my mistress’s knowledge.

When we went in again indifferent matters were talked over. My master, who was always amiable to his wife, was more so, that day, than ever. Doña Francisca could say nothing, however trivial, that he did not laugh at immoderately. He even made her a present of some trifles, doing his utmost to keep her in a good humor, and it was no doubt as a result of this conspicuous complaisance that my mistress was crosser and more peevish than I had ever seen her. No accommodation was possible; she quarrelled with Marcial over heaven knows what trifle, and desired him to quit the house that instant; she used the most violent language to her husband; and during dinner, though he praised every dish with unwonted warmth, the lady was implacable and went on grumbling and scolding.

At last it was time for evening prayers, a solemn ceremony performed in the dining-room in the presence of all the household; and my master, who would not unfrequently go to sleep while he lazily muttered the Paternoster, was that evening unusually wide awake and prayed with genuine fervor, his voice being heard above all the rest. Another incident occurred which struck me particularly. The walls of the rooms were decorated with two distinct sets of prints: sacred subjects and maps﻿—the hierarchy of Heaven on one hand and the soundings all round Europe and America on the other. After supper my master was standing in the passage, studying a mariner’s chart and tracing lines upon it with his trembling forefinger, when Doña Francisca, who had gathered some hints of the plan for evasion, and who always appealed to Heaven when she caught her husband red-handed in any manifestations of nautical enthusiasm, came up behind him, and throwing up her arms, exclaimed:

===Chinese===

我记得清清楚楚：堂娜·弗朗西斯卡为着我的冒失无礼动怒，又出于她对一切海上战事的切齿痛恨，赏了我一顿巴掌；第二天，我便出门侍候主人作每日的散步。他扶着我的胳膊，另一边走着马西亚尔；我们走得极慢，好迁就堂阿隆索衰弱的脚步和老水手那条木腿的蹒跚。那情景活像某些游行的队伍：一群摇摇欲坠、虫蛀空了的圣徒像，安置在一台晃晃悠悠的彩轿上抬着走，抬轿人只要步子稍稍加快，便有倾覆之虞。两位老人身上已经没有一丝气力、一点动力，只剩两颗勇敢的心还在活动，其忠实效用一如作坊里新出炉的机器；又像船上的罗盘针，纵然指得分毫不爽，却奈何不得它所指引的那条破船！散步途中，我的主人照例先宣称了一通：当初科尔多瓦海军上将若下令向左舷抢风掉头，而不是向右舷，“十四号”那一仗断无失利之理；随后他把话头重新转到他们那桩宏伟的计划上。他们虽然没有把打算明明白白说出来——无疑是因为有我在场——可我从话里听出，他们预备悄悄行事，神不知鬼不觉地达到目的：哪天早晨悄没声地走出家门，瞒过我的女主人便是。

回家以后，谈的是些不相干的闲话。我的主人待妻子素来和气，这一天更是和气到十分。堂娜·弗朗西斯卡不论说什么话，多么琐碎也罢，他无不笑得没了节制。他甚至还送了她几样小东西，千方百计想哄她高兴；而正是由于这份招眼的迁就，我的女主人那天的恶劣脾气和别扭劲儿，比我所见过的任何一天都凶。妥协调解是断无可能的：她跟马西亚尔为天晓得的什么鸡毛蒜皮吵了一架，勒令他即刻滚出这所房子；对丈夫，她出口的字眼也是最暴烈的；用正餐时，尽管他把每道菜都破例热心地夸了一遍，这位太太依然毫不容情，咕哝责骂个不停。

终于到了晚祷的时辰——那是在饭厅里、当着全家上下举行的一项庄严仪式。我的主人平日懒洋洋地念着《天主经》，念着念着常要打盹，这天晚上却出奇地清醒，祷告得一片至诚，声音在众人之上也听得见。另出了一桩事，分外叫我留心。各屋的墙上挂着两套截然不同的印画：圣像题材和地图——一边是天国的品级，一边是欧美四周的水深测录。晚饭过后，我的主人正站在过道里，端详一张航海图，用哆嗦的食指在上面描画线条；堂娜·弗朗西斯卡对那潜逃的计划已听出几分风声，每逢撞见丈夫露出行船的狂热，她总要求告上天，这时她走到他背后，双臂一举，喊道：

===Original===

“Merciful Heaven! If you are not enough to provoke a Saint!”

“But, my dear,” my master timidly replied, “I was only tracing the course taken by Alcalá Galiano and Valdés in the schooners *Sutil* and *Mejicana* when we went to explore the straits of Magellan. It was a delightful expedition﻿—I must have told you all about it.”

“I shall come to burning all that paper trash!” cried Doña Francisca. “A plague on voyages and on the wandering dog of a Jew who invented them. You would do better to take some concern for the salvation of your soul, for the long and the short of it is you are no chicken. What a man! to be sure﻿—what a man to have to take care of!”

She could not get over it; I happened to pass that way, but I cannot remember whether she relieved her fury by giving me a thrashing and demonstrating at once the elasticity of my ears and the weight of her hands. The fact is that these little endearments were so frequently repeated, that I cannot recollect whether I received them on this particular occasion; all I remember is that my master, in spite of his utmost amiability, entirely failed to mollify his wife.

===Chinese===

“仁慈的老天爷！你这个人呀，真是圣徒也要叫你气得跳脚！”

“可是，亲爱的，”我的主人怯生生地答道，“我不过是在描阿尔卡拉·加利亚诺和巴尔德斯当年乘纵帆船*苏蒂尔号*（Sutil）和*梅希卡纳号*（Mejicana）走的那条航线——我们就是乘它们去考察麦哲伦海峡的。那是一趟美妙的远征——我准是原原本本跟你讲过了。”

“总有一天，我要把那一堆破纸统统烧掉！”堂娜·弗朗西斯卡嚷道。“瘟病找上这些出海远航，也瘟病找上那个头一个想出远航的犹太野狗！你还是多操操心你自己灵魂的得救吧，说来说去，你已经是上了年纪的人了。好一位人物！真是的——照看他这么一个人，可真够受的！”

她这口气一时消不了。我恰巧打那里经过，却记不得她当时有没有拿我出一顿打来泄愤，既证明我耳朵的弹性，也证明她手掌的分量。实在是因为这类亲热的小节目重演得太过频繁，以致我记不清这一回究竟受用了没有；我只记得，我的主人尽管和气到了十分，终究没能叫太太回嗔作喜。

===Original===

Meanwhile I have neglected to speak of Rosita; she was in a very melancholy mood, for Señor de Malespina had not made his appearance all day nor written her a note; all my excursions to the marketplace having proved vain. Evening came and with it grief fell on the young girl’s soul, for there was no hope now of seeing him till next day﻿—but suddenly, after supper had been ordered up, there was a loud knock at the door. I flew to open it, and it was he; before I opened it my hatred had recognized him.

I fancy I can see him now as he stood before me then, shaking his cloak which was wet with rain. Whenever I recall that man I see him as I saw him then. To be frankly impartial, I must say he was a very handsome young fellow, with a fine figure, good manners, and a pleasant expression; rather cold and reserved at first, grave and extremely courteous with the solemn and rather exaggerated politeness of the old school. He was dressed that evening in a frock-coat, with riding breeches and top boots; he wore a Portuguese hat and a very handsome cloak of scarlet cloth, lined with silk, which was the height of fashion with the gilded youth of that time.

As soon as he had come in I saw that something serious had happened. He went into the dining-room where all were much surprised to see him at so late an hour, for he never called in the evening; but my young mistress had hardly time to be glad before she understood that this unexpected visit was connected with some painful occasion.

===Chinese===

这中间，我还一直没顾上说罗西塔。她整天怏怏不乐，因为马莱斯皮纳先生一整天没有露面，也没有捎来一张字条；我往市集跑了一趟又一趟，全都落了空。夜晚来临，忧愁也跟着压上了这姑娘的心头，反正要见着他，总得等到明天了——不料晚饭刚吩咐上去，忽然有人砰砰打门。我飞奔去开门，正是他；门还没开，我的恨已经把他认出来了。

我如今还仿佛看见他当时站在我面前的样子，抖搂着被雨水打湿的斗篷。无论什么时候想起那个人，我看见的都是我那时看见的他。平心而论，我必须说，他是个非常英俊的青年：身材挺秀，举止得体，神色叫人看着舒服；起初颇为冷淡矜持，庄重而极有礼，带着老派那种一本正经、甚至有点过分的客气。那天晚上他穿一件长礼服，配着马裤和高筒靴；头戴葡萄牙式帽子，肩上搭一条猩红呢面、绸子里子的斗篷，漂亮极了——那正是当年镀金青年中间顶时新的行头。

他一进门，我就看出出了要紧的事。他走进饭厅，一家人见他来得这么晚，都大为诧异，因为他从不在晚间登门；可我的小姐还没来得及高兴，就已经明白，这趟不速之访是同什么伤心事连在一起的。

===Original===

“I have come to take leave of you,” said Malespina. They all sat stupefied, and Rosita turned as white as the paper on which I am writing; then she turned scarlet and then again as pale as death.

“But what has happened? Where are you going Don Rafael?” asked my mistress. I have said that Malespina was an artillery officer, but I did not mention that he was stationed at Cádiz and at Vejer only on leave.

“As the fleet is short of men,” he replied, “we are under orders to embark and serve on board ship. They say a battle is inevitable and most of the vessels are short of gunners.”

===Chinese===

“我是来向你们辞行的，”马莱斯皮纳说。一屋子人全听得呆了；罗西塔的脸白得跟我此刻写字的这张纸一样；接着她涨得通红，随即又死一般地惨白下去。

“可是出了什么事？您上哪儿去，堂拉斐尔？”我的女主人问。我说过马莱斯皮纳是炮兵军官，却没有提到，他在加的斯和韦赫尔，不过是在休假。

“舰队里缺人，”他答道，“我们奉了命令，要登舰服役。都说这一仗是免定了的，大多数舰上都缺炮手。”

===Original===

“Christ, Mother Mary and Saint Joseph!” shrieked Doña Francisca almost beside herself. “And they are taking you too? That is too much. Your duties are on land, my friend. Tell them to manage as best they may; if they want men let them find them. Upon my soul this is beyond a joke!”

“But, my dear,” said Don Alonso humbly, “do not you see that they must.﻿ …” But he could not finish his sentence, for Doña Francisca, whose cup of wrath and grief was overflowing, proceeded to apostrophize all the potentates of the earth.

“You﻿—” she exclaimed, “anything and everything seems right in your eyes, if only it is to benefit those blessed ships of war. And who, I say, who is the demon from hell who has ordered land forces on board ship? You need not tell me.﻿—It is Bonaparte’s doing. No Spaniard would have concocted such an infernal plot. Go and tell them that you are just going to be married. Come now,” she added, turning to her husband, “write to Gravina and tell him that this young man cannot join the squadron.” Then, seeing that her husband only shrugged his shoulders, she cried:

“He is of no use whatever! Mercy on me! If only I wore trousers I would be off to Cádiz and stop there till I had got you out of this mess.”

===Chinese===

“基督啊！圣母玛利亚！圣约瑟！”堂娜·弗朗西斯卡尖声叫道，几乎失了常态。“连你也要叫他们拉去？这可太岂有此理了。你的职分在陆地上，我的朋友。叫他们自己去对付；他们要人，让他们自己找去。我对天起誓，这可不是闹着玩的了！”

“可是，亲爱的，”堂阿隆索低声下气地说，“难道你看不出他们是不能不……”一句话没说完，堂娜·弗朗西斯卡那杯怒火与悲苦的酒已经满溢出来，索性把地上的大小君王挨个儿指名痛骂了一遍。

“你呀——”她嚷道，“只要是为了便宜那些该死的战舰，你眼里就什么都是对的。还有，我问你，到底是哪个地狱里的魔鬼，下令把陆军调上船去的？不用你告诉我。——这是波拿巴干的勾当。西班牙人决计调不出这样狠毒的阴谋。你去对他们说，你眼看就要成亲了。来，”她转向丈夫，接着说，“写信给格拉维纳，告诉他这个年轻人不能加入分舰队。”见丈夫只是耸了耸肩，她叫道：

“他是一点儿用处也没有！老天可怜我吧！只要我穿的是裤子，我立刻动身去加的斯，待在那儿不回来，不把你们从这摊烂泥里捞出来不算完！”

===Original===

Rosita said not a word. I who was watching her narrowly perceived how agitated she was. She never took her eyes off her lover, and if it had not been for good manners and to keep up her dignity, she would have cried and sobbed loudly to relieve the grief that was almost suffocating her.

“The soldier,” said Don Alonso, “is the slave of duty, and our young friend is required by his country to serve on board ship in her defence. He will gain glory in the impending struggle, and make his name famous by some great deed which history will record as an example to future generations.”

“Oh yes﻿—this, that and the other!” said Doña Francisca mimicking the pompous tone in which her husband had made this speech. “We know﻿—and all for what? To humor those ne’er-do-weels at Madrid. Let them come themselves to fire the cannons, and fight on their own account!﻿—And when do you start?”

===Chinese===

罗西塔一言不发。我紧盯着她，看出她心里翻腾得厉害。她的眼睛一刻也没有离开她的情人；若不是为了礼数、为了端住身份，她早就放声痛哭、号啕出来，好松一松那几乎把她憋死的悲痛了。

“军人，”堂阿隆索说，“是职责的奴仆。我们这位年轻朋友是应他的国家之召，登舰服役，为国御侮的。这场迫近的搏斗会给他带来光荣，他将立下一桩大功业，让历史把它记载下来，垂为后世的楷模，从此名扬天下。”

“哦，是啊——这个呀，那个呀！”堂娜·弗朗西斯卡学着她丈夫说这番话时那副高谈阔论的腔调。“我们都明白——可图个什么呢？图着叫马德里那帮成事不足败事有余的家伙称心如意。要放炮，叫他们自己来放；要打仗，叫他们自己去打！——那么，您哪天动身？”

===Original===

“Tomorrow morning. My leave is cut short and I am under orders to proceed at once to Cádiz.”

It would be impossible to describe the look that came into my young mistress’s face as she heard these words. The lovers looked at each other, and a long and mournful silence fell after this announcement of Malespina’s immediate departure.

“But this is not to be borne!” exclaimed Doña Francisca. “They will be calling out the peasantry next﻿—and the women too, if the whim takes them. Lord of Heaven!” she went on looking up to the ceiling with the glare of a pythoness, “I do not fear to offend Thee by saying: Curses on the inventor of ships﻿—Curses on all who sail in them, and Curses on the man who made the first cannon, with its thunder that is enough to drive one mad, and to be the death of so many poor wretches who never did any harm!”

Don Alonso looked at the young officer, expecting to read some protest in his face against these insults to the noble science of gunnery. Then he said:

===Chinese===

“明天一早。假期给截短了，我奉命即刻前往加的斯。”

这几句话入耳时，我的小姐脸上是什么神色，实在无法描写。这对恋人你望着我、我望着你；马莱斯皮纳即刻启程的消息一宣布，屋里便落下一段长长的、悲切的沉默。

“可这实在叫人忍无可忍！”堂娜·弗朗西斯卡嚷道。“下一步就该征乡下人了——只要他们一时兴起，连女人也要一道征去。上天老爷！”她接着说，两眼直瞪着天花板，活像发神谕的女巫，“我说这话不怕冒犯您：发明船只的人该受诅咒——凡乘船出海的都该受诅咒，造出第一门大炮的人也该受诅咒，那大炮的雷声足以把人逼疯，夺走过多少从没害过人的可怜虫的性命！”

堂阿隆索望了望那位年轻军官，指望从他脸上看出一点抗议，反驳这些辱没炮兵这门高贵学问的话。随后他说：

===Original===

“The worst of it is that the ships will lack material too and it would be.﻿ …”

Marcial, who had been listening at the door to the whole conversation, could no longer contain himself. He came into the room saying:

“And why should they lack material?﻿—The *Trinidad* carries 140 guns﻿—32 thirty-six pounders, 34 twenty-four pounders, 36 twelve-pounders, 18 eighty-pounders, and 10 mortars. The *Príncipe de Astúrias* carries 118, the *Santa Ana* 120, the *Rayo* 100, the *Nepomuceno*, and the *San*﻿ …”

===Chinese===

“顶糟的是，船上连物料也要缺乏，那可就……”

马西亚尔一直在门口听着这番谈话，这时再也按捺不住了。他走进屋来，说道：

“怎么会缺物料？——*圣特立尼达号*载炮 140 门——32 门三十六磅炮、34 门二十四磅炮、36 门十二磅炮、18 门八十磅炮，还有 10 门臼炮。*阿斯图里亚斯亲王号*载 118 门，*圣安娜号* 120 门，*拉约号* 100 门，还有*圣胡安·内波穆塞诺号*、圣……”

===Original===

“What business have you to interfere!” exclaimed Doña Francisca. “And what does it matter to us whether they carry fifty or eighty?” But Marcial went on with his patriotic list all the same, but in a lower voice and speaking only to my master, who dared not express his approbation. Doña Francisca went on:

“But for God’s sake, Don Rafael, do not go. Explain that you are a landsman, that you are going to be married. If Napoleon must fight, let him fight alone: let him come forward and say: ‘Here am I﻿—kill me, you English﻿—or let me kill you.’ Why should Spain be subject to his lordship’s vagaries?”

“I must admit,” said Malespina, “that our alliance with France has proved most disastrous.”

“Then why was it made? Everyone says that this Godoy is an ignorant fellow. You might think a nation could be governed by playing the guitar!”

===Chinese===

“哪里轮得到你插嘴！”堂娜·弗朗西斯卡喝道。“船上装五十门炮还是八十门，跟咱们有什么相干？”可马西亚尔照旧念他那份爱国的清单，只是压低了嗓音，单念给我的主人一个人听，而我的主人也不敢表示赞许。堂娜·弗朗西斯卡又往下说：

“可是看在上帝分上，堂拉斐尔，您别去。跟他们讲清楚，您是旱地上的人，您眼看就要成亲了。拿破仑要打，让他自己一个人打去：叫他站出来说：‘我在这儿——英国人，你们来杀我，不然就让我来杀你们。’凭什么西班牙要听凭那位大人的怪念头摆布？”

“我不得不承认，”马莱斯皮纳说，“我们同法国结的盟，到头来是最遭殃不过的一件事。”

“那当初干吗要结这个盟？人人都说这个戈多伊是个不学无术的家伙。照那样，倒好像弹弹吉他就能治理一个国家似的！”

===Original===

“After the treaty of Basle,” the young man said, “we were forced to become the enemies of the English, who defeated our fleet off Cape St. Vincent.”

“Ah! there you have it!” exclaimed Don Alonso, striking the table violently with his fist. “If Admiral Córdova had given the word to tack to port, to the vessels in front﻿—in accordance with the simplest rules of strategy﻿—the victory would have been ours. I consider that proved to a demonstration, and I stated my opinion at the time. But every man must keep his place.”

“The fact remains that we were beaten,” said Malespina. “The defeat might not have led to such serious consequences if the Spanish ministry had not signed the treaty of San Ildefonso with the French republic. That put us at the mercy of the First Consul, obliging us to support him in wars which had no aim or end but the furthering of his ambition. The peace of Amiens was no better than a truce; England and France declared war again immediately, and then Napoleon demanded our assistance. We wished to remain neutral, for that treaty did not oblige us to take any part in the second war, but he insisted on our cooperation with so much determination that the King of Spain, to pacify him, agreed to pay him a subsidy of a hundred millions of reales﻿—it was purchasing our neutrality with gold. But even so we did not get what we had paid for; in spite of this enormous sacrifice we were dragged into war. England forced us into it by seizing, without any justification, four of our frigates returning from America freighted with bullion. After such an act of piracy the parliament of Madrid had no choice but to throw the country into the hands of Napoleon, and that was exactly what he wished. Our navy agreed to submit to the decision of the First Consul﻿—nay, he was already Emperor﻿—and he, hoping to conquer the English by stratagem, sent off the combined fleets to Martinique, intending to draw off the British naval forces from the coasts of Europe. Thus he hoped to realize his favorite dream of invading Great Britain; but this clever trick only served to prove the inexperience and cowardice of the French Admiral who, on his return to Europe would not share with our navy the glory of the battle off Finisterre. Then, in obedience to the Emperor’s orders, the combined fleets were to enter Brest. They say that Napoleon is furious with the French Admiral and intends to supplant him immediately.”

===Chinese===

“巴塞尔条约（treaty of Basle）之后，”年轻人说，“我们被迫与英国人为敌，结果英国人在圣文森特角外海打败了我们的舰队。”

“啊！这就说到根儿上了！”堂阿隆索嚷道，拳头狠狠地砸在桌上。“科尔多瓦海军上将当时只要一声令下，叫前面的各舰一齐向左舷抢风掉头——这原是战略上顶起码的法则——胜利就是我们的了。我认为这一点已经彰明较著，我当年就发表过这个意见。可是人人都得守着自己的位分。”

“打输了终归是打输了，”马莱斯皮纳说。“这场败仗本不至于闹出这样严重的后果，倘若西班牙内阁没有同法兰西共和国签订圣伊尔德丰索条约的话。那条约把我们置于第一执政的股掌之上，逼着我们为他打仗，那些战争除了助长他的野心，既无目的，也无尽头。亚眠和约（peace of Amiens）不过是一场休战；英法随即再度开战，跟着拿破仑便要我们出力相助。我们本想保持中立，因为那项条约并没有规定我们在第二场战争里承担任何义务，可他执意要我们合作，态度那样坚决，西班牙国王为了安抚他，答应付给他一亿里亚尔的补助金——那等于是拿黄金收买我们的中立。可即便这样，我们也没能买得所值；纵然付出这般巨大的牺牲，我们还是被拖进了战争。是英国人把我们拖进去的：他们毫无道理地拿走了我们四艘满载金银、从美洲返航的巡防舰。遭了这样一场海盗行径，马德里的议会便再没有别的选择，只好把国家交到拿破仑手里，而这恰恰是他求之不得的。我们的海军同意听凭第一执政裁决——不，那时他已经是皇帝了——他一心想用计谋降服英国人，便把联合舰队打发去马提尼克，打算借此把英国海军的兵力从欧洲海岸引开。这样他便指望实现他心爱的迷梦：入侵大不列颠；可这一手妙计，结果只证明了那位法国舰队司令的缺乏经验和怯懦：他返回欧洲的时候，不肯让我们海军分享菲尼斯特雷角外海那一仗的荣光。随后，奉皇帝的谕令，联合舰队应当开进布雷斯特。据说拿破仑对那位法国舰队司令恼怒已极，打算立即撤换了他。”

===Original===

“But from what they say,” Marcial began, putting his oar in again, as we say, “Monsieur Corneta wants to cancel it, and is on the lookout for some action which may wipe out the black mark against him. I am only too glad, for then we shall see who can do something and who cannot.”

“One thing is certain,” Malespina went on, “the English fleet is cruising in our waters and means to blockade Cádiz. The Spanish authorities think that our fleet ought not to go out of the bay, where they have every chance of conquering the foe; but it seems that the French are determined to go out to sea.”

“We shall see,” said my master. “It cannot fail to be a glorious battle, anyway.”

===Chinese===

“可照他们的说法，”马西亚尔开了口，照我们的说法，又把桨插进水里来了，“科尔内塔先生想把这桩事搅黄，正眼巴巴地寻摸一仗，好洗掉记在他名下的黑档。我巴不得这样——到那时就见分晓，谁有真本事，谁没有。”

“有一点是确定无疑的，”马莱斯皮纳接着说，“英国舰队正在我们这一带海面游弋，存心要封锁加的斯。西班牙当局认为，我们的舰队不该驶出海湾——待在湾里，我们处处占着上风，大克制胜的机会；可看来法国人是一心一意要出海了。”

“咱们等着瞧，”我的主人说。“反正这一仗总归是一场光荣的仗。”

===Original===

“Glorious! yes.﻿ …” replied Malespina. “But who can promise that fortune shall favor us. You sailors indulge in many illusions and, perhaps from seeing things too closely, you do not realize the inferiority of our fleet to that of the English. They, besides having a splendid artillery have all the materials at hand for repairing their losses at once. As to the men, I need say nothing. The enemy’s sailors are the best in the world﻿—all old and experienced seamen, while only too many of the Spanish vessels are manned by raw recruits, indifferent to their work and hardly knowing how to serve a gun; our marines, again, are not all we could wish, for they have been supplemented by land-forces﻿—brave enough, no doubt, but certain to be seasick.”

“Well, well,” said my master, “in the course of a few days we shall know the end of it all.”

“I know the end of it all very well,” said Doña Francisca. “All these gentlemen﻿—though I am far from saying they will not have gained glory﻿—will come home with broken heads.”

===Chinese===

“光荣！是啊……”马莱斯皮纳答道。“可谁能担保运气一定眷顾我们。你们海军里的人抱着太多幻想，也许因为把事情看得太切，竟看不出我们的舰队不如英国人。人家除了一手出色的炮术，修船补损的物料也样样在手边，随坏随修。至于人，更不必我说了。敌国的水手是天下第一流的——个个是久经风浪的老手；而我们西班牙的船上，偏偏太多塞的是新募的兵，干活不上心，连伺候一门炮都不大会；再说我们的海军陆战队也不尽如人意，因为拿陆军补了员——勇气自然不缺，可一上船，包管晕得七荤八素。”

“好了好了，”我的主人说，“过不了几天，这一切的结局自然见分晓。”

“这一切的结局，我知道得再清楚不过，”堂娜·弗朗西斯卡说。“这些老爷们——尽管我绝没有说他们赢不来光荣——一个个都要头破血流地回家的。”

===Original===

“What can you know about it?” exclaimed Don Alonso, unable to conceal an impulse of vexation, which, however, lasted but a moment.

“More than you do,” she retorted sharply. “But God have you in his keeping, Don Rafael, that you may come back to us safe and sound.”

This conversation had taken place during supper, which was a melancholy meal, and after Doña Francisca’s last speech no one said another word. The meal ended, Malespina took a tender leave of them all, and as a special indulgence on so solemn an occasion the kindhearted parents left the lovers together, allowing them to bid each other adieu at their ease and unseen, so that nothing might prevent their indulging in any demonstration which might relieve their anguish. It is evident that I was not a spectator of the scene and I know nothing of what took place; but it may be supposed that no reticence on either side checked the expression of their feelings.

When Malespina came out of the room he was as pale as death; he once more bid farewell to my master and mistress, who embraced him affectionately, and was gone. When we went up to Rosita we found her drowned in tears, and her grief was so desperate that her devoted parents could not soothe her by any persuasion or argument, nor revive her energy by any of the remedies for which I was sent backwards and forwards to the apothecary. I must confess that I was so deeply grieved at the distress of these hapless lovers that my rancorous feelings against Malespina died away in my breast. A boy’s heart is easily appeased, and mine was always open to gentle and generous impulses.

===Chinese===

“这你知道什么？”堂阿隆索失声嚷道，露出一阵按捺不住的恼怒——不过那火气也只一霎就过去了。

“比你知道的多，”她尖利地顶了回去。“愿上帝保佑你，堂拉斐尔，叫你平平安安、囫囫囵囵地回到我们身边。”

这番谈话是在晚饭桌上进行的，那顿饭吃得凄凄惶惶；堂娜·弗朗西斯卡说完最后那几句，再没有一个人吭声。饭吃完了，马莱斯皮纳深情地向众人一一辞行；在这般庄重伤心的时刻，心慈的双亲特意开恩，把这对恋人留在了一处，容他们自在地、背着人互道别离，好叫什么东西都不妨碍他们尽情倾诉，发泄心头的悲痛。不用说，这一幕我没有在场，里头的情形我一概不知；不过可以想见，双方谁也没有半点忸怩，拦着他们吐露衷肠。

马莱斯皮纳走出房门的时候，脸白得像死人；他又向我主人和女主人道了一回别，两位老人亲亲热热地搂抱了他，他便去了。我们上楼去看罗西塔，只见她泡在泪水里，悲恸得那样绝望，疼爱她的爹娘百般劝解也宽慰不了，我奉命一趟趟往药铺跑，抓回来的药也没一样能叫她缓过劲儿来。我必须承认，看着这对不幸的恋人受这般煎熬，我心里难受极了，对马莱斯皮纳那点积怨，就在胸中烟消云散了。孩子的心最容易消气，而我的心向来向温柔宽厚的情意敞开着。

## VII

===Original===
The following morning had a great surprise in store for me, and my mistress was thrown into the most violent passion I suppose she can ever have known in her life. When I got up I perceived that Don Alonso was in the best of humors, and his wife even more ill-tempered than usual. While she was gone to mass with Rosita, I saw my master packing in the greatest haste, putting shirts, and other articles of clothing, and among them his uniform, into a portmanteau. I helped him and it made me suspect that he was about to steal away; still, I was surprised to see nothing of Marcial. However, his absence was presently accounted for; for Don Alonso, having made his rapid arrangements, became extremely impatient till the old sailor made his appearance, saying: "Here is the chaise. Let us be off before she comes in." I took up the valise, and in a twinkling Don Alonso, Marcial, and I had sneaked out of the back gate so as to be seen by nobody; we got into the chaise, which set off as fast as the wretched hack could draw it and the badness of the road allowed. This, which was bad enough for horses was almost impassable for vehicles; however, in spite of jolting that almost made us sick, we hurried as much as possible, and until we were fairly out of sight of the town our martyrdom was allowed no respite.

I enjoyed the journey immensely, for every novelty turns the brain of a boy. Marcial could not contain himself for joy, but my master, who at first displayed his satisfaction with even less reticence than I, became sadder and more subdued when we had left the town behind us. From time to time he would say: "And she will be so astonished! What will she say when she goes home and does not find us!"

As for me, my whole being seemed to expand at the sight of the landscape, with the gladness and freshness of the morning, and above all with the idea of soon seeing Cádiz and its matchless bay, crowded with vessels; its gay and busy streets and its creek (the *caleta*) which remained in my mind as the symbol of the most precious gift of life—liberty; its Plaza, its jetty and other spots, all dear to my memory. We had not gone more than three leagues when there came in sight two riders mounted on magnificent horses, who were fast overtaking us and before long joined us. We had at once recognized them as Malespina and his father—the tall, haggard, and chattering old man of whom I have already spoken. They were both much surprised to see Don Alonso, and still more so when he explained that he was on his way to Cádiz to join a ship. The son took the announcement with much gravity; but the father, who as you will have understood was an arrant braggart and flatterer, complimented my master in high-flown terms on his determination, calling him the prince of navigators, the mirror of sailors, and an honor to his country.

===Chinese===
第二天早晨，一桩大大的意外正等着我，而我的女主人也动了肝火，那火气之旺，我想她这辈子怕是不曾有过第二回。我起床时便察觉，堂阿隆索心情好得不能再好，他的妻子却比平日更加恶声恶气。趁她带着罗西塔望弥撒去了，我看见主人火急火燎地收拾行装，把衬衫和别的衣物——其中还有他那身军服——一股脑儿塞进一只旅行皮箱。我帮着他拾掇，心里不免疑心他打算悄悄溜走；只是没见着马西亚尔的人影，又叫我暗暗纳罕。不过他为何不在，很快就有了分晓：堂阿隆索三下两下把行装安排停当，之后便等得心焦难耐，直到老水手终于露面，说道：“马车到了。趁她还没回来，咱们快走。”我提起旅行包，眨眼之间，堂阿隆索、马西亚尔和我便从后门溜了出去，免得被任何人瞧见；我们上了那辆轻便马车，车便上路了，那匹可怜的租来驽马拉得动多快，那条烂路容得下多快，它便走得多快。那条路，马走起来已经够呛，车辆简直无法通行；然而尽管颠得我们几乎要吐，我们还是没命地赶路，直到全然出了城、再也望不见了，我们这场受难才得到片刻喘息。

这趟旅行我快活极了，因为凡是新鲜事儿都能叫男孩子神魂颠倒。马西亚尔喜得按捺不住；我的主人起初流露得意，比我还毫不遮掩，可等我们把城甩在身后，他却渐渐愁闷下来，蔫了下去。他不时念叨着：“她该多吃惊啊！她回到家里找不着我们，会说什么呢！”

至于我，望着一路的田野风光，沐着清晨的欢快与清新，尤其是想到很快就要见到加的斯，见到那挤满船只的无双海湾，那些热闹繁忙的街道，还有那道小海湾——卡莱塔（caleta）——它在我心中始终是生命最珍贵馈赠的象征：自由；还有那广场、那栈桥和别的去处，一切都叫我怀念不已。我们走了不出三里格，便望见两个骑手，跨着两匹神骏的好马，飞快地追赶我们，不多时便赶上来同行。我们一眼认出，正是马莱斯皮纳和他的父亲——就是我前面说过的那个瘦高干瘪、喋喋不休的老头儿。他们两位见了堂阿隆索都大为诧异，等他说明自己正要赶去加的斯上船，就愈发吃惊了。儿子听了这话神色郑重；那位父亲——想必诸位已经明白——却是个天字第一号的自夸大王和马屁精，用最高的调门恭维我主人的决心，称他为航海家中的亲王、水手的明镜、祖国的光荣。

===Original===
We stopped to dine at the inn at Conil. The gentlemen had what they could get, and Marcial and I eat what was left, which was not much. I waited at table and heard the conversation, by which means I gained a better knowledge of the elder Malespina, who at first struck me as a boastful liar and afterwards as the most amusing chatterbox I ever in my life met with.

Don José Malespina, my young mistress's intended father-in-law—no relation to the famous naval officer of that name—was a retired colonel of artillery, and his greatest pride was founded on his perfect knowledge of that branch of military science and on his personal superiority in the tactics of gunnery. When he enlarged on that subject his imagination seemed to gain in vividness and in freedom of invention.

"Artillery," he said, without pausing for a moment in the act of deglutition, "is indispensable on board ships of war. What is a vessel without guns? But it is on land, Señor Don Alonso, that the marvellous results of that grand invention of the human mind are seen to the best advantage. During the war in Roussillon—you know of course that I took part in that campaign and that all our successes were due to my promptness in managing the artillery.—The battle of Mas Deu—: How do you suppose that was won? General Ricardos posted me on a hill with four pieces, ordering me not to fire till he sent the word of command. But I, not taking the same view of the case, kept quiet till a column of the French took up a position in front of me, in such a way as that my fire raked them from end to end. Now the French troops form in file with extraordinary precision. I took a very exact aim with one of my guns, covering the head of the foremost soldier.—Do you see? The file was wonderfully straight.—I fired, and the ball took off one hundred and forty-two heads Sir! and the rest did not fall only because the farther end of the line swerved a little. This produced the greatest consternation among the enemy, but as they did not understand my tactics and could not see me from where they stood, they sent up another column to attack our troops on my right, and that column shared the same fate, and another and another, till I had won the battle."

"Well, señor, it was wonderful!" said my master, who, seeing the enormity of the lie, had no mind to trouble himself to contradict his friend.

"Then in the second campaign, under the command of the Conde de la Unión, we gave the republicans a very pretty lesson. The defence of Boulou was not successful because we ran short of ammunition; but in spite of that I did great damage by loading a gun with the keys of the church—however, they did not go far, and as a last and desperate resource I loaded the cannon with my own keys, my watch, my money, a few trifles I found in my pockets and, at last, with my decorations. The strange thing is that one of the crosses found its billet on the breast of a French general, to which it stuck as if it had been glued there and did him no harm whatever. He kept it, and when he went to Paris, the Convention condemned him to death or exile—I forget which—for having allowed himself to accept an order from the hand of an enemy."

"The devil they did!" said my master, highly delighted with these audacious romances.

===Chinese===
我们在科尼尔（Conil）的客店歇脚吃饭。先生们有什么吃什么，马西亚尔和我吃剩下的，所剩无几。我在旁伺候用饭，听他们谈话，借此对老马莱斯皮纳有了更深的认识：起初我只觉得他是个吹牛皮的谎精，后来才觉得他是我平生所见最逗乐的饶舌虫。

堂何塞·马莱斯皮纳（Don José Malespina）——我那小姐未来的公公，与那位同名的大名鼎鼎的海军军官并无亲故——是位退役的炮兵上校，他最大的得意，一是自认对炮兵这门军事学问精通到家，二是自诩在枪炮战术上高出众人一头。一逢他铺陈起这个题目，他的想象力便愈发鲜活，愈发无拘无束地编派起来。

“火炮，”他开口道，嘴里的吞咽动作却一刻不停，“在战舰上是万万少不得的。一条船没有炮还算什么船？可是，堂阿隆索先生，人类心智这件伟大发明的奇效，唯有在陆上方才显示得淋漓尽致。鲁西永（Roussillon）打仗那阵子——想必您知道，我参加过那一役，我们打的所有胜仗，全亏我调度炮兵的果决。——马斯德乌（Mas Deu）那一战——您猜是怎么打赢的？里卡多斯（Ricardos）将军把我摆在一座小山上，配了四门炮，命令我不得到他号令不得开火。可我呢，对形势另有看法，只按兵不动，等一纵队法国人开到我跟前占了位置，正好让我的炮火从头到尾把他们犁上一遍。要知道法军列队齐整得非同寻常。我拿一门炮瞄得极准，正对着排头那个兵的脑袋。——您明白吗？那队人排得出奇地直。——我一炮打去，削掉了一百四十二颗脑袋，先生！余下那些没倒，只因为队伍末端稍稍偏了一点。这一下敌军乱作一团；可他们摸不透我的战术，站在原地又瞧不见我，便又开上来一队攻打我右翼的我军——那一队落得同样下场，一队接着一队，直到我把这一仗打赢。”

“哎呀，先生，真是神了！”我主人说。他眼看这牛皮吹得没了边，也懒得费神去驳他的朋友。

“后来第二战役，在拉乌尼翁伯爵（Conde de la Unión）麾下，我们好好给共和党人上了一课。布卢（Boulou）的守卫战没打赢，是因为弹药告罄；可尽管如此，我拿教堂的钥匙装进炮里当弹药，也着实教敌人吃了亏——只是那些钥匙打不出多远，万般无奈之下，我把自己的钥匙、怀表、钱、口袋里摸得着的几样零碎，最后连我的勋章，统统装进了炮膛。说也奇怪，其中一枚十字章不偏不倚，正落在一个法国将军的胸口上，粘在那儿就像用胶粘住了似的，对他毫发无损。他把它留下了；等他回到巴黎，国民公会（Convention）判了他死刑还是流放——我记不清是哪个——罪名是他竟肯从一个敌人手里接受勋章。”

“竟有这等事！”我主人说，这些胆大包天的海外奇谈听得他快活极了。

===Original===
"When I was in England," continued the old soldier, "you know of course, that I was sent for by the English to make improvements in their artillery—I dined every day with Pitt, with Burke, with Lord North, Lord Cornwallis, and other distinguished personages, who always called me 'the amusing Spaniard.' I remember that once, when I was at the Palace, they entreated me to show them what a bullfight was like and I had to throw my cloak over a chair and to prick it and kill it, which vastly diverted all the court, and especially King George III, who was very great friends with me, and was always saying that I must send to my country to fetch some good olive-trees. Oh! we were on the best terms possible. All his anxiety was that I should teach him a few words of Spanish, and above all some of our beautiful Andalusian—but he could never learn more than '*otro toro*' (another bull) and '*vengan esos cinco*' (that makes five), and he greeted me with these phrases every day when I went to breakfast with him off *pescadillas*2 and a few *cañitas* of Manzanilla."

"That was what he took for breakfast?"

"That was what he preferred. I had some *pescadillas* bottled and brought from Cádiz. They kept very well by a recipe I invented and have at home."

===Chinese===
“我在英国那阵子，”老兵接着说，“您当然知道，是英国人特意把我请去改良他们的炮兵——我天天同皮特（Pitt）、伯克（Burke）、诺斯勋爵（Lord North）、康沃利斯勋爵（Lord Cornwallis）还有别的显贵共进晚餐，他们总管我叫‘有趣的西班牙人’。记得有一回我在宫里，他们恳求我表演一番斗牛是什么样子，我只得把斗篷往一把椅子上一蒙，拿扎枪又戳又刺，把它‘杀死’，逗得满朝上下乐不可支，乔治三世（George III）尤其开心——他同我交情极好，总念叨说我该打发人回本国去弄些好橄榄树来。噢！我们俩的交情再好没有。他一心惦记的，是让我教他几句西班牙话，尤其是咱们安达卢西亚那些漂亮话——可他翻来覆去只学得会‘*otro toro*’（再来一头牛）和‘*vengan esos cinco*’（这就五个了）两句，每天我去陪他用早点——吃几口*pescadillas*（炸小鱼）2，再拿*cañitas*（细苇管）吸上几口曼萨尼亚酒（Manzanilla）——他就拿这两句话向我打招呼。”

“这就是他用的早点？”

“他就好这一口。我从前把些*pescadillas*装了罐子，从加的斯运了来。靠我发明的一个方子——那方子如今还搁在我家里——它们保存得好极了。”

===Original===
"Wonderful! And you succeeded in reforming the English artillery?" asked my master, encouraging him to go on for he was greatly amused.

"Perfectly. I invented a cannon which could never be fired, for all London, including the ministers and parliament, came to entreat me not to attempt it, because they feared that the explosion would throw down a number of houses."

"So that the great gun has been laid aside and forgotten?"

"The Emperor of Russia wanted to buy it, but it was impossible to move it from the spot where it stood."

"Then you surely can get us out of our present difficulties by inventing a cannon to destroy the whole English fleet at one discharge."

"Yes," replied Malespina. "I have been thinking of it, and I believe I may realize my idea. I will show you the calculations I have made, not only with regard to increasing the calibre of guns to a fabulous degree, but also for constructing armor plates to protect ships and bastions. It is the absorbing idea of my life."

===Chinese===
“妙极了！那您把英国人的炮兵改良成了吗？”我主人问，一心逗他往下讲，因为这些胡话让他乐不可支。

“改良得十全十美。我发明了一门永远打不响的大炮，因为全伦敦——连各位大臣和议会都在内——都跑来恳求我千万别试炮，生怕那一炸会震塌一大片房子。”

“这么说，那门巨炮就搁起来，被人忘啦？”

“俄国皇帝想买它来着，可它立在哪里就动不了哪里，搬都没法搬。”

“那么您眼下准能救我们出这难关：发明一门炮，一炮就把整支英国舰队轰个精光。”

“行啊，”马莱斯皮纳答道，“我正琢磨着这件事，我看我这构想是能实现的。我要给您看看我做的计算，不光是把炮的口径加大到匪夷所思的程度，还包括造出装甲板来护卫舰船和碉堡。这是我毕生魂牵梦萦的念头。”

===Original===
By this time the meal was ended. Marcial and I disposed of the fragments in less than no time, and we set out again; the Malespinas on horseback by the side of the chaise and we, as before, in the tumbledown vehicle. The effects of the dinner, and of the copious draughts of liquor with which he had moistened it, had stimulated the old gentleman's inventive powers and he went on all the way, pouring out a flood of nonsense. The conversation returned to the subject with which it had begun, the war in Roussillon, and as Don José was preparing to relate fresh deeds of valor, my master, weary of so many falsehoods, tried to divert him to something else, by saying: "It was a disastrous and impolitic war. We should have done better never to have undertaken it."

"Oh! the Conde de Aranda, as you know," exclaimed Malespina, "condemned that unlucky war with the Republic from the first. How often have we discussed the question—for we have been friends from our childhood. When I was in Aragon we lived together for six months at Moncayo. Indeed, it was for him that I had a very curious gun constructed. …"

"Yes, Aranda was always opposed to it," interrupted Don Alonso, intercepting him on the dangerous ground of gunnery.

"So he did," said Don José to whom rodomontade was irresistible, "and I may say that when that distinguished man so warmly advocated peace with the republicans, it was because I advised it, being convinced from the first that the war was a mistake. But Godoy, who was then supreme, persisted in it, simply and solely to contradict me, as I have learned since. But the best of it is that Godoy himself was obliged to put an end to the war in 1795, when he understood what it really was, and at the same time he adopted the high-sounding title of Prince of Peace."

"How much we want a good statesman, my worthy friend," said my master. "A man on a level with the times, who would not throw us into useless wars but who could maintain the dignity of the crown."

"Well, when I was at Madrid last year," continued Don José, "proposals were made to me to accept the post of Secretary of State. The Queen was most anxious for it—the King said nothing. I went with him every day to the Prado to fire a few shots.—Even Godoy would have agreed, recognizing my superior qualifications; and indeed, if he had not I should have had no difficulty in finding some snug little fortress where I might lock him up so that he might give me no trouble. However, I declined, preferring to live in peace in my own country-town; I left the management of public affairs in Godoy's hands. There you have a man whose father was a mule-boy on my father-in-law's estate in Estremadura. …"

===Chinese===
这时饭已用罢。马西亚尔和我三口两口把残羹剩饭消灭干净，我们便重新上路；马莱斯皮纳父子骑着马，傍着马车走，我们呢，照旧坐在那辆破得要散架的车里。那顿饭，外加润饭时大口大口灌下去的烧酒，把老先生的发明之力刺激得愈发旺盛，他一路上滔滔不绝，倾泻出一大股胡话的洪流。话题又绕回了开头谈的那件事，鲁西永战争。堂何塞正要再讲几桩英勇事迹，我的主人被这一连串的谎话弄得腻烦了，想引他岔开去，便说：“那是一场灾祸，也是失策。我们压根儿就不该打那一仗。”

“噢！阿兰达伯爵（Conde de Aranda），这您知道，”马莱斯皮纳嚷道，“打头一开始就不赞成同共和国打那场倒霉的仗。我们商讨这个问题不知有多少回——我俩打小就是朋友。我在阿拉贡（Aragon）那阵子，我们还一起在蒙卡约（Moncayo）住了半年呢。说实在的，我还专门为他造过一门顶古怪的炮。……” 

“是啊，阿兰达一直反对那场仗，”堂阿隆索插嘴道，把他从枪炮这块险地半路截了回去。

“他正是反对，”堂何塞说道——大话到了他嘴边是一句也忍不住的——“我还可以说一句：那位杰出人物之所以那样热烈主张同共和党人讲和，正是因为我劝的他，因为我从头一眼就看出那仗打错了。可戈多伊当时大权独揽，偏偏打下去，据我后来得知，就是单单为了跟我抬杠。可最妙的是，到一七九五年，戈多伊自己明白这仗究竟是怎么回事了，也不得不把它收了场，同时还给自己加上了和平亲王那响亮的封号。”

“我们多么需要一位好的政治家啊，我可敬的朋友，”我主人说，“需要一个跟得上时代的人，既不把我们拖进无谓的战争，又能保住王冠的体面。”

“咳，去年我在马德里那阵子，”堂何塞接着说，“有人提议请我出任国务大臣（Secretary of State）。王后巴望得不得了——国王一声不吭。我天天陪他上普拉多大道（the Prado）去打几枪。——连戈多伊也会点头的，他认得出我资望过人；说真的，他若不答应，我也毫不费事就能寻一座舒舒服服的小要塞，把他锁在里头，省得他给我添乱。不过我谢绝了，宁可回我自己的乡镇安安静静过日子；国家大事，我就留给戈多伊去打理。您瞧瞧这么个人：他老子当年还是我岳父在埃斯特雷马杜拉（Estremadura）田庄上的赶骡娃呢。……” 

===Original===
"I did not know that. …" said Don Alonso. "Although he is a man of obscure origin I always supposed the Prince of Peace to belong to a family of good birth, whose fortune was impaired but whose ancestry was respectable."

And so the dialogue went on; Señor Malespina uttering his falsehoods as if they were gospel, and my master listening with angelic calmness, sometimes annoyed by them, and sometimes amused at listening to such nonsense. If I remember rightly, Don José María took the credit of having advised Napoleon to the bold deeds of the 18th Brumaire.

Talking of these and of other matters we reached Chiclana as night overtook us, and my master, who was utterly tired and worn out by the villainous chaise, remained in the town, while the others went on, being anxious to reach Cádiz the same night. While we were at supper Malespina poured out a fresh farrago of lies, and I could see that his son heard them with pain, as if he were horrified at having for his father the most romancing liar in the world probably. We took leave of them and rested there till next day when we proceeded on our journey by daybreak, and as the road from Chiclana to Cádiz was much easier than that we had already traversed, we reached the end of our journey by about eleven o'clock in the morning, without adventure, safe in body and in excellent spirits.

===Chinese===
“这我还不知道。……”堂阿隆索说，“虽说此人出身寒微，我一直以为和平亲王好歹生在体面人家，家道虽然中落，门第总算可敬。”

谈话就这样进行下去：马莱斯皮纳先生把谎话当作福音一般宣讲，我的主人以天使般的平静听着，有时给惹恼，有时又被这些胡话逗乐。如果我没记错，堂何塞·马里亚（Don José María）还把向拿破仑进言、教他放手大干雾月十八日那桩壮举的功劳，记在了自己头上。

谈着这些又谈着那些，夜幕降临时我们赶到了奇克拉纳（Chiclana）。我的主人被那辆作孽的破马车颠得精疲力竭，便留在城里；其余的人急于当晚赶到加的斯，继续上路了。吃晚饭时，马莱斯皮纳又倒出一锅新謷的谎话，我看得出他儿子听得满脸痛楚，仿佛一想到天底下最会编故事的谎精多半正是自己的父亲，便惊骇无地。我们同他们道了别，在那里歇宿一夜；第二天破晓继续登程。从奇克拉纳到加的斯的路比我们先前的路好走得多，到上午十一点光景，我们便到了旅程的尽头，一路平安无事，身子囫囵，兴头十足。

## VIII

===Original===
I cannot describe the enthusiasm that fired my mind at the sight of Cádiz. As soon as I had a moment to myself—as soon, that is to say, as my master was fairly settled in his cousin’s house—I went out into the streets and ran to and fro without any fixed destination, intoxicated as it were by the atmosphere of my beloved native city. After so long an absence all I saw attracted my attention as though it were something new and beautiful. In how many of the passersby did I recognize a familiar face? everything charmed me and appealed to my feelings—men, women, old folks, children—the dogs, nay the houses even; for my youthful imagination discovered in each a personal and living individuality; I felt towards them as towards intelligent creatures; they seemed to me to express, like all else, their satisfaction at seeing me, and to wear, in their balconies and windows, the expression of gay and cheerful faces. In short my spirit saw its own gladness reflected in every surrounding object.

I hurried through the streets with eager curiosity, as if I wanted to see them all at once. In the Plaza San Juan I bought a handful of sweetmeats, less for the satisfaction of eating them than for that of introducing myself under a new aspect to the sellers, whom I addressed as an old friend; some of them with gratitude as having been kind to me in my former misery and others as victims, not yet indemnified, to my childish propensity for pillage. Most of them did not remember me; some, however, received me with abusive language, bringing up the deeds of my youth against me and making ironical remarks on my new fit-out and the dignity of my appearance, reducing me to flight as quickly as possible and damaging my appearance by pelting me with the rind or husks of fruit, flung by skilful hands at my new clothes. However, as I was fully convinced of my own importance, these insults increased my pride more than they hurt my feelings.

Then I went to the ramparts, and counted all the ships at anchor within sight. I spoke to several sailors that I met, telling them that I too was about to join the fleet, and asking them with eager emphasis whether they had seen Nelson’s fleet; and then I assured them that Monsieur Corneta was no better than a coward and that the impending fight would be a grand affair. At last I reached the creek and there my delight knew no bounds. I went down to the shore and, taking off my shoes, I leaped from rock to rock; I sought out my old comrades of both sexes but I found only a few, some who were now men had taken to some better mode of living, others had been impressed into the ships, and those who were left hardly recognized me. The undulating motion of the water excited my very senses; I could not resist the temptation—urged by the mysterious spell of the sea whose eloquent murmurs have always sounded to me—I know not why—like a voice inviting me to happiness or calling me with imperious threats to rave and storm. I stripped myself as quick as thought and threw myself into the water as if I were flying to the arms of a lover. I swam about for more than an hour, happy beyond all words, and then, having dressed myself, I continued my walk to the purlieus of La Viña where, in the taverns, I came across some of the most famous rascals of my young days. In talking with them I gave myself out to be a man of position, and as such, I wasted the few *cuartos* I possessed in treating them. I asked after my uncle but no one could give me any news of that gentleman, and after we had chatted for awhile they made me drink a glass of brandy which instantly went to my head and lay me prone on the floor. During the crisis of my intoxication I thought the scoundrels were laughing at me to their hearts’ content; but as soon as I recovered a little I sneaked out of the tavern much ashamed of myself. I still had some difficulty in walking; I had to go by my own old home and there, at the door, I saw a coarse-looking woman frying blood and tripe. Much touched by recognizing the home of my childhood I could not help bursting into tears and the heartless woman, seeing this, took it for granted it was some jest or trick to enable me to steal her unsavory mess. However, I was able to take to my heels and so escape her clutches, postponing the expression of my emotion till a more favorable opportunity.

===Chinese===
一见到加的斯，我心中燃起的那股热狂，实在无从描摹。主人刚在他表亲家里安顿妥当——也就是说，我一得了空——便出门上街，漫无目的地来回奔跑，仿佛被我挚爱的故乡城郭的气息灌醉了。阔别多年之后，眼前所见无不引我注目，仿佛件件都是新鲜美妙的物事。多少迎面走过的行人身上，我都认出了熟识的面孔！样样都叫我心醉、牵动我的情肠——男人、女人、老人、孩子——连狗也不除外，甚至那些房屋也是；因为我那少年的想象力，在每一件东西上都发现出一个活生生、各有其性的生命；我对它们，就像对有灵性的生物一般相待；它们也同万事万物一样，仿佛在向我表达见着我的欢喜，在各自的阳台和窗口上，挂出一副副欢欣愉悦的容颜。总之，我的灵魂在四周每一样物体上，都照见了自己的欢畅。

我怀着热切的好奇在街上匆匆穿行，恨不得把所有的街道一眼看尽。在圣胡安广场（Plaza San Juan），我买了一捧糖果点心，倒不图吃的痛快，而图以一副新面目向卖主们自我引见——我拿老朋友的口吻同他们攀谈；对有些人心怀感激，因为当年我落魄时他们待我不薄；对另一些人，则把他们看作我孩提劫掠的癖好尚未赔偿的苦主。他们大半都不记得我了；不过也有几个，一见我就破口大骂，把我少年时的种种行迹翻出来数落，讥诮我这一身新行头、这副人模人样的气派，撵得我抱头鼠窜，一双双巧手抓起果皮果壳，专朝我的新衣裳上招呼，把我的体面糟蹋了个够。不过，我既对自己的身份地位深信不疑，这些羞辱与其说伤了我的心，不如说更添了我的骄傲。

随后我登上城垣，把望得见的锚泊船只一一数了个遍。我同遇到的几个水手搭话，告诉他们我也即将加入舰队，还急切地向他们打听，可曾见过纳尔逊的舰队；接着我又向他们担保，科尔内塔先生不过是个贪生怕死的懦夫，眼下这场迫在眉睫的大战必定盛况空前。最后我到了卡莱塔，在那儿我的欢喜简直没了边际。我下到岸边，脱了鞋，从一块岩石蹦到另一块岩石；我去寻访旧日的男女游伴，却只找到寥寥几个：有的如今长大成人，谋了更好的营生；有的被强征上了船；剩下的那几个，几乎认不得我了。海水的荡漾撩动着我的感官；我抵不住那诱惑——大海神秘的魔法催逼着我，它那雄辩的低语，在我听来一向——不知为何——像是一个声音，邀我赴向幸福，或是带着威严的恫喝，唤我去发狂、去咆哮。我闪电似的脱光衣裳，扑进水里，仿佛飞奔进情人的怀抱。我游了一个多钟头，快活得无法言喻；随后穿好衣裳，接着走去拉维尼亚（La Viña）一带的街巷角落，在那些酒馆里，撞见了我少年时代最有名气的几个泼皮。同他们交谈时，我自称是个有身份的人物，并且以此为份，把身上仅有的几个*夸尔托*（cuartos，铜钱）全挥霍在请他们的酒上。我打听我叔叔的下落，可谁也说不出那位先生的音讯；我们闲扯了一阵之后，他们灌了我一杯白兰地，那酒立时冲上我的头，把我放倒在地板上。在我烂醉的高潮里，我觉得那帮无赖正痛痛快快地取笑我；可等我稍稍缓过来，便溜出酒馆，臊得无地自容。我走路还直打晃；我又得打自己老家门前经过，就在那门口，我看见一个面目粗蠢的女人在煎猪血和下水。认出儿时的家，我百感交集，忍不住泪如泉涌；那没心肝的女人见了，只当我是耍什么花招伎俩，好趁机偷她那锅难闻的吃食。好在我还能撒腿就跑，逃出了她的魔爪，把这份感情的抒发，推迟到更为相宜的时机。

===Original===
After this I thought I should like to see the old Cathedral, with which the tenderest memory of my childhood was inseparably linked, and I went into it; the interior seemed to me most beautiful; never have I felt a deeper impulse of religious veneration in any church. It gave me a passionate desire to pray, and I did in fact throw myself on my knees, before the very altar where my mother had offered an *ex-voto* for my escape from death. The waxen image which I believed to be an exact likeness of myself was still in its place which it filled with all the solemnity of sanctity, but it struck me as very like a chestnut-husk. And yet this trumpery doll, the symbol of piety and maternal devotion, filled me with tender respect. I said my prayers on my knees, in memory of my good mother’s sufferings and death, and trying to realize that she was now happy in Heaven; but as my head was not yet very clear of the fumes of that accursed brandy, I stumbled and fell as I rose from my knees and an indignant sacristan turned me out into the street. A few steps took me back to the Calle del Fideo, where we were staying, and my master scolded me for being so long absent. If Doña Francisca had been cognizant of my fault I should not have escaped a sound drubbing, but my master was merciful and never beat me, perhaps because his conscience told him he was as much a child as I was.

We were staying at Cádiz in the house of a cousin of my master; and the reader must allow me to describe this lady somewhat fully, for she was a character deserving to be studied. Doña Flora de Cisniega was an old woman who still pretended to be young. She was certainly past fifty, but she practised every art that might deceive the world into believing her not more than half that terrible age. As to describing how she contrived to ally science and art to attain her object—that would be an undertaking far beyond my slender powers. The enumeration of the curls and plaits, bows and ends, powders, rouges, washes and other extraneous matters which she employed in effecting this monumental work of restoration, would exhaust the most vivid fancy; such things may be left to the indefatigable pen of the novelist—this, being History, deals only with great subjects and cannot meddle with those elegant mysteries. As far as her appearance was concerned what I remember best was the composition of her face, which all the painters of the Academy seemed to have touched up with rose color; I remember too that when she spoke she moved her lips with a grimace, a mincing prudery which was intended either to diminish the width of a very wide mouth, or to conceal the gaps in her teeth from whose ranks one or two proved deserters every year; but this elaborate attempt was so far a failure that it made her uglier rather than better looking. She was always richly dressed, with pounds of powder in her hair, and as she was plump and fair—to judge from what was visible through her open tucker, or under the transparency of gauze and muslin—her best chance lay in the display of such charms as are least exposed to the injurious inroads of time, an art in which she certainly was marvellously successful.

Doña Flora was devoted to everything antiquated, and much addicted to piety, but not with the genuine devoutness of Doña Francisca; indeed she was in everything diametrically the opposite of my mistress; for while Doña Francisca hated even the glory that was won at sea, she was an enthusiastic admirer of all fighting-men and of the navy in particular. Fired by patriotic passion—since at her mature age she could not hope to feel the flame of any other—and intensely proud of herself as a woman and as a Spaniard, love of her country was symbolized in her mind by the roar of cannon, and she thought the greatness of a nation was measured by tons of gunpowder. Having no children her time was spent in gossip, picked up and passed round in a small circle of neighbors by two or three chatterboxes like herself; but she also amused herself by her indefatigable mania for discussing public affairs. At that time there were no newspapers, and political theories, like public news, were passed on from mouth to mouth, these being even more falsified then than now, in proportion as talk is less trustworthy even than print.

===Chinese===
这以后，我想去看看老主教座堂——我儿时最温柔的回忆同它密不可分——便走了进去；那内部在我看来美极了；在任何一座教堂里，我都不曾感到过比这更深的宗教敬畏的冲动。它在我心里激起一股恳切祷告的热望，我也果真扑通跪倒，就跪在我母亲当年为我死里逃生供过*还愿像*（ex-voto）的那座祭坛前。那尊蜡像——我一向信为同我本人分毫不差——还端坐在原处，带着圣物的庄严充盈着那个所在；只是我瞧着它，觉得活脱是个栗子壳。然而这个寒伧的小蜡人，这虔敬与母爱的象征，却使我心里充满温柔的敬意。我跪着念了祷告，追念我善良母亲的苦难与死亡，努力想领会她如今已在天上享福；可我的脑袋还没从那可诅咒的白兰地的烟雾里完全清醒过来，起身时一个趔趄栽倒在地，一位怒气冲冲的教堂司事把我撵到了大街上。没几步路，我就回到了我们寄宿的菲德奥街（Calle del Fideo），主人为我去了这么久训斥了我一顿。这事若是让堂娜·弗朗西斯卡知道了，我准逃不过一顿好揍；可我主人心慈，从不打我——兴许是因为他心里明白，他自己同我一样是个孩子。

我们在加的斯寄住在我主人一位表亲的家里；这位夫人，请容我稍费笔墨细细描摹一番，因为她实在是个值得研究的人物。堂娜·弗洛拉·德·西斯涅加（Doña Flora de Cisniega）是位还硬要冒充年轻的老婆子。她确实年过五十了，却使出十八般解数，哄得世人相信她顶多只有那个可怕岁数的一半。至于说她怎样把科学与艺术联起手来达成这个目标——那可是远非我这支拙笔所能胜任的差事。要把她用来完成这项纪念碑式修复工程的鬈发、辫子、蝴蝶结、发梢、香粉、胭脂、面水以及其他种种外敷之物一一列举出来，最能活跃的想象力也要枯竭；这类事情只好留给小说家那支不知疲倦的笔——本书既是历史，只谈大题目，不便搅和进这些雅致的秘密。就她的容貌而言，我记得最清楚的是她那张脸的调子，仿佛美术学院所有的画家都拿玫瑰红给它润过色；我还记得，她一开口说话，嘴唇就作怪样地扭动，一种忸怩作态的假正经，意在或者缩小那张阔得出奇的嘴巴，或者遮掩牙齿间的豁口——她的牙每年总要有一两颗开小差离队；可这番苦心经营却弄巧成拙，反倒使她更难看而不是更好看。她总是穿得十分华丽，头发里搀着成磅的香粉；而且她丰腴白皙——从敞开的胸衣和薄纱细麻纱的半透明处所能望见的一切来判断——她最好的指望，便在于炫耀那些最不容易受岁月侵蚀之害的部位，在这门艺术上她无疑取得了非凡的成功。

堂娜·弗洛拉一味迷恋一切陈年古旧之物，又笃信宗教，却没有堂娜·弗朗西斯卡那种真正的虔诚；其实她样样都同我的女主人恰恰相反：堂娜·弗朗西斯卡连海上的荣光都憎恨，她却是天下好汉、尤其是海军的热烈崇拜者。她被爱国的激情点燃——因为以她的成熟年纪，别的火焰已无从指望了——又为自己身为女人、身为西班牙人而无比自豪，对祖国的爱在她心里由大炮的轰鸣来象征，她认定一个国家的伟大，是按火药的吨数来衡量的。她没有儿女，光阴便消磨在闲话里，在左邻右舍的小圈子里，由两三个跟她一样的长舌妇捡起来又传出去；不过她还爱摆弄一桩癖好来自娱：不知疲倦地议论国事。那时候还没有报纸，政治理论同社会新闻一样口口相传，而口传的东西被歪曲的程度比如今更甚，因为嘴上传的话连白纸黑字都还不如可靠。

===Original===
In all the large towns, and particularly in Cádiz, which was one of the foremost cities of Spain, there were a number of idle persons who made it their business always to have the latest news from Madrid and Paris, and to be diligent in distributing it, priding themselves, in fact, on a mission which gained them so much consideration. Some of these newsmongers would meet in the evening at Doña Flora’s house, and this, seconded by excellent chocolate and still better cakes, attracted others eager to learn what was going on. Doña Flora, knowing that she could not hope to inspire a tender feeling or be quit of the burden of her fifty years, would not have exchanged the part she was thus enabled to play for any other that could have been offered to her; for, at that time, to be the centre to which all news was conveyed was almost as precious a distinction as the majesty of a throne. Doña Flora and Doña Francisca could never get on together, as may easily be supposed when we consider the enthusiastic military tastes of one, and the pacific timidity of the other. Thus, speaking to Don Alonso the day we arrived, the good lady said:

“If you had always listened to your wife you might have been a common sailor to this day. What a woman! If I were a man and married to such a wife I should burst up like a bombshell. You did very rightly not to follow her advice but to come to join the fleet. Why you are not an old man yet, Alonsito; you may still rise to the rank of commodore, which you would have been sure of if Paca had not clipped your wings, as we do to chickens to prevent their straying.”

When, presently, my master’s eager curiosity made him press her for the latest news, she went on:

===Chinese===
在所有大城市，尤其是在加的斯——西班牙数一数二的名城——有一批闲人，把永远抢先掌握马德里和巴黎的最新消息当作自己的正经营生，并勤勤恳恳地加以散布，实际上颇为这项使命感到自豪，因为它替他们赢得了不少敬重。这帮播报新闻的人，有几个每晚聚在堂娜·弗洛拉家里，加上上好的巧克力、更好的糕饼助阵，又引来了另一些急于探听动静的客人。堂娜·弗洛拉自知不能指望唤起谁的柔情，也卸不下五十岁这个包袱，便觉得由这层关系所演的角色，拿什么别的来换她也不干；因为在那个年头，做众望所归的新闻总汇，几乎是同王座的尊严一样贵气的殊荣。堂娜·弗洛拉同堂娜·弗朗西斯卡永远合不来，这不难想见：一个热衷军旅，一个怯懦怕事、但求平安。于是，在我们到达的那一天，这位好心夫人对堂阿隆索说道：

“你要是向来都听你老婆的话，到今天也不过是个寻常水手。好一个女人！我要是个男人，娶了这么个老婆，非像颗炸弹似的炸开不可。你不听她的劝、来投舰队，做得太对了。怎么，你还没老呢，阿隆西托（Alonsito）；你还升得上海军准将的位子呢——要不是帕卡（Paca）剪了你的翅膀，这准将是稳稳到手的，就像咱们剪鸡翅膀，好叫它们不乱飞。”

过了一会儿，我主人急切的好奇心催着向她打听最新的消息，她便接着说道：

===Original===
“The most important news is that all the naval men here are extremely dissatisfied with the French Admiral, who displayed his incapacity in the expedition to Martinique and the fight off Finisterre. He is so timid and so mortally afraid of the English that, when the combined fleets ran in here last August, he dared not seize the cruisers commanded by Collingwood though they were but three ships in all. All our officers are greatly disgusted at finding themselves obliged to serve under such a man; indeed Gravina went to Madrid to tell Godoy so, foreseeing some terrible disaster if the command were not placed in more able hands; but the minister gave him some vague answer as to why he could not venture to decide in the matter, and as Bonaparte is in Germany, dealing with the Austrians, he cannot be appealed to.—But it is said that he too is dissatisfied with Villeneuve and has determined to dismiss him; but meanwhile. … If only Napoleon would put the whole fleet under the command of some Spaniard—you, for instance, Alonso—promoting you at once as I am sure you richly deserve. …”

“Oh! I am not fit for it!” replied my master, with his habitual modesty.

“Well, to Gravina, or to Churruca, who is said to be a very first-rate sailor. If not I am afraid mischief will come of it. You cannot see the French from here; only think, when Villeneuve’s ships arrived they were short of victuals and ammunition, and the authorities here did not care to supply them out of the arsenal. They forwarded a complaint to Madrid, and as Godoy’s one idea is to do what the French ambassador M. de Bernouville asks him, he sent orders that our allies should have as much of everything as they required. But this had no effect. The commandant of the navy yard and the commissary of the ordnance stores declared they would deliver nothing to Villeneuve till he paid for it money down and in hard cash. This seems to me very right and fair. The last misfortune that could come upon us was that these fine gentlemen should take possession of the little we had left! Pretty times we live in! Everything is ruinously dear, and yellow fever on one side and hard times on the other had brought Andalusia to such a state that she was not worth a doit—and now, to that you add all the miseries of war. Of course the honor of the nation is the first thing and we must go on now to avenge the insults we have received. I do not want to go back to the fight of Finisterre where, through the meanness of our allies, we lost the *Firme* and the *Rafael*, two splendid ships—nor of the piratical seizure of the *Real Cárlos*, which was such an act of treachery that the Barbary pirates would have been disgraced by it—nor of the plunder of the four frigates—nor of the battle off Cape St. …”

===Chinese===
“顶要紧的新闻是：这儿的海军人员个个对那位法国海军上将极端不满，他在远征马提尼克和菲尼斯特雷角外的那一仗里露尽了无能。他胆子小得要命，怕英国人怕得要死——去年八月联合舰队驶进这港里来的时候，科林伍德手下的巡航舰拢共才三条，他都不敢去动一动。我们所有的军官一想到不得不在这样的统帅手下服役，就腻烦透了；格拉维纳果真为此专程去马德里面告戈多伊，预言指挥权若不交到更能干的人手里，必有大祸临头；可那位大臣支支吾吾地回了他一通，说什么这事他不敢贸然裁断；至于波拿巴，他正在德意志对付奥地利人，也没处去求他。——不过据说连他也不满意维尔纳夫，已经决意撤换他了；只是眼下嘛。……要是拿破仑肯把整支舰队交给哪个西班牙人指挥——比方说交给你，阿隆索——立刻升你的衔，我敢说你完全当之无愧。……”

“哦！我哪里配！”我主人答道，带着他惯常的谦逊。

“那么，就交给格拉维纳，或者丘鲁卡，据说他是顶呱呱的一流海员。不然的话，我怕要出乱子。从这儿望不见法国人；你想想，维尔纳夫的船开来的时候，缺粮缺弹药，本地当局又不想从军械库拨给他们。他们递了状子到马德里，而戈多伊一心只想着照法国大使贝尔努维尔先生（M. de Bernouville）的要求办事，便下令咱们的盟友要什么、给什么。可这也不管用。海军船厂的司令官和军械库的军需官声明：维尔纳夫不现钱结账、不付硬邦邦的银币，他们一星东西也不交割。我看这非常正当、非常公道。咱们所能遭到的最后一桩倒霉事，就是让这帮体面人物把咱们剩下的这一点儿家当占了去！咱们赶上的好时世！样样东西贵得吓人，一边是黄热病，一边是市面艰难，把安达卢西亚折腾得一文不值——如今又添上战争的种种苦难。当然，国家的荣誉是头一位的，如今咱们必须接着干下去，去报咱们受的那些羞辱。我本不愿再回头去数说菲尼斯特雷角那一仗——由于盟友的卑劣，我们在那儿损失了*菲尔梅号*（Firme）和*拉斐尔号*（Rafael）两艘出色的战舰——也不说海盗式劫夺*皇家卡洛斯号*那桩事，那一手卑鄙得连巴巴里海盗干了都要丢脸——也不说劫掠那四条巡防舰——也不说圣……角外的那场海战……”

===Original===
“That was the thing,” interrupted my master eagerly. “Every man must keep his own place, but if Admiral Córdova had given the word to tack. …”

“Yes, yes—I know,” exclaimed Doña Flora, who had heard the story a hundred times before. “We must positively give them a thorough beating and we will. You, I know, are going to cover yourself with glory. It will enfuriate Paca.”

“I am of no use for fighting,” said my master sadly. “I am only going to look on, for sheer love of it and devotion to the Spanish flag.”

===Chinese===
“正是那桩事！”我主人急不可待地插嘴道。“各人自有各人的本分；可倘若科尔多瓦海军上将当时下了转舵的令。……”

“是，是——我知道，”堂娜·弗洛拉嚷道，这个故事她已听过一百遍了。“咱们非得痛痛快快揍他们一顿不可，也一定会揍。我知道，你是要去挣一身荣耀了。这可要把帕卡气坏了。”

“我打仗是不中用的，”我主人怏怏地说。“我去不过是旁观瞧个热闹，纯粹出于对这一行的痴爱和对西班牙旗帜的忠忱。”

===Original===
The day after our arrival my master received a visit from a naval officer, an old friend of his, whose face I can never forget though I saw him but that once. He was a man of about five and forty, with a really beautiful and gentle face and an expression of such tender melancholy that to see him was to love him. He wore no wig, but his abundant hair, untortured by the barber into the fashionable *ailes de pigeon*, was carelessly tied into a thick pigtail and heavily powdered, though with less elaborate care than was usual at that time. His eyes were large and blue, his nose finely chiselled, perfect in outline, rather wide, but not so wide as to disfigure him—on the contrary, it seemed to give distinction to his expressive countenance. His chin, which was carefully shaved, was somewhat pointed, and added to the melancholy charm of an oval face which was indicative of delicate feeling rather than of energetic determination. This noble exterior was well matched by the elegance of his manners—a grave courtesy of which the fatuous airs of the men of the present day retain no trace, any more than the modish graces of our *jeunesse dorée*. His figure was small, slight and even sickly looking. He looked more like a scholar than a warrior, and a brow, behind which lofty and subtle thoughts must have lain hid, looked ill-fitted to defy the horrors of battle. His fragile form, inhabited by a soul so far above the common, looked as though it must succumb to the first shock. And yet—as I afterwards learned—this man’s heart was as brave as his intellect was supreme. It was Churruca.

Our hero’s uniform, though it was not in holes nor threadbare, bore the marks of long and honorable service; afterwards, when I heard it authoritatively stated that the Government owed him nine quarters’ pay, I could account for this dilapidated appearance. My master asked after his wife, and I gathered from the answer that he was only lately married, which filled me with pity; it seemed to me so terrible a thing to be dragged off to battle in the midst of so much happiness. Then they talked of his ship, the *San Juan Nepomuceno*, which he seemed to love as much as his young wife; for, as was well known, he had had it planned and fitted to his own taste, under a special privilege, and had made it one of the finest ships in the Spanish fleet. Then of course they discussed the absorbing subject of the day: whether the squadrons would or would not put out to sea and the Commodore expressed his opinion at much length, in very much such words as these; for their substance had always remained in my memory so that now, by the help of dates and historical records, I can reconstruct his speech with considerable accuracy.

===Chinese===
我们到达的第二天，我主人接待了一位海军军官的来访，他是主人的一位老友；他那副面容，我虽只见过那一面，却终生难忘。此人四十五岁上下，生着一张真正俊秀温和的脸，神情里带着那样一种温柔的忧郁，叫人一见便生爱敬。他不戴假发，一头浓发没有受理发师的折磨去盘成时兴的*鸽子翼*发式（ailes de pigeon），只随随便便挽成一根粗辫子，扑了厚厚的发粉，只是不及当时通行的那么讲究。他的眼睛又大又蓝，鼻梁雕琢般端正，轮廓完美，略显宽阔，却又宽不到损伤仪容的地步——恰恰相反，它似乎给他那张富于表情的脸平添了气派。他的下巴修剃得干干净净，略见尖削，给那张椭圆形的脸更添了几分忧郁的魅力——那是一张显示细腻多感、而非果决刚毅的脸。这高贵的仪表，同他举止的优雅正相匹配——那是一种庄重的谦和，今日之辈那副昏聩自负的神气里已寻不到半点踪影，正如我们这班*镀金青年*（jeunesse dorée）的时髦风雅里也寻不到一样。他身材瘦小，单薄，甚至带几分病态，看上去更像位学者而非武人；那颗额头——其后必有高远精微的思想隐藏着——瞧上去不像敢于直面战争的恐怖。这副孱弱的躯壳，里面住着一个远非凡俗的灵魂，看上去一击便倒。然而——我后来才知道——此人的心，同他冠绝人寰的才智一样勇敢。他就是丘鲁卡。

我们这位英雄的制服，虽说没有破洞、也没有磨得露线，却带着长久而光荣的服役留下的痕迹；后来我听有头有脸的人说，政府欠着他九个季度的薪饷，这才明白了这身寒素相的来历。我主人问起他的太太，我从答话里听出他新婚未久，这使我满心怜惜；在那样美满的幸福之中竟被拖去打仗，在我看来实在是可怕的事。接着他们谈起他的座舰*圣胡安·内波穆塞诺号*（San Juan Nepomuceno），他爱它似乎不下于爱他年轻的妻子；因为众所周知，他得了特别恩准，亲自按自己的心意规划、装备了这条船，把它造成了西班牙舰队里最精良的军舰之一。然后他们自然谈起了当日人人萦怀的话题：舰队究竟出海不出海。这位准将长篇大论地发表了自己的见解，用的大致就是下面这些话；因为那些话的实质始终留在我的记忆里，如今借助种种日期和史料的帮助，我能把他的谈话相当准确地复原出来。
===Original===
“The French admiral,” said Churruca, “not knowing what course to pursue and being anxious to do something which might cast his errors into oblivion, has, ever since we arrived, manifested an inclination to go and seek the English. On the 8th of October he wrote to Gravina, saying that he wished to hold a council of war on board the *Bucentaure* (Villeneuve’s ship) to agree on the best course of action. Gravina went to the council, taking with him the Vice-Admiral Álava, Rear-Admirals Escaño and Cisneros, Commodore Galiano and myself. Of the French there were present Rear-Admirals Dumanoir and Magon and Captains Cosmas, Maistral, Villiegries, and Prigmy.

“Villeneuve having expressed his wish to go out to sea, we Spaniards unanimously opposed it.
