The discussion was warm and eager, and Alcalá Galiano and Magon exchanged such hard words that it must have come to a duel if we had not intervened to pacify them. Our opposition greatly annoyed Villeneuve, and in the heat of argument he even threw out certain insolent hints to which Gravina promptly retorted.—And indeed these worthies display a curious anxiety to go forth to seek a powerful foe, considering that they forsook us at the battle off Cape Finisterre, depriving us of what would have been a victory if they had seconded us in time. But there are many reasons, which I fully explained to the council—such as the advanced season, which render it far more advantageous for us to remain in the bay, forcing them to form a blockade which they cannot maintain, particularly if at the same time they blockade Toulon and Cartagena. We cannot but admit the superiority of the English navy, as to the completeness of their armament, their ample supply of ammunition, and, above all, the unanimity with which they maneuver.

“We—manned for the most part with less experienced crews, inadequately armed and provided, and commanded by a leader who dissatisfies everyone—might nevertheless act to advantage on the defensive, inside the bay. But we shall be forced to obey, to succumb to the blind submission of the ministry at Madrid and put our vessels and men at the mercy of Bonaparte, who, in return for this servility has certainly not given us a chief worthy of so much sacrifice. We must go if Villeneuve orders it, but if the result is a disaster our opposition to his insane resolution stands on record as our acquittal. Villeneuve in fact is desperate; his sovereign has used harsh language to him, and the warning that he will be degraded from his command is prompting him to the maddest acts, in the hope of recovering his tarnished reputation, in a single day, by death or victory.”

===Chinese===
“那位法国海军上将，”丘鲁卡说，“不知该走哪条路才好，又急于做点什么好让世人忘掉他的种种失误，所以自从我们到港以来，一直显出要去寻英国人一战的意向。十月八日，他写信给格拉维纳，说要在*布森陶尔号*（Bucentaure，维尔纳夫的座舰）上开一个军事会议，商定最妥当的行动方案。格拉维纳赴会，带上了海军中将阿拉瓦、海军少将埃斯卡尼奥（Escaño）和希斯内罗斯、准将加利亚诺，还有我。法方出席的有海军少将迪马努瓦（Dumanoir）和马贡（Magon），还有舰长科斯马斯（Cosmas）、梅斯特拉尔（Maistral）、维利格里（Villiegries）和普里格米（Prigmy）。

“维尔纳夫表示了出海的意愿，我们西班牙人一致反对。讨论得激烈而亢奋，阿尔卡拉·加利亚诺同马贡互出恶言，若不是我们从中劝解，非闹到决斗不可。我们的反对大大触怒了维尔纳夫，争到火头上，他甚至放出几句无礼的口风，格拉维纳当即顶了回去。——说实在的，这帮大人物急煎煎地要出海去寻强敌交手，也真算得上奇事一桩——想当年菲尼斯特雷角外那一仗，正是他们丢下我们不管，害得我们失去一场胜利——倘若他们及时接应，那胜利本是我们的。但是有种种理由——我在会上已充分陈明——比如时令已晚，使我们留在湾内远为有利，逼他们去维持一道守不住的封锁线，尤其在他们还要同时封锁土伦和卡塔赫纳的情况下。我们不能不承认英国海军的优越：武备齐全，弹药充裕，尤其是他们调度操演上下的齐心一致。

“我们呢——船员大半经验不足，武备给养两缺，又归一个人人不满意的统帅指挥——然而据守湾内、以防御制敌，未尝没有可乘之利。可是我们只得服从，只得屈从于马德里朝廷的盲目听命，把我们的舰船和官兵交由波拿巴摆布；而波拿巴受了这分奴颜婢膝，却并没有给我们一位配得上如此牺牲的统帅。维尔纳夫若下令，我们就得出海；可万一结局是大祸，我们对他这疯狂决定的反对已有案可稽，便是我们的开脱之证。维尔纳夫实在是走投无路了；他的君王对他恶语相加，又警告说要撤他的指挥权，这就驱使着他去干最疯狂的事，指望凭一死或一胜，在区区一日之内挽回他蒙尘的名声。”

===Original===
So spoke my master’s friend. His words impressed me deeply; child as I still was, I took an eager interest in the events going on around me, and since—reading in history all the facts to which I was then witness, I have been able to aid my memory by authenticated dates so that I can tell my story with considerable accuracy.

When Churruca left us, Doña Flora and my master sang his praises in the warmest terms; praising him especially for the expedition he had conducted to Central America to make charts of those seas. According to them Churruca’s merits as a navigator and a man of learning were such that Napoleon himself had made him a magnificent present and heaped civilities upon him. But we will leave the sailor and return to Doña Flora.

By the end of the second day of our stay in her house I became aware of a phenomenon which disgusted me beyond measure, which was that my master’s cousin seemed quite to fall in love with me; that is to say, that she took it into her head that I was made to be her page. She never ceased to load me with every sort of kindness, and on hearing that I too was to join the fleet she bewailed herself greatly, swearing that it would be a pity if I should lose an arm or a leg, or even some less important part of my person—even if I escaped with my life. Such unpatriotic pity roused my indignation, and I believe I even went so far as to declare, in so many words, that I was on fire with warlike ardor. My gasconade delighted the old lady and she gave me a heap of sweetmeats to recover her place in my good graces.

===Chinese===
我主人的这位朋友就是这样说的。他的话给我留下了极深的印象；我虽还是个孩子，却对身边的种种事变怀着热切的关切；而后来——通过阅读史书，把我当时亲历亲见的一切事实一一对照，我已能用确凿的日期辅助记忆，因此能把我的故事讲得相当准确。

丘鲁卡告辞之后，堂娜·弗洛拉同我主人用最热烈的字句把他赞了个不绝口；尤其称道他率队远赴中美洲、测绘那一带海域海图的功绩。照他们的说法，丘鲁卡作为航海家和学问家的才具，连拿破仑本人都曾厚礼相赠、礼遇有加。不过咱们且撇下这位海员，回到堂娜·弗洛拉身上来。

在她家盘桓到第二天将尽的时候，我察觉了一桩令我厌恶透顶的怪事：我主人的这位表亲竟像是爱上我了；也就是说，她异想天开，认定我天生就该当她的贴身小僮。她不停地把我疼了又疼，百般恩宠；一听说我也要随舰队出海，便大大地悲叹起来，赌咒说我要是丢了条胳膊少了条腿，哪怕丢的是身上哪个不大要紧的部位——就算留得性命——也太可惜了。这般不爱国的怜悯激起了我的义愤，我相信我甚至明明白白说出了口：我正满腔尚武的热忱，烧得如火如荼。我的这通牛皮哄得老夫人眉开眼笑，她又赏了我一大堆糖果，好挽回她在我心中的地位。

===Original===
The next day she made me clean her parrot’s cage—a most shrewd bird that talked like a preacher and woke us at all hours of the morning by shrieking “*perro inglés!*”—(dog of an Englishman.) Then she took me to mass with her, desiring me to carry her stool, and in church she was incessantly looking round to see if I were there. Afterwards she kept me to look on while her hair was dressed—an operation that filled me with dismay as I saw the catafalque of curls and puffs that the hairdresser piled on her head. Observing the stupid astonishment with which I watched the skilful manipulation of this artist—a perfect architect of headpieces—Doña Flora laughed very heartily, and assured me that I should do better to remain with her as her page than to join the fleet, adding that I ought to learn to dress her hair, and by acquiring the higher branches of the art I might earn my living and make a figure in the world. Such a prospect, however, had nothing seductive to my fancy, and I told her, somewhat roughly, that I would rather be a soldier than a hairdresser. This pleased her mightily and as I was giving up the comb for something more patriotic and military she was more affectionate than ever. But notwithstanding that I was treated here with so much indulgence, I must confess that the lady annoyed me beyond measure, and that I really preferred the angry cuffing and slapping of Doña Francisca to Doña Flora’s mawkish attentions. This was very natural; for her ill-timed caresses, her prudery, the persistency with which she invited my presence, declaring that she was delighted with me and my conversation, prevented my going with my master on his visits to the different ships. A servant of the house accompanied him on these delightful expeditions, while I, deprived of the liberty to run about Cádiz as I longed to be doing, was left at home, sick of life, in the society of Doña Flora’s parrot and of the gentlemen who came every evening to announce whether or no the fleets would quit the bay, with other matters less to the purpose and far more trivial.

My vexation rose to desperation when I saw Marcial come to the house, and he and my master went out together, though not to embark finally; and when, after seeing them start, my forlorn spirit lost the last faint hope of being one of the party, Doña Flora took it into her head that she must have me to walk with her to the Alameda and then to church to attend vespers. This was more than I could bear and I began to dream of the possibility of putting a bold scheme into execution; of going, namely, on my own account to see one of the ships, hoping that, on the quay, I might meet some sailor of my acquaintance who would be persuaded to take me.

I went out with the old lady and as we went along the ramparts I tried to linger to look at the ships, but I could not abandon myself to the enjoyment of the spectacle for I had to answer the hundred questions with which Doña Flora persistently persecuted me. In the course of our walk we were joined by some young men and a few older ones. They all seemed very conceited, and were the most fashionable men of Cádiz, all extremely witty and elegantly dressed. Some of them were poets, or—to be accurate—wrote verses though sorry ones, and I fancied I heard them talking of some Academy where they met to fire shots at each other in rhyme, an amusement which could break no bones.

===Chinese===
第二天，她差我打扫她的鹦鹉笼子——那是只精明透顶的鸟，说起话来像布道的神父，一清早不管什么钟点，总要尖声怪叫「*英国狗*！」（perro inglés），把我们吵醒。随后她带我一同去望弥撒，叫我给她捧跪凳；在教堂里她不停地左顾右盼，看我还在不在。之后她又留我旁观她梳头——那道工序看得我心惊胆战，只见理发师在她头顶上垒起一座鬈发和发垫堆成的灵柩台。堂娜·弗洛拉瞧着我盯住这位艺术家——一位十全十美的头顶建筑师傅——摆弄手法时那副呆相，笑得前仰后合，并向我保证，我留在她身边当小僮，比去投舰队强得多；又说我不妨学学给她梳头，把这门艺术的高深造诣学到手，将来好挣饭吃，好在世上出人头地。可这般前程在我的幻想里毫无诱人之处，我便相当生硬地告诉她：我宁当兵，不当理发匠。这话大讨她喜欢——我既为着更有爱国气、更有武人气的事业丢下了梳子，她便比先前更加亲热。不过，尽管在这里受到百般姑息，我必须承认，这位夫人把我烦得要死；我实在宁可爱堂娜·弗朗西斯卡怒气冲冲的巴掌拳头，也不爱堂娜·弗洛拉的甜腻殷勤。这是很自然的：她那些不合时宜的爱抚，她那副假正经，还有她那样锲而不舍地要我把陪在跟前，口口声声说喜欢我、喜欢同我谈话——这些都害得我不能随主人去各条军舰上拜访。这些赏心乐事，由宅里的一名仆人陪他去了；而我呢，渴望着在加的斯满城乱跑而不得，被撂在家里，腻烦得要死，只同堂娜·弗洛拉的鹦鹉，以及每晚来通报舰队究竟出海不出海的先生们做伴，此外便是些更不着边际、更加琐碎的话题。

及至看见马西亚尔上门来，同我主人一道出门——虽还不是最后登舰——我的懊恼便升到了绝望的地步；目送他们动身之后，我那孤苦的心灵失去了入伙的最后一星微茫希望，堂娜·弗洛拉却又异想天开，非要我陪她散步到阿拉梅达（Alameda），再上教堂去望晚祷。这我可忍无可忍了，于是开始盘算，一件胆大包天的计划有无施行的可能：那就是自作主张，独自去看一条战舰，指望在码头上撞见哪个相识的水手，能被我说动，把我也带上。

我随老夫人出了门，沿着城垣走的时候，我想磨蹭着看看那些船，却不能纵情享受这番景致，因为堂娜·弗洛拉喋喋不休地拿一百个问题来折磨我。走着走着，凑上来几位青年，还有几位年纪大些的。个个看着都自负得很，都是加的斯最时髦的人物，人人妙语如珠，衣冠楚楚。其中有几个是诗人，或者——说得准确些——会写诗，尽管写得蹩脚；我恍惚听得他们谈起某个文社，说他们聚在那里用押韵的句子互相开火——一种绝不断人骨头的消遣。

===Original===
As I observed all that was going on round me, their extraordinary appearance fixed my attention—their effeminate gestures and, above all, their clothes, which to me looked preposterous. There were not many persons who dressed in this style in Cádiz; and, reflecting afterwards on the difference between their costume and the ordinary clothes of the people I was in the habit of seeing, I understood that it was that men in general wore the Spanish habit while Doña Flora’s friends followed the fashions of Madrid or of Paris. The first thing to attract my attention were their walking-sticks, which were twisted and knotted cudgels, with enormous knobs. Their chins were invisible, being hidden by the cravat, a kind of shawl wrapped round and round the throat and brought across below the lips so as to form a protuberance—a basket, a dish, or, better still, a barber’s basin—in which the chin was quite lost. Their hair was dressed with elaborate disorder, looking as if it had been done with a birch-broom rather than with a comb. The corners of their hats came down to their shoulders; their coats, extremely short-waisted, almost swept the ground with their skirts; their boots were pointed at the toes; dozens of seals and trinkets hung from their waistcoat pockets; their breeches, which were striped, were fastened at the knee with a wide ribbon, and to put the finishing stroke to these figures of fun, each carried an eyeglass which, in the course of conversation, was constantly applied to the right eye, half-closing the left, though they would have seen perfectly well by using both.

The conversation of these gentlemen, also, turned on the plans of the fleet, but they varied it by discussing some ball or entertainment which they talked of a great deal, and one of them was the object of the greatest admiration for the perfection with which he cut capers, and the lightness of his heels in dancing the gavotte.

After chattering for some time the whole party followed Doña Flora into the church del Cármen, and there, each one pulling out a rosary, they remained praying with much energy for some little time, and one of them, I remember, gave me a smart rap on the top of my head because, instead of attending devoutly to my prayers like them, I was paying too much attention to two flies that were buzzing round the topmost curl of Doña Flora’s structure of hair. After listening to a tiresome sermon, which they praised as a magnificent oration, we went out again, and resumed our promenade; the chat was soon more lively than ever; for we were joined by some other ladies dressed in the same style and among them all there was such a noisy hubbub of compliments, fine speeches, and witticisms, with here and there an insipid epigram, that I could gather nothing from it all.

===Chinese===
我观察着周围的一切动静，他们那副奇特的行头攫住了我的注意——那娘儿们似的手势，尤其是那身衣裳，在我眼里简直荒唐透顶。加的斯穿这路打扮的人并不多；事后我回想他们的装束同我平素看惯的常人衣着之间的差异，才明白过来：原来寻常男子穿的是西班牙本土的服色，而堂娜·弗洛拉的朋友们追随的是马德里或巴黎的时新款式。头一样惹我眼的是他们的手杖——一根根扭曲多结的棍棒，顶着老大老大的疙瘩头。他们的下巴是看不见的，全让领巾埋没了——那是种披巾似的东西，在脖子上绕了一圈又一圈，兜上来遮到嘴唇底下，鼓成一个大鼓包——一只篮子、一只盘子，或者形容得更贴切些，一只理发匠的铜盆——下巴就整个儿丢在了里面。他们的头发梳得煞费苦心地乱，瞧那样子与其说是用梳子梳的，不如说是用桦树枝扫帚扫出来的。他们帽子的檐角一直耷拉到肩膀；上衣的腰身奇短，下摆却几乎扫地；靴子头尖尖的；马甲口袋上垂挂着几十枚图章和小玩意儿；条纹短裤在膝盖处用宽缎带系住；而给这班活宝压轴的一笔，是人手一只单眼镜，谈话之间不住地举到右眼上，左眼眯起一半——其实两眼一齐用，本来看得再清楚不过。

这几位先生的谈话，也绕着舰队的动向打转，不过他们拿某场舞会或游艺会来换换花样，谈了个不亦乐乎；其中一位更是众人叹服的对象——叹服他蹦花步蹦得十全十美，跳起加沃特舞来脚跟何等轻巧。

闲扯了一阵之后，这一行人跟着堂娜·弗洛拉进了卡门教堂（del Cármen）；到了那里，人人掏出一串念珠，精神抖擞地祷告了好一会儿；我记得其中一位还照我头顶不轻不重敲了一记，因为我不肯像他们那样虔诚地专注于祷告，却把太多心思放在两只苍蝇身上——它们正绕着堂娜·弗洛拉那座头发建筑的最高一撮鬈发嗡嗡打转。听罢一场乏味的布道——他们盛赞为一篇雄辩滔滔的演说——我们又走出来，接着散步；谈兴很快比先前更加高涨：又来了几位同样装束的夫人加入进来，于是在这一群人中间，恭维话、漂亮话、俏皮话闹成一片喧嚣，其间还夹着几句寡淡无味的短诗，我一个字也没听出个所以然。

===Original===
And all this time Marcial and my dear master were arranging the day and hour when they should embark! While I was perhaps doomed to remain on shore to gratify the whims of this old woman whom I positively loathed, with her odious petting! Would you believe that that very evening she insisted on it that I must remain forever in her service? Would you believe that she declared that she was very fond of me, and in proof of the fact kissed me and fondled me, desiring me to be sure to tell no one? Horrible spite of fate! I could not help thinking what my feelings would have been if my young mistress had treated me in such a fashion. I was confused to the last degree; however, I told her that I wished to join the fleet, and that when I came back she might keep me if it was her fancy, but that if she did not allow me to have my wish I should hate her as much as that—and I spread my arms out wide to express the immensity of my aversion.

Then, as my master came in unexpectedly, I thought it a favorable opportunity for gaining my purpose by a sudden stroke of oratory which I had hastily prepared; I fell on my knees at his feet, declaring in pathetic accents, that if he did not take me on board with him I should fling myself into the sea in despair.

My master laughed at this performance and his cousin, pursing her lips, affected amusement with a grimace which made her sallow wrinkled face uglier than ever; but, finally, she consented. She gave me a heap of sweetmeats to eat on board, charged me to keep out of the way of danger, and did not say another word against my embarking, as we did very early next morning.

===Chinese===
而就在这整个当口，马西亚尔同我亲爱的主人正在商定他们登舰的日子和时辰！我却也许命中注定要留在岸上，去讨这个我厌憎透了的老婆子的欢心，受她那可憎的疼爱！你信不信，就在那天晚上，她一口咬定，要我永远留在她身边当差？你信不信，她口口声声说喜欢我，为了作证，又是亲我又是搂我，还叮嘱我千万不许告诉别人？命运的狠毒捉弄！我禁不住想，倘若我的小姐这样待我，我心里会是什么滋味。我窘到了极点；不过我还是告诉她，我要去投舰队，等我回来，她若高兴，可以收留我；可她若不许我如愿，我就恨她——恨到这么宽——我把双臂张到最开，好表达我这厌恶之情的无边无际。

这时我主人不意走了进来，我灵机一动，觉得这是天赐良机，正好用一段仓促间备好的即兴雄辩一举成事；我扑倒在他脚边，用凄楚的声调宣告：他若不把我带上船，我绝望之下便一头扎进大海。

我主人看着这场表演笑了；他这位表亲抿着嘴唇，装出被逗乐的样子，那副怪相把她那张蜡黄多皱的脸弄得比平时更丑；不过她终究应允了。她给了我一堆糖果带在船上吃，叮嘱我千万躲着危险走，再没说一句反对我登舰的话——第二天一大早，我们便上船了。

## IX

===Original===
It was the 18th of October. I can have no doubt as to the date because the fleet sailed out of the bay next day. We rose very early and went down to the quay, where a boat was waiting to carry us on board.

Imagine if you can my surprise—nay surprise do I say?—my enthusiasm, my rapture, when I found myself on board the *Santísima Trinidad*, the largest vessel on the main, that floating fortress of timber which, seen from a distance, had appeared to my fancy some portentous and supernatural creature; such a monster as alone was worthy of the majesty of the seas. Each time our boat passed under the side of a ship I examined it with a sort of religious astonishment, wondering to see the hulls so huge that from the ramparts had looked so small; and in the wild enthusiasm that possessed me I ran the greatest danger of falling into the water as I gazed in ecstasy at a figurehead—an object which fascinated me more than anything else.

At last we reached the *Santísima Trinidad*. As we approached, the colossal mass loomed larger and larger, and when the launch pulled up alongside, lost in the black transparent void made where its vast shadow fell upon the water—when I saw the huge hulk lying motionless on the dark waves which gently plashed against the side—when I looked up and saw the three tiers of cannon with their threatening muzzles thrust through the portholes—my excitement was changed to fear; I turned pale and sat silent and motionless by my master's side.

===Chinese===
那天是十月十八日。这日子我绝不会记错，因为舰队就在第二天驶出了海湾。我们起得极早，下到码头，一条小艇已等在那里，要送我们上船。

请你想像一下吧，当我发觉自己置身于*圣特立尼达号*——洋面上最大的船只、一座木头筑成的浮动堡垒——之上时，我是何等惊奇；不，我说惊奇么？——简直是热情迸发、心醉神迷。从远处望去，这座浮动堡垒在我幻想中一直像某种骇人的超自然造物，一头唯有它才配得上海洋威严的巨怪。每当我们的小艇从一艘大船的舷侧下驶过，我总怀着近乎虔敬的惊诧细细打量，惊异这些从城墙上望去曾显得那么渺小的船身，竟会如此庞大；我沉浸在痴狂的兴奋里，只顾出神仰望一尊尊船首像——世上再没有比它更让我着迷的东西——好几次险些一头栽进水里。

我们终于驶近了*圣特立尼达号*。随着小艇划近，那庞然的船体越逼越高；待到艇身靠拢大船，我们已没入它巨大的阴影投在水面所形成的一片乌黑透明的虚空里——当我看见那庞大的船体一动不动地卧在暗色波涛之上，微波轻轻地拍打着船侧——当我抬头望见三层火炮、一门门威吓的炮口从炮窗里探出来——我的兴奋变成了恐惧；我脸色发白，在主人身边坐下，一声不响，一动不动。

===Original===
But when we went up the side and stood on deck my spirits rose. The intricate and lofty rigging, the busy scene on the quarterdeck, the open view of the sky and bay, the perfect order of everything on deck, from the hammocks lashed in a row to the bulwarks, to the capstans, shells, windsails and hatchways; the variety of uniforms—everything I saw, in short, amazed me to such a degree that for some time I stood blankly gazing at the stupendous structure heedless of all else. You can form no idea of any of those magnificent vessels, much less of the *Santísima Trinidad*, from the wretched prints I have seen of them. Still less, again, from the ships of war of the present day, covered with ponderous plates of iron, heavy looking, uninteresting and black, with no visible details on their vast sides, looking to me for all the world like enormous floating coffins. Invented by a materialistic age and calculated to suit the naval science of a time when steam has superseded manual labor, and the issue of a sea-fight is decided by the force and impetus of the vessels, our ships are now mere fighting-machines, while those of that day were literally Men-of-War, wielding all the implements of attack and defence but trusting mainly to skill and valor.

I, who not only see, but observe, have always been in the habit of associating—perhaps to an extravagant extent—ideas and images, things and persons, which in appearance seem most dissimilar or antagonistic. When, at a later period, I saw the cathedrals—Gothic, as they call them—of Castile and of Flanders, and noted the impressive majesty with which those perfect and elaborate structures stand up among the buildings of more modern style, built only for utility—such as banks, hospitals, and barracks—I could never help remembering all the various kinds of vessels that I have seen in the course of a long life, and comparing the old ones to those Gothic cathedrals. Their curves, so gracefully prolonged, the predominance of vertical over horizontal lines, a certain indefinable poetry about them—not historical only but religious too—underlying the complication of details and the play of colors brought out by the caprices of the sunshine, are, no doubt, what led to this far-fetched association of ideas—the result in my mind of the romantic impressions of my childhood.

The *Santísima Trinidad* had four decks; the largest ships in the world had but three. This giant, constructed at Havana, in 1769, of the finest woods of Cuba, could reckon thirty-six years of honorable service. She measured 220 feet from stem to stern, 58 feet in the waist, that is to say in width, and 28 feet deep from the keel to the deck, measurements which no other vessel at the time could approach. Her huge ribs, which were a perfect forest, supported four decks. When she was first built 116 portholes gaped in her sides which were thick walls of timber; after she was enlarged in 1796 she had 130, and when she was newly fitted in 1805 she was made to carry 140 guns, cannons and carronades. The interior was a marvel of arrangement; there were decks for the guns, the forecastle for the crew, holds for stores of all kinds, state-cabins for the officers, the galley, the cockpit and other offices. I was quite bewildered as I ran through the passages and endless nooks of this floating fortress. The stern cabins on the main deck were a little palace within, and outside like some fantastic castle; the galleries, the flag-turrets at the corners of the poop—exactly like the oriels of a Gothic tower—looked like huge cages open to the sea, whence the eye could command three quarters of the horizon.

===Chinese===
可是一等我们攀上舷梯、站上甲板，我的胆气又回来了。繁复而高耸的索具，后甲板上繁忙的景象，天空与海湾的辽阔视野，甲板上一应器物井然的秩序——从一排排捆扎在舷墙上的吊床，到绞盘、炮弹、通风帆和舱口——形形色色的制服——总之，我眼见的一切无不令我惊叹到极点，以致好一阵子我只顾呆呆望着这座惊人的庞大构造，把其余一切全都忘了。从我见过的那些蹩脚版画上，你对那些壮丽的船只根本无从想像，更不必说*圣特立尼达号*了。想从当今的铁甲战舰去认识它们，就更不可能了：如今的战舰包裹着沉重的铁甲，看上去笨重、单调而黢黑，巨大的舷侧见不到任何细节，在我看来活脱是一口口硕大无朋的浮动棺材。它们由一个实利至上的时代发明出来，为的是适应蒸汽已取代人力、海战胜负系于舰只威力与冲劲的那个时代的海军科学；我们如今的船只是纯粹的战斗机器，而那个时代的船才是名副其实的"海上武士"（Men-of-War）——攻守器械无所不备，而所倚仗的主要是技艺与胆气。

我这人不但看，而且观察，一向惯于把表面看来最不相干、乃至相互抵触的观念与形象、事物与人物联到一处——也许联得过了头。后来我见到卡斯蒂利亚（Castile）和佛兰德斯（Flanders）那些人称哥特式的大教堂，注意到那些完美而精巧的建筑，在银行、医院、兵营之类纯为实用而建的近代样式房屋中间何等庄严地巍然耸立，我便总禁不住想起自己漫长一生中见过的各式船只，把那些老帆船比作这些哥特式大教堂。它们那优雅绵长的曲线，垂直线条压倒水平线条的格局，还有某种说不清道不明的诗意——不仅是历史的，也是宗教的——潜藏在繁复的细节之下，潜藏在任性变幻的阳光映出的色彩之下：毫无疑问，正是这一切引出了我这番看似牵强的联想——它在我心里，原是童年浪漫印象结出的果实。

*圣特立尼达号*有四层甲板，而世上最大的船也不过三层。这艘巨舰于一七六九年在哈瓦那（Havana）建成，用的是古巴（Cuba）最上等的木料，至此已可夸耀三十六年的光荣役龄。它从船首到船尾长二百二十英尺，船腰——也就是宽度——为五十八英尺，从龙骨到甲板深二十八英尺；这些尺寸，当年的任何船只都无从企及。它巨大的肋骨密密匝匝，宛如一片森林，托着四层甲板。初造之时，它那厚如木墙的两舷共张开一百一十六个炮口；一七九六年扩建之后增为一百三十个；一八〇五年重新装备时，它被配上一百四十门炮，加农炮与卡龙炮俱全。舰内布置之精巧堪称一大奇观：有安放火炮的炮甲板，有水手栖身的艏楼，有贮存各种给养的底舱，有军官们的官舱，还有厨房、伤病舱以及其他种种舱室。我在这座浮动堡垒的过道和数不清的旮旯里跑来串去，简直晕头转向。主甲板后部的艉舱，里面俨然是一座小宫殿，外面看去又像哪座奇幻的城堡；那一道道舷廊，那艉楼四角的旗楼——恰似哥特式塔楼的凸窗——好像一只只向大海敞开的巨大笼子，凭眺其间，四分之三的海平线尽收眼底。

===Original===
Nothing could be grander than the rigging—those gigantic masts thrust up to heaven like a menace to the storm. It was difficult to believe that the wind could have strength enough to fill those vast sails. The eye lost its way and became weary in gazing at the maze of the rigging with the shrouds, stays, braces, halyards, and other ropes used to haul and reef the various sails.

I was standing lost in the contemplation of all these wonders when I felt a heavy hand on the nape of my neck; I thought the mainmast had fallen on the top of me. I turned round in alarm and gave a cry of horror at seeing a man who was now holding me by the ears as if he were going to lift me up by them. It was my uncle.

"What are you doing here, vermin!" he asked, in the amiable tone that was habitual with him. "Do you want to learn the service? Hark ye Juan," he added, turning to a sailor of most sinister aspect, "send this landlubber up to the main-yard to take a walk there."

===Chinese===
再没有比这索具更壮丽的了——一根根巨桅直指苍穹，恰似向暴风雨发出的一声威吓。很难相信风竟有那般神力，能鼓满那么巨大的帆。侧支索、固定支索、转帆索、升降索，还有牵引和收拢各色帆布的其他缆绳，交织成一座迷宫；目光在里面迷了路，望得疲倦不堪。

我正站着对这一切奇观出神，忽觉一只沉甸甸的手按在我后颈上；我还当是主桅倒下来砸着了我的头顶。我惊慌地回头一看，吓得叫出声来——一个人正揪着我两只耳朵，仿佛要提着它们把我拎起来。那是我叔叔。

"你在这儿干什么，小害人虫！"他用他惯有的那副和气口吻问道。"想学学当差么？听着，胡安（Juan），"他扭头对一个满脸凶相的水手说，"把这个旱鸭子弄上主桅桁去遛一遛。"

===Original===
I excused myself as best I might from the pleasure of taking a walk on the main-yard, explaining that I was body-servant to Don Alonso Gutierrez de Cisniega and had come on board with him. Three or four sailors, my affectionate uncle's particular friends, wanted to torment me so I decided on quitting their distinguished society and went off to the cabin in search of my master. An officer's toilet is no less elaborate on board than on shore, and when I saw the valets busied in powdering the heads of the heroes they waited on, I could not help asking myself whether this was not, of all occupations, the least appropriate in a man-of-war, when every minute was precious and where everything that was not directly serviceable to the working of the ship was a hindrance. However, fashion was as tyrannical then as now, and even at such a moment as this enforced her absurd and inconvenient rules with inexorable rigor. The private soldiers even had to waste their valuable time in tying their pigtails, poor men! I saw them standing in a line, one behind another, each one at work on the pigtail of the man in front of him; by which ingenious device the operation was got through in a short space of time. Then they stuck on their fur hats, a ponderous headpiece the use of which no one was ever able to explain to me, and went to their posts if they were on duty or to pace the deck if they were not. The sailors did not wear this ridiculous queue of hair and I do not see that their very sensible costume has been altered to any great extent since that time.

In the cabin I found my master eagerly conversing with the captain in command of the ship, Don Francisco Xavier de Uriarte, and the commander of the squadron, Don Baltasar Hidalgo de Cisneros. From what I overheard I could have no doubt that the French admiral had ordered the fleets to put out to sea the next morning.

Marcial was highly delighted at this, and he and a knot of veteran sailors who held council on their own account in the forecastle, discoursed grandiloquently on the imminent fight. Their society suited me far better than that of my amiable uncle, for Marcial's companions indulged in no horseplay at my expense; and this difference was of itself enough to mark the difference of training in the two classes of sailors; for the old sea-dogs were of the pure breed originally levied as voluntary recruits; while the others were pressed men, almost without exception lazy, refractory, of low habits, and ignorant of the service.

===Chinese===
我百般推辞了这番上主桅桁散步的雅兴，说明自己是堂阿隆索·古铁雷斯·德·西斯涅加的贴身仆人，是随他一同上船来的。可我那位亲爱的叔叔的三四个至交偏要捉弄我，我便决计退出他们那高贵的圈子，往舱里找主人去。军官的梳妆，在船上同岸上一样讲究；眼看仆役们忙着替各自伺候的英雄扑发粉，我不禁暗自思量：在军舰上，这岂不是百业之中最不合适的一桩么——在这儿每一分钟都金贵，凡与行船无直接用处的东西无不是累赘。然而时尚在当年同如今一样专横，哪怕值此关头，也毫不容情地推行着它那套荒唐而不便的规矩。就连舰上的士兵也不得不把宝贵的光阴耗费在打辫子上，可怜的人！我看见他们站成一列，一个挨着一个背后站，每人都给前面那人打辫子；靠这个巧妙的法子，这道工序不消片刻便告完成。随后他们扣上毛皮帽子——一种沉甸甸的头上穿戴，其用处从来没有人能给我讲明白——当值的奔各自的岗位，不当值的便到甲板上去踱步。水手们倒不梳这可笑的辫子；而且依我看，他们那身十分合理的装束，自那时以来也没有多大改动。

在舱里，我见主人正谈得起劲，对方一是本舰舰长堂弗朗西斯科·哈维尔·德·乌里亚尔特（Don Francisco Xavier de Uriarte），一是分舰队司令堂巴尔塔萨尔·伊达尔戈·德·希斯内罗斯（Don Baltasar Hidalgo de Cisneros）。从旁听来的话里，我可以毫不怀疑：法国海军上将已命令各舰队于次日清晨启航出海。

马西亚尔听了喜不自胜。他和一小伙老水手在艏楼里自开军事会议，就那场迫在眉睫的战斗高谈阔论。同他们做伴，比同我那位和蔼的叔叔做伴合意多了，因为马西亚尔的伙伴们决不拿我恶作剧取乐；单是这一桩差别，就足以见出两类水手教养的高下：那些老海狼血统纯正，当初本是自愿应募投军的；另一类却是强征来的，几乎无一例外，又懒又犟、习气卑下、不通业务。

===Original===
I made much better friends with the former than with these and was always present at Marcial's conferences. If I did not fear to weary the reader, I might report the explanation he gave us that day of the diplomatical and political causes of the war—a most comical parody of all he had heard said, a few nights previously, by Malespina at my master's house. I learned from him that my young mistress' lover was on board the *Nepomuceno*.

All these colloquies came round at last to the same point, the impending battle. The fleet was to sail out of the bay next morning—what joy! To ride the seas in this immense vessel—the largest in the world; to witness a fight at sea; to see what a battle was like, how cannon were fired, how the enemy's ships were taken—what a splendid triumph! and then to return to Cádiz covered with glory.—To say afterwards to all who cared to hear: "Yes, I was there, I was on board, I saw it all. …" To tell Rosita too, describing the glorious scene, winning her attention, her curiosity, her interest.—To say to her: "Oh yes! I was in the most dangerous places and I was not afraid;"—and to see her turn pale with alarm, or faint, as she heard my tale of the horrors of the battle—and then to look down in contempt on all who would ask me: "Tell us, Gabrielito, was it so terrible after all?"—All this was more than enough to fire my imagination, and I may frankly say that I would not, that day, have changed places with Nelson himself.

The morning of the 19th dawned, the day I hailed so eagerly; indeed it had not yet dawned when I found myself at the stern of the vessel with my master, who wanted to look on at the working of the ship. After clearing the decks the business of starting the ship began. The huge topsails were hoisted, and the heavy windlass, turning with a shrill clatter, dragged the anchor up from the bottom of the bay. The sailors clambered along the yards, while others handled the braces, obedient to the boatswain's call; and all the ship's voices, hitherto mute, filled the air with threatening outcries. The whistles, the bell, the discordant medley of men's voices, mixed with the creaking of the blocks, the humming of the ropes, the flapping of the sails as they thrashed the mast before they caught the wind—all these various sounds filled the air as the huge ship got under way. The bright ripples seemed to caress her sides, and the majestic monster made her way out of the bay without the slightest roll or even lurch, with a slow and solemn advance which was only perceptible to those on board by watching the apparent motion of the merchantmen lying at anchor and the landscape beyond.

===Chinese===
我同前一类人结下的交情，远比同后一类人深，马西亚尔他们开什么会我回回在场。若不是怕读者生厌，我尽可以把那天他给我们讲的这场战争在外交和政治上的种种缘由照录下来——那是对几天前马莱斯皮纳在我主人家里那番宏论的一通最滑稽的戏仿。我从他嘴里得知，我小姐的心上人就在*圣胡安·内波穆塞诺号*上。

这一场场闲谈，兜来转去，末了总落回同一处：那场眼看要打起来的海战。舰队明晨就要驶出海湾了——多大的欢喜！乘着这条天下第一大船去破浪大洋；亲眼见识一场海战；瞧瞧打仗究竟是什么模样，炮是怎么放的，敌舰是怎么夺的——何等辉煌的凯旋！然后满载荣光回到加的斯。——日后对一切乐意听的人说："没错，我在场，我在船上，这一切我都亲眼见了。……"还要讲给罗西塔听，把那光荣的场面描摹一番，赢得她的注目、她的好奇、她的关切。——对她说："可不是嘛！最险的地方我都待过，我一点儿也不怕；"——看她听着我细数战斗的种种惨状，吓得脸色煞白，或者干脆晕过去——然后再睥睨一切胆敢来问我："给我们讲讲吧，加布里埃尔西托（Gabrielito），到底有那么可怕么？"的人。——这一桩桩一件件，早把我的想像烧得滚烫；可以坦白地说，那一天，哪怕拿纳尔逊本人来同我换位置，我也是不换的。

十九日清晨到了，正是我望眼欲穿的日子；其实天还没亮，我已同主人一道站在船尾，他要看船是如何起动的。甲板清理停当，起航的活计便开了头。巨大的上帆升了起来，沉重的起锚绞盘尖利地咔咔转动，把锚从湾底拽了上来。水手们攀上桁桅，另一些人应着水手长的哨令收放转帆索；全船上下一直沉默着的千百个嗓门，此刻齐声发出威吓般的呼号，震彻天空。哨声、钟声、嘈杂鼎沸的人声，混着滑车的吱呀、缆绳的嗡鸣、帆布吃风之前抽打桅杆的噼啪——巨舰起动之际，这万般声响一齐灌满了长空。明亮的涟漪仿佛在爱抚船舷，这威严的巨怪驶出海湾，不见丝毫摇摆，连一点倾侧也没有；它那缓慢而庄严的前进，只有船上的人望着锚泊的商船与岸上景物好似在移动，才觉察得出来。

===Original===
At this moment I stood looking back at the scene behind us. And what a scene it was! Thirty-two men-of-war, five frigates, and two brigantines, Spanish and French together—some in front, some behind, and some abreast of us—were bursting into sail, as it were, and riding before the light breeze. I never saw a lovelier morning. The sun flooded those lovely shores with light; a faint purple tinge colored the sea to the east, and the chain of hills which bound the horizon on the side of the town seemed to be on fire in the sunrise; the sky was perfectly clear excepting where, in the east, a few rose and golden clouds floated above the horizon. The blue sea was calm, and over that sea and beneath that sky the forty ships with their white sails rode forward, one of the noblest fleets that human eyes ever rested on.

The vessels did not all sail with equal speed. Some got ahead, others were slow to get under way; some gained upon us, while we passed others. The solemnity of their advance, the height of their masts, covered with canvas, and a vague and obscure harmony which my childish ears fancied they could detect proceeding from those glorious hulls—a kind of hymn, which was no doubt the effect of my own imagination—the loveliness of the day, the crispness of the air, the beauty of the sea, which seemed to be dancing with joy outside the gulf at the approach of the vessels—all formed the grandest picture that the mind of man can conceive of.

Cádiz, itself, like a moving panorama, unfolded itself before our eyes, displaying in turn every aspect of its vast amphitheatre. The low sun, illuminating the glass in its myriad windows, sprinkled it with living sparks of gold, and its buildings lay so purely white above the blue water that it looked as if it might have been that moment called into being, or raised from the sea like the fanciful city of San Genaro. I could see the wall extending from the mole as far as the fort of Santa Catalina; I could distinguish the bastions of Bonete and Orejon, and recognize the *caleta*; and my pride rose as I reflected what I had risen from and where I now was. At the same time the sound of the bells of the waking city came to my ear like some mysterious music, calling the inhabitants to early mass, with all the confused clamor of the bells of a large town. Now they seemed to me to ring gladly, and send good wishes after us—I listened to them as if they were human voices bidding us Godspeed; then again they tolled sadly and dolefully—a knell of misfortune; and as we sailed further and further away their music grew fainter till it was lost in space.

===Chinese===
这时我站在那里，回望身后的景象。那是何等的景象啊！三十二艘战舰、五艘巡防舰、两艘双桅小帆船，西班牙的与法国的会合在一处——有的在前，有的在后，有的与我们并肩而行——正仿佛一齐绽满风帆，乘着轻风前驶。我从没见过比这更可爱的早晨。阳光把那一带明媚的海岸照得通亮；东方的海面染着一抹淡紫，城市一侧环抱地平线的层层冈峦，在朝阳里好似燃着一片野火；天空明净极了，只有东面地平线上浮着几朵玫瑰色与金色的云。蓝色的大海风平浪静；在那海之上、那天之下，四十艘白帆高张的船一路前驶——这是人的眼睛所见过的最壮丽的舰队之一。

各船行驶的速度并不齐一。有的抢到了前头，有的迟迟开不动身；有的渐渐赶上我们，有的又被我们越过。它们行进的庄严，挂满帆布的桅杆之高，还有我这双少年的耳朵自以为能从那些光荣的船体间辨出的一种恍惚而幽深的和谐——好似一首颂歌，那无疑是我自己想像的产物——再加上天气的明媚、空气的清冽、大海的秀美（大海仿佛在湾外欢欣起舞，迎候舰队的到来）——这一切构成了一幅人类心智所能构想的最宏伟的图景。

加的斯城本身，则像一幅徐徐转动的全景画，在我们眼前次第展开，把它那巨大的圆形剧场般的层层面貌一一呈现。初升的太阳照亮它千万扇窗上的玻璃，给它洒满了流动的金点；它的房舍卧在蓝水之上，白得那样纯净，看上去仿佛是方才这一刻才被造出来，或是像那座幻想中的圣热纳罗城（San Genaro）一样从海里升起的。我望得见从防波堤一直延伸到圣卡塔利娜（Santa Catalina）炮台的城墙；我分得清博内特（Bonete）和奥雷洪（Orejon）两座棱堡，认得出那处*卡莱塔*；想到自己从何处起身、如今又身在何方，我的自豪便油然而生。与此同时，那座苏醒中的城市传来的钟声送到我耳边，像某种神秘的音乐，正唤着居民们去望早课弥撒，带着一座大城特有的纷乱的钟鸣。它们此刻在我听来敲得欢欢喜喜，把祝福遥遥送在我们身后——我听着，仿佛那是人的嗓音，在祝我们一路平安；可转眼间，它们又敲得悲悲切切——像是不幸的丧钟；随着我们越驶越远，那钟声愈来愈轻，终于消失在天际。

===Original===
The fleet slowly made its way out of the bay—some of the ships taking several hours in getting fairly to sea. Marcial meanwhile made his comments on each, watching their behavior, laughing them to scorn if they were clumsy, and encouraging them with paternal advice if they were swift and well-handled.

"What a lump that Don Federico is!" he exclaimed as he looked at the *Príncipe de Astúrias* commanded by Gravina. "There goes Mr. Corneta!" he exclaimed as he saw the *Bucentaure* with Villeneuve on board. "He was a clever man that called you the *Rayo*!" (Thunderbolt) he cried ironically, as he watched the ship so named, which was the least manageable of all the fleet. "Well done Papá Ignacio!" he added, pointing to the *Santa Ana* commanded by Álava.

"Hoist your topsail properly, senseless oaf!" he went on, addressing Dumanoir's ship, *Le Formidable*. "That Frenchman keeps a hairdresser to crimp the topsail and to clew up the sails with curling tongs!"

===Chinese===
舰队缓缓驶出海湾——有几艘船花了几个钟头才真正到了外海。马西亚尔趁机对每一艘都加以点评，瞧着它们各自的表现：笨手笨脚的，他讪笑挖苦；麻利驯熟的，他便报以父辈般的鼓励。

"好一个笨家伙，这位堂费德里科（Don Federico）！"他望着格拉维纳指挥的*阿斯图里亚斯亲王号*（Príncipe de Astúrias）嚷道。"科尔内塔先生来了！"他一见维尔纳夫乘坐的*布森陶尔号*又喊。"当初给你起名叫*拉约号*（Rayo）的人，可真是个聪明人！"（意为"霹雳"）他讥讽地叫道，眼睛盯着那艘以此得名的船——全舰队就数它最不听使唤。"好样的，伊格纳西奥老爹（Papá Ignacio）！"他又指着阿拉瓦指挥的*圣安娜号*（Santa Ana）补了一句。

"把上帆给我张得像样些，没脑子的蠢货！"他接着冲迪马努瓦的*福米达布尔号*（Le Formidable）喊。"那法国佬船上养着理发师，专给上帆烫卷花，收起帆来都使卷发钳子！"

===Original===
Towards evening the sky clouded over, and as night fell we could see Cádiz, already at a great distance, gradually vanish in the mist till the last faint outline became one with the darkness. The fleet then steered to the Southward.

All night I kept close to Marcial, as soon as I had seen my master comfortably settled in his cabin. The old sailor, eagerly listened to by a couple of veteran comrades and admirers, was explaining Villeneuve's plan of battle.

===Chinese===
傍晚时分，天上布满了云；夜幕四合，我们望着已经很远的加的斯渐渐隐没在雾霭里，直到最后一缕淡淡的轮廓与黑暗融为一体。舰队随即转舵向南。

把主人安顿进舱里舒舒服服住下之后，我一整夜都紧跟在马西亚尔身边。老水手正讲解着维尔纳夫的作战部署，两三位老伙计兼仰慕者围着他听得入神。

===Original===
"Mr. Corneta," said he, "has divided the fleet into four lines. The vanguard led by Álava consists of six vessels; the centre, likewise of six, is commanded by Mr. Corneta in person; the rear, again of six, is under Dumanoir, and the reserve of twelve ships is led by Don Federico. This seems to me not badly planned. I imagine that the French and Spanish ships are mixed, in order that they may not leave us impaled on the bull's horns as they did at Finisterre.

"From what Don Alfonso tells me the Frenchman says that if the enemy comes up to leeward we are to form in line of battle and attack at once. … This is very pretty talk in the stateroom; but do you think the *Señorito* will be such a booby as to come up to leeward of us? Oh yes—his lordship has not much brains in his figurehead and is sure to let himself be caught in that trap! Well! we shall see—if we see, what the Frenchman expects!—If the enemy gets to windward and attacks us we are to receive him in line of battle, and as he must divide to attack if he does not succeed in breaking our line, it will be quite easy to beat him. Everything is easy to Mr. Corneta." (Applause.) "He says too that he shall give no signals, but expects every captain to do his best. If we should see what I have always prophesied, ever since that accursed subsidy treaty, and that is—but I had better hold my tongue.—Please God…! Well I have always told you that Mr. Corneta does not understand the weapons he has in his hands; there is not room in his head for fifty ships. What can you think of an admiral, who, the day before a battle, sends for his captains and tells each of them to do what he thinks will win the day.—After that!" (Strong expressions of sympathy.) "However, we shall see what we shall see.—But do you just tell me: If we Spanish want to scuttle a few of those English ships, are we not strong enough and many enough to do it? Then why in the world need we ally ourselves with the French, who would not allow us to do anything we had a mind to, but would have us dancing attendance at the end of their towline? Whenever we have had to work with them they have got us into mischief and we have had the worst of it. Well—may God and the Holy Virgin del Cármen be on our side, and rid us of our French friends forever and ever, Amen." (Great applause.)

===Chinese===
"科尔内塔先生，"他说，"把舰队分成了四队。阿拉瓦领的前卫由六艘船组成；中军也是六艘，由科尔内塔先生亲自统带；后卫又是六艘，归迪马努瓦；预备队十二艘，由堂费德里科率领。依我看，这盘子布得不坏。我琢磨着，法国船和西班牙船是掺和开的，免得他们像在菲尼斯特雷角那样，把我们撂在牛角尖上不管。

"据堂阿隆索告诉我，那法国人说，若是敌人从下风头赶上来，我们就列成战列，即刻攻击。……这话在舱房里讲讲自然漂亮；可你们以为，那位小老爷会蠢到从我们的下风头上前来么？噢，可不是——他那位爵爷的船首像里没安多少脑子，保管乖乖钻进那个圈套！好哇！咱们瞧着吧——要是瞧得着的话——瞧瞧法国人巴望的会不会应验！——若是敌人抢了上风来攻，我们就列好战列迎他；他若冲不破我们的战列，就必得分兵来攻，那时要打垮他，易如反掌。什么事到了科尔内塔先生嘴里都不费吹灰之力。"（掌声。）"他还说，他不打信号，只巴望每位舰长各竭其力。至于我打从那该死的津贴条约以来一直预言的事会不会应验——那个嘛——我还是闭嘴的好。——上帝保佑……！唉，我一向跟你们说，科尔内塔先生根本不明白他手里攥着的是些什么家伙；他那个脑袋瓜里，可装不下五十条船。你们说，一位海军上将，开战的前一天把舰长们召了来，吩咐他们各人瞧着怎么赢就怎么打——这算怎么回事！——这还用多说么！"（众人连声附和，深表同情。）"不过，咱们走着瞧。——你们倒说说：若是我们西班牙人想凿沉它几条英国船，难道我们的力气和人手还不够么？那又何苦非要跟法国人结什么盟？他们不容我们干任何想干的事，只叫我们拴在他们拖缆梢上当跟班。凡是同他们搭伙的时候，我们总落得倒霉吃亏。得——愿上帝和卡门圣母保佑我们，把这些法国朋友永远永远地打发掉，阿门。"（掌声大作。）

===Original===
All his audience agreed heartily; the discussion was continued till a late hour, rising from the details of naval warfare to the science of diplomacy. The night was fine and we ran before a fresh breeze—I must be allowed to say "We" in speaking of the fleet. I was so proud of finding myself on board the *Santísima Trinidad* that I began to fancy that I was called to play some important part on this great occasion, and I could not forbear from swaggering about among the sailors to let them see that I was not there for nothing.

===Chinese===
满座听众都衷心赞同；这场谈论一直延续到深夜，从海战的细节一路升到外交的学问。夜色晴好，我们乘着一阵劲风疾驶——说到舰队时，请容我用一句"我们"。登上*圣特立尼达号*令我自豪万分，竟渐渐觉得自己是受命在这重大关头充当什么要角的，忍不住在水手堆里大摇大摆踱来踱去，好让他们瞧瞧，我上这条船来，可不是白来的。

## X

===Original===
On the morning of the 20th there was a stiff breeze blowing and the vessels kept at some distance from each other; but as the wind had moderated soon after noon the admiral signalled that the ships were to form in five lines—the van, centre, and rear, and two lines of reserve. I was enchanted with watching the docile monsters, obediently taking their places; for, although the conditions of naval maneuvers did not admit of great rapidity nor of perfect uniformity in the line, it was impossible to see them without admiration. The wind was from the southwest, according to Marcial, and the fleet, catching the breeze on the starboard quarter, ran towards the straits. During the night a few lights were seen and by dawn on the 21st we saw twenty-seven ships to windward, among which Marcial pointed out three as three-deckers. By eight o’clock the thirty-three vessels of the enemy’s fleet were in sight, forming two columns. Our fleet displayed a wide front, and to all appearance Nelson’s two columns, advancing in a wedge, were coming down upon us so as to cut our lines through the centre and rear.

This was the position of the hostile fleets when the *Bucentaure* signalled that we were to put about; maybe you do not understand this. It means that we were to turn completely round and that whereas the wind was on our port side it would now be on the starboard, so that we should sail in the opposite direction. The ships’ heads were now turned northwards and this maneuver, which was intended to place us to windward of Cádiz so that we might reach it in case of disaster, was severely criticised on board the *Trinidad*, especially by Marcial, who said:

“The line of battle is all broken up; it was bad before and is worse now.”

In point of fact what had been the vanguard was now in the rear and the reserve ships, which as I heard said, were the best, were hindmost of all. The wind had fallen and the ships, being of various tonnage and inefficiently manned, the new line could not form with due precision; some of the vessels moved quickly and rushed forward; others went slowly, hanging back or losing their course, and forming a wide gap that broke the line before the enemy took the trouble of doing it.

===Chinese===
二十日早晨，风头正紧，各舰彼此相隔颇远；但午后不久风势转缓，舰队司令便打出信号，命各舰列成五路纵队——前卫、中军、后卫，外加两路预备队。我出神地望着这些驯顺的巨兽服服帖帖地各就各位；虽说海上机动的条件既容不得十分迅捷，也难求战线整齐划一，可任谁见了都不能不叹赏。据马西亚尔说，风是西南风，舰队兜着右舷船尾吹来的风，朝海峡方向驶去。夜间望见了几点灯火，到二十一日拂晓，我们看见上风处有二十七艘军舰，其中三艘，经马西亚尔指点，认出是三层甲板的大舰。到八点钟，敌方舰队三十三艘舰只已尽数在望，排成两路纵队。我们舰队摆开一道宽大的正面，看那架势，纳尔逊的两路纵队正结成楔形阵扑将过来，要从中军和后卫两处切断我们的战列线。

两军对峙、阵势正是如此之际，*布森陶尔号*打出信号，命我们掉头转向；这话你们也许听不明白。意思是：全舰队要来个彻头彻尾的大掉头，原先风在左舷，掉头之后便换到右舷，我们也就朝相反的方向行驶了。各舰舰首由此转而向北。这一机动，本意是让舰队占据加的斯的上风位置，万一遭了大难也好退回港去；可它在*圣特立尼达号*上挨了狠狠的批评，批得最凶的又数马西亚尔，他说：

“战列线全给搅散了；原先就够糟，如今是糟上加糟。”

实际上，原先的前卫如今落到了后卫，而据我听人说本是全军精华的那些预备舰船，竟统统掉在了末尾。风已经软了下去，各舰吨位参差，人手又不齐整，新的战列线怎么也列不精确；有的船走得快，一抢就抢到了前头；有的走得慢，磨磨蹭蹭，或者迷失了航向，结果裂出一道大豁口——不等敌人劳神动手，战线就先自断了。

===Original===
“Reform the line” was now the signal; but, though a good ship answers her helm with wonderful docility, it is not so easy to manage as a horse. As he stood watching the movements of the ships nearest to us, Marcial observed: “The line is wider than the Milky Way. If the *Señorito* cuts through it, Heaven help us! we shall not be able to sail in any sort of order; they will shave our heads for us if they fire upon us. They are going to give us a dose through the centre and how can the *San Juan* and the *Bahama* come up to support us from the rear—or the *Neptuno* and the *Rayo* which are in front.” (Murmurs of applause.) “Besides, here we are to leeward and the Greatcoats can pick and choose where they will attack us, while all we can do is to defend ourselves as best we may. All I have to say is: God get us well out of the scrape and deliver us from the French forever and ever, Amen.”

The sun had now nearly reached the meridian and the enemy was coming down upon us.

“And is this a proper hour to begin a battle?” asked the old sailor indignantly. “Twelve o’clock in the day!”

But he did not dare to express his views publicly and these discussions were confined to a small circle into which I, with my eternal and insatiable curiosity, had squeezed myself. I do not know why, but it seemed to me that there was an expression of dissatisfaction on every face. The officers on the quarterdeck, and the sailors and noncommissioned officers at the bows, stood watching the ships to leeward, quite out of the line of battle, four of which ought to have been in the centre.

===Chinese===
这时的信号是「重整战线」；然而，一条好船尽管应舵驯顺得出奇，驾驭起来却终究不如一匹马那样听使唤。马西亚尔站在那里，望着离我们最近的几条军舰的动静，发表议论道：“这道战线比银河还宽。*小老爷*要是从中间凿进来，老天爷保佑！咱们就再也排不出半点阵形了；他们一开炮，会把咱们的脑袋剃个精光。他们要从中路给咱们灌上一剂药，*圣胡安号*和*巴哈马号*（Bahama）怎么从后头赶上来接应？——还有排在前头的*内普图诺号*（Neptuno）和*拉约号*（Rayo），又怎么办？”（四下响起一片低低的喝彩。）“再说了，咱们占着下风，大氅佬们尽可以挑肥拣瘦，爱从哪儿进攻就从哪儿进攻；咱们能做的，只有尽力自卫。我只想说一句：上帝保佑咱们平平安安脱了这场灾，叫我们永远永远摆脱法国人，阿门。”

太阳这时差不多升到了中天，敌人正朝我们压过来。

“这算是开仗的正经时辰吗？”老水手愤愤地质问道，“大白天的十二点钟！”

不过他不敢把意见公开亮出来，这些议论只限于一个小圈子，而我凭着那股永不餍足的好奇心，早把自己塞进了这个圈子。不知怎的，我总觉得人人脸上都挂着不满。后甲板上的军官们，艏部的水兵和军士们，都站着观望下风处那几条军舰——它们完全脱出了战列线，而其中本该有四条位居中军。

===Original===
I forgot to mention one preliminary in which I myself had borne a hand. Early in the morning the decks were cleared for action, and when all was ready for serving the guns and working the ship, I heard someone say: “The sand—bring the sand.” Marcial pulled me by the ear, and taking me to one of the hatchways set me in a line with some of the pressed men, ship’s boys, and other supernumeraries. A number of sailors were posted on the ladders from the hatchway to the hold and between decks, and in this way were hauling up sacks of sand. Each man handed one to the man next to him and so it was passed on without much labor. A great quantity of sacks were thus brought up from hand to hand, and to my great astonishment they were emptied out on the upper deck, the poop, and the forecastle, the sand being spread about so as to cover all the planking; and the same thing was done between decks. My curiosity prompted me to ask the boy who stood next to me what this was for.

“For the blood,” he said very coolly.

“For the blood!” I exclaimed unable to repress a shudder. I looked at the sand—I looked at the men who were busily employed at this task—and for a moment I felt I was a coward. However, my imagination reverted to the ideas which had previously filled it, and relieved my mind of its alarms; I thought no more of anything but victory and a happy issue.

Everything was ready for serving the guns and the ammunition was passed up from the storerooms to the decks by a chain of men, like that which had brought up the sandbags.

===Chinese===
有一桩临战的预备活计，我自己也曾搭过一把手，方才忘了交代。一大早，各层甲板已按战斗要求清理停当；等到炮位备妥、全舰随时可以开炮操船的时候，我听见有人喊：“沙子——把沙子搬来。”马西亚尔扯着我的耳朵，把我领到一处舱口，叫我跟一些强征来的水兵、舰上的少年水手以及其他编外人员排成一列。一批水兵把守着从舱口直到底舱和各层甲板之间的扶梯，就这样把一袋袋沙子往上传递。每人把手里的袋子递给身旁的人，一袋袋便不费什么力气地传了上去。大批沙袋就这样手手相传地运了上来，而叫我大为惊异的是，人们竟把沙子倒在上甲板、艉楼和艏楼上，摊撒开来，把船板盖了个严严实实；各层甲板之间也是如法炮制。好奇心怂恿我去问身旁那个少年，这是干什么用的。

“防血用的。”他说得平平淡淡。

“防血用的！”我失声喊道，禁不住打了个寒噤。我望望那些沙子——又望望那些埋头忙这活计的人——一时间，竟觉得自己是个懦夫。好在想象力又把我带回到先前盘踞满脑子的那些念头上，替我卸下了心头的惊惶；此外我什么都不想了，一心只想着胜利和圆满的结局。

开炮的一切准备已经就绪，弹药也靠一条人链从弹药库传上甲板，正像方才传递沙袋那样。

===Original===
The English advanced to attack us in two sections. One came straight down upon us, and at its head, which was the point of the wedge, sailed a large ship carrying the admiral’s flag. This, as I afterwards learned, was the *Victory*, commanded by Nelson. At the head of the other line was the *Royal Sovereign*, commanded by Collingwood. All these names, and the strategical plan of the battle, were not known to me till later.

My recollections, which are vividly distinct as to all the graphic and picturesque details, fail me with regard to the scheme of action which was beyond my comprehension at the time. All that I picked from Marcial, combined with what I subsequently learned, sufficed to give me a good idea of the arrangement of our fleets; and for the better intelligence of the reader I give in the next page a list of our ships, indicating the gaps left by those that had not come up, and the nationality of each.

It was now a quarter to twelve. The fatal moment was approaching. The anxiety was general, and I do not speak merely from what was going on in my own mind, for I was absorbed in watching the ship which was said to contain Nelson, and for some time was hardly aware of what was going on round me.

===Chinese===
英国人分作两路攻上前来。一路径直朝我们压下来，打头的正是楔形的尖端，驶着一艘挂着海军上将旗的大舰。这艘船，我后来才知道，是纳尔逊指挥的*胜利号*（Victory）。另一路的领头者是科林伍德指挥的*皇家主权号*（Royal Sovereign）。这些名字，连同这一仗的战略部署，我都是过后才弄明白的。

我的回忆，凡属生动如绘、历历如画的细节，至今清楚分明；唯独讲到作战部署——那在当时就超出我的理解力——它们就帮不上我的忙了。我从马西亚尔那里零星拾掇来的，加上后来补学的知识，合在一起，足够让我对我方舰队的部署有个清楚的概念；为便于读者明了，我在下一页开列一份我方舰只的清单，注明落伍缺席者留下的空档，并标明每条船的国籍。

这时已是十一点三刻。那决定命运的时刻步步逼近。人人心头焦灼——我这话并不只是说自己心里如何，因为我当时全神贯注地盯着据说载着纳尔逊的那艘船，有一阵子几乎不知道自己周围在发生什么。

===Original===
Suddenly a terrible order was given by our captain—the boatswains repeated it; the sailors flew to the tops; the blocks and ropes creaked, the topsails flapped in the wind.

“Take in sail!” cried Marcial, with a good round oath. “The infernal idiot is making us work back.”

And then I understood that the *Trinidad* was to slacken her speed so as to run alongside of the *Bucentaure*, because the *Victory* seemed to be taking measures to run in between those two ships and so cut the line in the middle.

In watching the working of our vessel I could see that a great many of the crew had not that nimble ease which is usually characteristic of sailors who, like Marcial, are familiar with war and tempests. Among the soldiers several were suffering from seasickness and were clinging to the ropes to save themselves from falling. There were among them many brave souls, especially among the volunteers, but for the most part they were impressed men, obeying orders with an ill-will and not feeling, I am very sure, the smallest impulse of patriotism. As I afterwards learned, nothing but the battle itself made them worthy to fight. In spite of the wide differences in the moral stamp of all these men, I believe that during the solemn moments that immediately preceded the first shot a thought of God came to every mortal there.

===Chinese===
突然，我们的舰长下了一道可怕的命令——水手长们一声声复述下去；水兵们飞身攀上桅楼；滑车与缆索吱嘎作响，上帆在风里噼噼啪啪地鼓荡。

“收帆！”马西亚尔嚷道，痛痛快快骂了一句粗话。“这该死的蠢货，硬要咱们开倒车。”

于是我才明白：*圣特立尼达号*要放慢速度，好靠上*布森陶尔号*并肩而行，因为*胜利号*看来正设法要插进这两舰之间，从正中把战线切断。

望着我们军舰上人来人往的操作，我看得出，船员里有许多人欠缺那种机敏利落——那是像马西亚尔这样见惯了战火与风暴的水手所特有的本色。士兵当中有几位正晕着船，紧抓着缆索，才不至于栽倒。这些人里头不乏勇毅之魂，志愿兵中尤其多；但大多数是被强征来的，老大不情愿地服从命令，而且我可以肯定，他们心里连一星半点爱国的冲动都没有。据我后来所知，唯有战斗本身，才使他们配得上作战二字。尽管这些人精神品格千差万别，我却相信，在第一声炮响之前的庄严时刻，在场每一个凡人的心里，都掠过一个关于上帝的念头。

===Original===
So far as I am concerned, in all my life my soul has never gone through any experiences, to compare with those of that hour. In spite of my youth, I was quite capable of understanding the gravity of the occasion, and for the first time in my life, my mind was filled with grand ideas, lofty aspirations and heroic thoughts. A conviction that we must conquer was so firmly rooted in my mind that I felt quite pitiful towards the English, and wondered to see them so eagerly advancing to certain destruction. For the first time too I fully understood the ideal of patriotism, and my heart responded to the thought with a glow of feeling such as I had never experienced before. Until now my mother-country had been embodied in my mind in the persons of its rulers—such as the King and his famous minister, for whom I felt different degrees of respect. As I knew no more of history than I had picked up in the streets, it was to me a matter of course that everybody’s enthusiasm must be fired by knowing that the Spaniards had, once upon a time, killed a great number of Moors, and, since then, swarms of French and of English. I considered my countrymen as models of valor; but valor, as I conceived of it, was as like barbarity as one egg is like another; and with such ideas as these, patriotism had been to me nothing more than boastful pride in belonging to a race of exterminators of Moors.

But in the pause that preceded the battle I understood the full significance of that divine word; the conception of nationality, of devotion to a mother-country, was suddenly born in my soul, lighting it up, as it were, and revealing a thousand wonderful possibilities—as the rising sun dissipates the darkness that has hidden a beautiful landscape. I thought of my native land as a vast place full of people all united in brotherly regard—of society as divided into families, married couples to be held together, and children to be educated—of honor, to be cherished and defended; I imagined an unspoken agreement among all these human beings to help and protect each other against any attack from without, and I understood that these vessels had been constructed by them all for the defence of their native land; that is to say, for the soil on which they lived, the fields watered by their sweat, the homes where their ancestors had dwelt, the gardens where their children played, the colonies discovered and conquered by their forefathers, the harbors where their ships found shelter after long voyages—the magazines where they stored their wealth—the Church which was the mausoleum of those they had loved, the dwelling-place of their saints, and the ark of their belief—the public places where they might take their pleasure, the private homes where the venerable household gods, handed down from generation to generation, seemed to symbolize the perpetuity of the nation—their family hearth round which the smoke-dyed walls seem still to reecho with the time-honored legends with which the grand dame soothes the flightiness or the naughtiness of the little ones, the street where friendly faces meet and smile—the field, the sea, the sky—everything which from the moment of birth makes up the sum of existence, from the crib of a pet animal to the time-honored throne of the king; every object into which the soul seems to go forth to live, as if the body that clothes it were too narrow a shell.

===Chinese===
就我自己而论，平生的心灵从来不曾经历过可与那个时刻相比的事。别看我年纪小，那场面的分量我完全掂得出来，而且生平头一回，我心里涨满了宏大的观念、高远的抱负和英雄的思想。我们必胜的信念在我心里扎得那样深，以致我对英国人竟生出几分怜悯，眼见他们那样急切地扑向必毁之途，只觉得纳闷。也是生平头一回，我完全懂得了爱国主义的理想，我的心为这个念头报以一股从未体验过的热流。在那以前，祖国在我的头脑里，向来是由它的统治者们的形象来体现的——比如国王和他那位赫赫有名的大臣，我对他们怀着程度不等的敬意。我对历史的全部知识，无非是街头巷尾拾来的那一点，于是理所当然地认定：一想到西班牙人当年杀过千千万万的摩尔人，此后又杀过成群结队的法国人和英国人，人人的热血就必定沸腾。我把我的同胞看作勇敢的楷模；只是我心目中那种勇敢，同野蛮残暴简直像一个蛋跟另一个蛋；怀着这样的观念，爱国主义于我，不过是自夸出身于一个把摩尔人斩尽杀绝的种族罢了。

可就在大战前的那段静默里，我懂得了这个神圣字眼的全部意义；民族的观念、为祖国献身的观念，骤然在我灵魂里降生，仿佛把它照亮了，展现出千百种奇妙的可能——一如朝阳驱散遮蔽秀美景色的黑暗。我把故土想成一片广袤的天地，住满了亲如手足、同心相系的人民——把社会想成由一个个家庭组成，有该厮守终身的夫妻，有该教养成人的儿女——还有荣誉，该珍重，该捍卫；我想象所有这些人之间有一份不成文的盟约：彼此扶助，彼此护卫，共御一切外来的攻击；我于是明白，这些军舰正是他们全体合力建造，为的是保卫他们的祖国；也就是说，保卫他们生息的泥土，他们汗水浇灌的田野，他们祖先栖居的家宅，他们儿女嬉戏的花园，他们先辈发现并征服的殖民地，他们的船只远航归来栖身的港湾——他们储存财富的仓廪——那教堂，是他们挚爱者的陵庙，是他们诸圣的居所，是他们信仰的方舟——他们行乐的公共场所，那些私宅，宅中世代相传、令人敬畏的家宅神祇，仿佛正象征着民族的绵延不绝——他们的家常炉灶，烟熏火燎的四壁似乎还在回响着古老的传说，老主母正是拿这些传说，哄得欢蹦乱跳、淘气撒泼的小家伙们安静下来——友善面孔相逢一笑的街道——田野，海洋，天空——人自降生那一刻起构成一生总和的一切，从一只爱畜的摇篮到国王那历史悠久的宝座；每一件事物，灵魂仿佛都要前去栖居其中，仿佛裹着它的这具身体，竟是个太狭窄的躯壳。

===Original===
I believed too that the disputes between Spain and France or England were always about something that those countries ought to give up to us, and in which Spain could not, on the whole, be wrong. Her self-defence seemed to me as legitimate as the aggression was brutal; and as I had always heard that justice must triumph, I never doubted of victory. Looking up at our red and yellow flag—the colors nearest to that of fire—I felt my bosom swell, and could not restrain a few tears of enthusiasm and excitement; I thought of Cádiz, of Vejer, of the whole Spanish nation assembled, as it were, on a vast platform and looking on with eager anxiety; and all this tide of emotion lifted up my heart to God to whom I put up a prayer, which was neither a Paternoster nor an Ave, but a gush of inspiration that came to me at the moment.

A sudden shock startled me from my ecstasy, terrifying me with its violent vibration. The first broadside had been fired.

===Chinese===
我还相信，西班牙与法国或英国之间的争端，争的从来都是那些国家本该让给我们的东西，而在这种事情上，西班牙总的说来是不可能错的。她的自卫在我看来何等正当，对方的侵略在我看来就何等残暴；又因为我素来听人说正义必胜，我对胜利也就从未有过一丝怀疑。仰起头望着我们那面红黄两色的旗帜——最接近火焰的颜色——只觉胸中激荡，禁不住淌下几滴激昂兴奋的眼泪；我想起加的斯，想起韦赫尔，想起整个西班牙民族仿佛全都聚在一座巨大的看台上，焦灼地翘首观望；这情感的浪潮把我的心高高托起，送到上帝面前，我向他做了一篇祈祷——那既不是《天主经》，也不是《圣母经》，而是那一刻涌上心头的一股灵感的倾泻。

一声突如其来的巨震把我从忘我之境中惊醒，那剧烈的震颤吓得我魂不附体。第一轮齐射打响了。

## XI

===Original===
A vessel in the rear had been the first to fire on the *Royal Sovereign*, commanded by Collingwood, and while that ship carried on the fight with the *Santa Ana* the *Victory* came down on us. On board the *Trinidad* everyone was anxious to open fire; but our captain would not give the word till he saw a favorable opportunity. Meanwhile, as if the ships were in such close communication that a slow-match was lighted from one to the other, the fire ran along from the *Santa Ana* in the middle, to each end of the line.

The *Victory* fired first on the *Redoutable*, and being repulsed, came up to the windward of the *Trinidad*. The moment had come for us; a hundred voices cried “fire!”—loudly echoing the word of command, and fifty round-shot were hurled against the flank of the English man-of-war. For a minute I could see nothing of the enemy for the smoke, while he, as if blind with rage, came straight down upon us before the wind. Just within gunshot he put the ship about and gave us a broadside. In the interval between our firing and theirs, our crew, who had taken note of the damage done to the enemy, had gained in enthusiasm. The guns were rapidly served, though not without some hitches owing to want of experience in some of the gunners. Marcial would have been only too glad to undertake the management of one of the cannon, but his mutilated body was not equal to the heroism of his spirit. He was forced to be satisfied with superintending the delivery of the charges and encouraging the gunners by word and gesture.

===Chinese===
殿后的一艘战舰率先向科林伍德统领的*皇家主权号*（Royal Sovereign）开了火；那舰与*圣安娜号*（Santa Ana）缠斗方酣，*胜利号*（Victory）便朝我们压了过来。*圣特立尼达号*上人人摩拳擦掌急着开火，可我们的舰长不见有利战机，就是不下令。与此同时，炮火仿佛各舰挨得极近、一条缓燃火绳从这条船引到那条船上似的，从中路的*圣安娜号*起，沿着战列向两端一路烧延开去。

*胜利号*最先朝*可畏号*（Redoutable）开火，被顶了回来，便抢到*圣特立尼达号*的上风处。我们的时机到了；一百条嗓门轰然应和着口令，齐喊“开火！”，五十颗实心弹一齐砸向那艘英国军舰的侧舷。有那么一分钟，硝烟里我全然望不见敌舰的影子；而它，像是气疯了眼，乘风直朝我们扑下来。刚进炮程，它便掉转船头，还了我们一舷齐射。在我们开炮与它回击的间隙里，我们的炮手掂出了敌舰受创的分量，劲头愈发高涨。各炮伺候得飞快，只是几个炮手经验欠缺，难免偶有磕绊。马西亚尔巴不得亲手操办一尊炮，可他那残缺的身子配不上他一腔的英豪气概，只好满足于督着传送弹药，用手势和言语给炮手们鼓劲。

===Original===
The *Bucentaure*, just at our stern, was, like us, firing on the *Victory* and the *Téméraire*, another powerful English vessel. It seemed as though the *Victory* must fall into our hands, for the *Trinidad*’s fire had cut her tackle to pieces, and we saw with pride that her mizzenmast had gone by the board.

In the excitement of this first onslaught I scarcely perceived that some of our men were wounded or killed. I had chosen a place where I thought I should be least in the way, and never took my eyes off the captain who stood on the quarterdeck, issuing his orders with heroic coolness; and I wondered to see my master, no less calm though less enthusiastic, encouraging the officers and men in his quavering voice.

“Ah!” said I to myself, “if only Doña Francisca could see him now!”

===Chinese===
紧贴我们舰尾的*布森陶尔号*，同我们一样，正朝*胜利号*和另一艘强劲的英舰*忒米赖尔号*（Téméraire）开火。眼看*胜利号*多半要落到我们手里了：*圣特立尼达号*的炮火已把它的索具打得稀烂，我们还骄傲地望见它的后桅连根栽进了海里。

头一场恶战的亢奋之中，我几乎没察觉我们已有人挂彩、有人阵亡。我给自己拣了个自以为最不碍事的角落，眼睛一刻不离舰长——他站在后甲板上发号施令，镇定得英雄一般；我也惊奇地望着我的主人，他虽不那么激昂，沉着的功夫却毫不逊色，正用他那发颤的嗓音给军官和水兵们打气。

“啊！”我暗自感叹，“堂娜·弗朗西斯卡这会儿要是能看见他该多好！”

===Original===
I am bound to confess that at times I felt desperately frightened, and would gladly have hidden myself at the very bottom of the hold, while, at others, I was filled with an almost delirious courage, when I longed to see the glorious spectacle from the most dangerous posts. However, I will set aside my own insignificant individuality and relate the most terrible crisis of our fight with the *Victory*. The *Trinidad* was doing her immense mischief when the *Téméraire*, by a wonderfully clever maneuver, slipped in between the two vessels thus sheltering her consort from our fire. She then proceeded to cut through the line behind the *Trinidad*, and as the *Bucentaure*, under fire, had got so close alongside of the *Trinidad* that their yards touched, there was a wide space beyond into which the *Téméraire* rushed down and, going about immediately, came up on our lee and delivered a broadside on that quarter, till then untouched. At the same time the *Neptune*, another large English ship, ran in where the *Victory* had previously been, while the *Victory* veered round so that, in a few minutes, the *Trinidad* was surrounded by the enemy and riddled on all sides.

===Chinese===
我不能不承认，我时时怕得要命，恨不得一头钻进舱底最深处藏起来；可另一些时候，我又满怀一股近乎癫狂的勇气，巴不得站到最危险的岗位上，去观赏这场壮丽的奇观。不过，还是把我这无足轻重的小人物搁到一边，来讲讲我们同*胜利号*交手中最凶险的关头。*圣特立尼达号*正大发神威、大施荼毒，冷不防*忒米赖尔号*使出一手漂亮至极的机动，插进两舰之间，把它的伙伴从我们炮火底下遮护了出去。它随即切进*圣特立尼达号*背后的战列；*布森陶尔号*挨着炮火，早已紧贴到*圣特立尼达号*一侧，两船帆桁都搭在了一处，那后面便空出一片开阔水面，*忒米赖尔号*一头扎进去，随即掉头，抢上我们的下风舷，朝那一侧——直到那时还毫发无伤的一侧——倾下一舷齐射。与此同时，另一艘英国巨舰*尼普顿号*（Neptune）又压进了*胜利号*原先的阵位，*胜利号*则转过方向；不出几分钟，*圣特立尼达号*已陷敌围，周身上下处处中弹开花。

===Original===
From my master’s face, from Uriarte’s heroic fury, and from a volley of oaths delivered by Marcial and his friends, I understood that we were lost and the idea of defeat was anguish to my soul. The line of the combined fleets was broken at several points, and the bad order in which they had formed after turning round, gave place to the most disastrous confusion. We were surrounded by the enemy whose artillery kept up a perfect hail of round and grapeshot on our ship, and on the *Bucentaure* as well. The *Agustín*, the *Héros*, and the *Leandro* were engaged at some distance from us where they had rather more sea-room, while the *Trinidad*, and the Admiral’s ship, utterly hemmed in and driven to extremities by the genius of the great Nelson, were fighting heroically—no longer in hopes of a victory which was impossible but anxious, at any rate, to perish gloriously.

The white hairs which now cover my old head almost stand on end as I remember those terrible hours, from two to four in the afternoon. I think of those five ships, not as mere machines of war obeying the will of man, but as living giants, huge creatures fighting on their own account, carried into action by their sails as though they were active limbs and using the fearful artillery they bore in their sides for their personal defence. As I looked at them then, my fancy could not help personifying them and to this hour I feel as though I could see them coming up, defying each other, going about to fire a broadside, rushing furiously up to board, drawing back to gather more force, mocking or threatening the enemy;—I can fancy them expressing their suffering when wounded or loftily breathing their last, like a gladiator who in his agony forgets not the dignity which beseems him;—I can imagine that I hear the voices of the crews like the murmur of an oppressed sufferer, sometimes eager with enthusiasm, sometimes a dull roar of desperation the precursor of destruction, sometimes a hymn of triumph in anticipation of victory, or a hideous storm of voices lost in space and giving way to the awful silence of disgrace and defeat.

===Chinese===
从主人的脸色，从乌里亚尔特（Uriarte）怒狮般的狂暴，从马西亚尔和他那帮弟兄劈头盖脸的一串咒骂，我明白了：我们完了；战败的念头像刀绞一样剜着我的魂灵。联合舰队的战列已有多处被凿断，掉头转向之后本就欠佳的队形，此刻更让位给一场灾难性的大乱。我们陷在敌人包围之中，对方的炮火在我舰和*布森陶尔号*头上，泼下一阵实心弹与葡萄弹交织的不歇的弹雨。*圣阿古斯丁号*、*埃罗号*（Héros）和*利安德号*（Leandro）在离我们稍远处接战，那边海域还算宽敞，有些回旋余地；而*圣特立尼达号*连同司令座舰，被伟大的纳尔逊的天才彻底围死、逼入绝境，仍在作英雄的搏杀——已不再指望那不可能的胜利，只求至少死得光荣。

如今覆满我这颗老脑袋的白发，一想起午后两点到四点那几个可怕的钟头，几乎要根根倒竖。追想那五艘巨舰，我从不觉得它们是单单听命于人意的战争机器，而觉得是活生生的巨人，是各自为战的庞然巨兽：船帆是它们的活络臂膀，载着它们扑入厮杀；护侧那可怖的排炮，则充作它们自卫的爪牙。当年望着它们，我的想象力不由自主地把它们拟成了人；直到此时此刻，我仍仿佛看得见它们逼上前去、互相叫阵，看得见它们掉转船身放一舷齐射，看得见它们狂怒地冲上去要跳帮肉搏，又退下来蓄一股新力，嘲弄着或威吓着敌人；——我仿佛看得见它们负伤时表露痛楚，或高傲地咽下最后一口气，像一位角斗士，在垂死的苦痛里仍不忘自己应有的尊严；——我仿佛听得见各舰船员的声息，像受难者压抑的呻吟：有时热忱激越，有时是绝望的闷吼——覆灭的前奏；有时是预祝凯旋的颂歌，有时则化作一阵骇人的声浪喧嚣，散入空茫，让位于蒙耻战败那可怕的死寂。

===Original===
The scene on board the *Santísima Trinidad* was nothing short of infernal. All attempt at working the ship had been abandoned, for it did not and could not move. The only thing to be done was to serve the guns with the utmost rapidity, and to do as much damage to the enemy as they had done to us. The English small-shot rent the sails just as if huge and invisible nails were tearing slits in them. The splinters of timber and of masts, the stout cables cut through as if they were straws, the capstans, spindles, and other heavy machinery torn from their place by the enemy’s fire, strewed the deck so that there was scarcely room to move. Every minute men, till then full of life, fell on deck or into the sea; the blasphemy of those who were fighting mingled with the cries of the wounded, till it was impossible to say whether the dying were defying God or the living crying to him for mercy while they fought.

I offered my services for a melancholy task, which was carrying the wounded into the cockpit where the surgeons were busy doing their utmost. Some were dead before we could get them there, and others had to suffer painful operations before their exhausted bodies could be left to repose.

Then I had the extreme satisfaction of helping the carpenters who were constantly employed in repairing the holes made in the ship’s sides; but my youth and inefficiency made me less useful than I would fain have been.

Blood was flowing in rivulets on the upper and lower decks and in spite of the sand the motion of the ship carried it from side to side making sinister patterns on the boards. The cannonballs, fired at such a short range, mutilated those they killed in a terrible manner, and I saw more than one man still standing with his head blown away, the force of the shock not having been great enough to fling the victim into the sea, whose waters would have extinguished almost painlessly the last sensation of existence. Other balls struck a mast or against the bulwarks, carrying off a hail of hot splinters that pierced and stung like arrows. The rifle-shots from the tops and the round-shot from the carronades dealt a more lingering and painful death, and there was hardly a man to be seen who did not bear the marks, more or less severe, of the foe’s iron and lead.

===Chinese===
*圣特立尼达号*（Santísima Trinidad）舰上的景象，简直同地狱没有两样。操纵军舰的一切尝试都已放弃，因为它动弹不得，也不可能动弹。唯一可做的，就是以最快的速度操炮，敌人加于我们的损害，也要如数奉还。英国的霰弹把船帆撕出一条条口子，仿佛有巨大而看不见的钉子在帆面上豁开裂缝。木料和桅杆的碎片，像稻草一般被削断的粗缆，被敌军炮火从原位掀翻的绞盘、转轴和其余笨重器械，狼藉满地铺满甲板，简直没有挪脚的地方。前一刻还生龙活虎的人，转眼便倒在甲板上或栽进海里；战斗者的咒骂同伤者的哀号搅作一团，分不清垂死的人是在亵渎天主，还是活着的人一边厮杀一边向祂哀告求饶。

我自告奋勇去做一桩凄楚的差事：把伤员抬进下层医疗舱，军医们正在那里拼尽全力。有的人还没抬到就断了气；另一些人还要熬过痛楚的手术，才能让耗竭的身子躺下将息。

后来我又极其称心地帮起了木匠们的忙——他们一直在忙着修补舰壳上被打出的窟窿；只是我年纪轻、手艺潮，使不出我巴望能使的那份力气。

上甲板下甲板上，鲜血淌成了一道道细流；尽管撒了沙，船身一晃，血还是从这边荡到那边，在木板上洇出狰狞的花样。相距这么近打出的炮弹，把毙命之人的躯体残毁得骇人听闻；我不止看见一个人脑袋已被轰掉，身子却还立在那儿——冲击的力道不够大，没能把死者抛进海里，而海水的凉，本可以几乎无痛地熄灭他最后一丝生的知觉。另一些炮弹撞上桅杆或舷墙，迸起一阵滚烫的碎片，像箭镞一样扎人蜇人。桅盘里射来的步枪弹和卡龙炮的实心弹，则送给人更缠绵、更痛苦的死法；放眼望去，几乎找不到一个人身上不带敌人铁与铅留下的、或轻或重的印记。

===Original===
The crew—the soul of the ship—being thus thrashed by the storm of battle and utterly unable to deal equal destruction, saw death at hand though resolved to die with the courage of despair; and the ship itself—the glorious body—shivered under the cannonade. I could feel her shudder under the fearful blows; her timbers cracked, her beams creaked, her ribs groaned like limbs on the rack, and the deck trembled under my feet with audible throbs, as though the whole huge creature was indignant at the sufferings of her crew. Meanwhile the water was pouring in at a hundred holes in the riddled hull, and the hold was fast filling.

The *Bucentaure*, the Admiral’s vessel, surrendered before our very eyes. Villeneuve struck to the *Victory*. When once the leader of the fleet was gone, what hope was there for the other ships? The French flag vanished from the gallant vessel’s mast and she ceased firing. The *San Augustín* and the *Héros* still persevered, and the *Rayo* and *Neptuno*, of the van, made an effort to rescue us from the enemy that was battering us. I could see what was going on in the immediate neighborhood of the *Trinidad*, though nothing was to be seen of the rest of the line. The wind had fallen to a calm and the smoke settled down over our heads shrouding everything in its dense white wreaths which it was impossible for eye to pierce. We could catch a glimpse now and then of a distant ship, mysteriously magnified by some inexplicable optical effect; I believe indeed that the terror of that supreme moment exaggerated every impression.

Presently this dense cloud was dispersed for an instant—but in what a fearful manner! A tremendous explosion, louder than all the thousand guns of the fleet fired at once, paralyzed every man and filled every soul with dread; and just as the ear was stunned by the terrific roar an intense flash lighted up the two fleets, rending the veil of smoke and revealing the whole panorama of the battle. This catastrophe had taken place on the side towards the South where the rear line had been posted.

“A ship blown up!” said one to another. But opinion differed as to whether it was the *Santa Ana*, the *Argonauta*, the *Ildefonso*, or the *Bahama*. We afterwards learned that it was a Frenchman, the *Achille*. The explosion scattered in a myriad fragments what had a few moments before been a noble ship of 74 guns and 600 men. But a few seconds after we had already forgotten the explosion in thinking only of ourselves.

===Chinese===
船员——舰船的灵魂——就这样在战斗的风暴里挨着鞭笞，全然无力回敬同等的毁灭；死亡近在眼前，他们却抱定绝望者的勇气，唯求一死。而舰船本身——那光荣的躯壳——在炮火下瑟瑟发抖。我能觉出她在可怕的打击下阵阵战栗：船板咔咔碎裂，横梁吱嘎作响，肋骨像受了酷刑的四肢一样哀号，甲板在我脚下带着听得见的悸动震颤，仿佛这整个庞然大物正为她船员的苦难而震怒。与此同时，海水正从被打成筛子般的舰壳上百来个窟窿里灌进来，舱底飞快地涨着。

舰队司令的座舰*布森陶尔号*，就在我们眼皮底下投降了。维尔纳夫向*胜利号*降了旗。主帅一去，其余各舰还有什么指望？法国旗从那艘英勇军舰的桅顶上消失了，它停止了射击。*圣阿古斯丁号*和*埃罗号*仍在苦撑，前卫的*拉约号*（Rayo）和*内普图诺号*（Neptuno）也奋力想来把我们从痛揍我们的敌手那里解救出去。*圣特立尼达号*左近的动静我都看在眼里，战列其余部分却什么也望不见。风完全平息了，硝烟沉落在我们的头顶，用它浓白的一团团卷裹住万事万物，任什么眼睛也扎不透。偶尔能瞥见远处一艘船，被某种说不清道不明的光学效应神秘地放大了；我实在相信，那生死关头的恐怖把一切印象都夸大了。

不一会儿，这团浓云被驱散了片刻——却是何等可怖的方式！一声惊天动地的爆炸，比全舰队千门大炮一齐开火还要响，把每个人都震得呆若木鸡，把每个魂灵都灌满了恐惧；耳朵被那骇人的巨响震聋的当口，一道炽烈的闪光照亮了两支舰队，撕开硝烟的帷幕，把整幅战场的全景暴露出来。这场大难发生在南面，也就是后卫战列原先布防的那一侧。

“有船炸了！”人们你一言我一语。可它是*圣安娜号*、*阿尔戈纳塔号*、*圣伊尔德丰索号*，还是*巴哈马号*（Bahama），众说纷纭。我们后来才知道，那是法国船*阿希尔号*（Achille）。一瞬之间，几分钟前还是一艘载着七十四门炮、六百条性命的高贵战舰，就化作万千碎片四散飞迸。可没过几秒钟，我们已经把这场爆炸忘在了脑后——只顾得上想我们自己的命了。

===Original===
The *Bucentaure* having struck, the enemy’s fire was directed on us, and our fate was sealed. The enthusiasm of the first hour was by this extinct in my soul; my heart quaked with terror that paralyzed my limbs and smothered every other emotion excepting curiosity. This I found so irresistible that I could not keep away from places where the danger was greatest. My small assistance was of no great use now, for the wounded were too numerous to be carried below and the guns had to be served by those who had some little strength left. Among these was Marcial who was here, there, and everywhere, shouting and working to the best of his small ability, acting as boatswain, gunner, sailor, and carpenter all at once, doing everything that happened to be needed at this awful moment. No one could have believed that, with hardly more than half a body, he could have done the work of so many men. A splinter had struck him on the head and the blood had stained his face and given him a most horrible appearance. I could see his lips move as he licked the blood from them and then he spit it out viciously over the side, as if he thought he could thus punish the enemy.

What astonished me most, and indeed shocked me somewhat, was that Marcial even in this scene of horror could still cut a good-humored joke; whether to encourage his dejected comrades or only to keep his own courage up I do not know. The foremast fell with a tremendous crash, covering the whole of the foredeck with rigging, and Marcial called out to me: “Bring the hatchets, boy; we must stow this lumber in Davy Jones’ locker,” and in two minutes the ropes were cut and the mast went overboard.

Then, seeing that the enemy’s fire grew hotter, he shouted to the purser’s mate, who had come up to serve a gun: “Daddy, order up some drink for those Greatcoats, and then they will let us alone.”

To a soldier, who was lying like a dead creature with the pain of his wounds and the misery of seasickness, he exclaimed as he whisked the slow-match under his nose: “Take a whiff of orange-flower, man, to cure your faintness. Would you like to take a turn in a boat? Nelson has invited us to take a glass of grog with him.”

===Chinese===
*布森陶尔号*既已降旗，敌人的炮火便全数转向我们，我们的命运就此注定。头一个钟头的那股热忱，此刻在我心里已经熄灭；恐惧攫住了我的心，麻了我的四肢，压灭了其余一切情感——只剩好奇心还活着。而这好奇心偏偏抗拒不得，害我怎么也离不得那些最危险的地方。我这点小小的助力如今派不上什么用场了：伤员多得抬不过来，火炮只能交给还剩下些许力气的人去伺候。马西亚尔便是其中之一，他东奔西走、无处不在，扯着嗓子喊，拼着他那点微末的气力干活，一时间身兼水手长、炮手、水手、木匠数职，这可怕时刻缺什么他就干什么。谁也不会相信，凭他只剩下大半条的残缺身子，居然能顶这么多人的活。一块碎片击中了他的头，血糊了他一脸，模样狰狞至极。我看得见他嘴唇翕动，把血舔掉，又狠命朝舷外啐出去，仿佛觉得这样就能惩罚敌人似的。

最叫我吃惊、甚至多少有点叫我发怵的，是马西亚尔在这种骇人的场面里居然还能开出好性子的玩笑来——是为了给垂头丧气的伙伴们打气，还是只为了给自己撑住胆子，我就不知道了。前桅轰隆一声巨响倒了下来，索具铺满了整个前甲板，马西亚尔冲我喊道：“小子，拿斧头来；这堆破烂得收进戴维·琼斯的海底柜子里去。”两分钟工夫，绳索砍断，桅杆翻出了舷外。

接着，眼见敌人的炮火越打越凶，他朝一位上来帮着操炮的事务长副手嚷道：“老爹，给那些大氅佬们上点喝的，他们便肯放过咱们了。”

有个士兵，伤口疼、晕船又晕得死去活来，直挺挺躺在那儿像没了气儿；马西亚尔把火绳往他鼻子底下一晃，冲他嚷道：“伙计，来闻一口橙花香，治治你那晕劲儿。想不想坐小艇出去兜一圈？纳尔逊请咱们上他那儿喝杯格罗格酒呢。”

===Original===
This took place amidships; looking up at the quarterdeck I saw that Cisneros was killed; two sailors hastily carried him down into his cabin. My master remained immovable at his post, but his left arm was bleeding severely. I ran up to help him, but before I could reach the spot an officer had gone to him to persuade him to retire to his stateroom. He had not spoken two words when a ball shot away half his head and his blood sprinkled my face. Don Alonso withdrew, as pale as the corpse which fell on the quarterdeck. When my master had gone down the commander was left standing alone, so perfectly cool that I could not help gazing at him for a few minutes, astounded by such courage. His head was uncovered, his face very white, but his eyes flashed and his attitude was full of energy, and he stood at his post, commanding the desperate strife, though the battle was lost past retrieval. Even this fearful disaster must be conducted with due order, and the captain’s duty was still to keep discipline over heroism. His voice still controlled his men in this struggle between honor and death. An officer who was serving in the first battery came up for orders, and before he could speak he was lying dead at the feet of his chief; another officer of marines who was standing by his side fell wounded on the deck, and at last Uriarte stood quite alone on the quarterdeck, which was strewn with the dead and wounded. Even then he never took his eyes off the English ships and the working of our guns—the horrible scene on the poop and in the roundhouse, where his comrades and subalterns lay dying, could not quell his noble spirit nor shake his firm determination to face the fire till he too should fall. As I recall the fortitude and stoical calmness of Don Francisco Xavier de Uriarte, I understand all that is told us of the heroes of antiquity. At that time the word “sublime” was as yet unknown to me, but I felt that there must be, in every language under heaven, some human utterance to express that greatness of soul which I here saw incarnate and which revealed itself to me as a special grace vouchsafed by God to miserable humanity.

===Chinese===
这一幕发生在船腰；抬头朝后甲板望去，我看见希斯内罗斯阵亡了；两名水手匆匆把他的遗体抬进他的舱房。我主人仍钉在岗位上纹丝不动，只是左臂血流如注。我奔上去要帮他，可还没跑到跟前，一名军官已先他一步去劝主人退回舱房歇息。那位军官话没说上两句，一发炮弹轰掉了他的半个脑袋，鲜血溅了我一脸。堂阿隆索退了下去，脸色白得同倒在后甲板上的尸首一样。主人一下去，舰长便独自站在那里，镇定得那么十全十美，叫我忍不住盯着他望了好几分钟，为这份胆气惊愕不已。他光着头，脸色惨白，双眼却灼灼放光，身姿浑身是劲；他钉在岗位上，指挥着这场背水的苦斗，尽管战局已无可挽回。就连这样一场可怕的大难，也须办得井然有法；身为舰长，职分仍是以纪律统摄英勇气性。在这场荣誉与死亡之间的搏杀里，他的声音仍旧镇得住他的部下。一名在第一炮组服役的军官上来请示，话还没出口，就已经倒在长官脚边断了气；一名立在他身旁的海军陆战队军官，也挂彩倒在甲板上；到后来，乌里亚尔特便孤零零一个人，站在横陈着死伤者的后甲板上。即便到了那时，他的目光也始终不离英国战舰和我们火炮的操作——艉楼上、艉楼舱室里，他的同僚和部属们正躺着咽气，那骇人的景象压不倒他高贵的魂魄，也撼不动他坚定的决心：直面炮火，直到他自己倒下。每忆起堂弗朗西斯科·哈维尔·德·乌里亚尔特（Don Francisco Xavier de Uriarte）的坚毅与斯多噶式的沉静，我便懂得了人们传说的古代英雄们的一切。那时节，“崇高”这个词我还不知道，可我已感觉到：普天之下无论哪种语言，必定都有一个人间的字眼，来表达我此刻亲眼见其化身的灵魂之伟大——它向我显明，那是上天特别降赐给苦难众生的一场恩典。

===Original===
By this time most of our guns were silenced, more than half of our men being incapable of serving them. I might not, however, have been aware of the fact, but that being impelled by curiosity I went out of the cabin once more and heard a voice saying in a tone of thunder:

“Gabrielillo, come here.”

It was Marcial who was calling me; I ran to his side and found him trying to work one of the guns which had been left silent for lack of men. A ball had shot away the half of his wooden leg, which made him exclaim: “Well! so long as I can manage to keep the one of flesh and bone …!”

Two sailors lay dead by the gun; a third, though horribly wounded, still tried to go on working it.

“Let be, mate!” said Marcial. “You cannot even light the match,” and taking the linstock from his hand, he put it into mine, saying: “Take it, Gabrielillo.—If you are afraid you had better jump overboard.”

===Chinese===
到这时，我们的大炮多半已经哑了——一半以上的炮员都已无力操作。若不是好奇心又驱使我走出舱去，再听见一个雷霆般的声音喊话，我原本兴许还不知道这个情形：

“加布里埃尔西托（Gabrielillo），过来。”

叫我的正是马西亚尔；我跑到他身边，见他正设法伺候一门因缺人而哑了的炮。一发炮弹打飞了他那条木腿的一半，害他嚷道：“好啊！只要我这条血肉长的还保得住……！”

炮旁躺着两具水手的尸体；第三个虽然伤得怕人，还想接着干。

“撂下吧，伙计！”马西亚尔说，“你连火绳都点不着了。”说着他从那人手里夺过火绳杆，塞进我手里，道：“拿着，加布里埃尔西托。——你要是害怕，顶好跳海去。”

===Original===
He loaded the cannon as quickly as he was able, helped by a ship’s boy who happened to come up; we ran it forward: “fire!” was the word, I applied the match and the gun went off.

We repeated this operation a second and a third time, and the roar of the cannon fired by my own hand produced an extraordinary effect on my nerves. The feeling that I was no longer a spectator but an actor in this stupendous tragedy for the moment blew all my alarms to the winds; I was eager and excited, or at any rate determined to appear so. That moment revealed to me the truth that heroism is often simply the pride of honor. Marcial’s eye—the eyes of the world were upon me; I must bear myself worthy of their gaze.

“Oh!” I exclaimed to myself with an impulse of pride: “If only my young mistress could see me now! … Bravely firing cannon like a man!” Two dozen of English were the least I might have sent to the other world.

These grand visions, however, did not last long for Marcial, enfeebled by age, was beginning to sink with exhaustion; he breathed hard as he wiped away the blood which flowed profusely from his head, and at last his arms dropped by his side, and closing his eyes, he exclaimed: “I can do no more; the powder is rising to my head. Gabrielillo, fetch me some water.”

===Chinese===
他凭着还使得动的力气飞快装弹，一个恰好赶来的见习水手搭了把手；我们把炮推上前：“开火！”口令一下，我把火绳凑上去，炮响了。

同样的活计我们又干了第二回、第三回，这由我亲手点放的炮声，对我的神经产生了奇妙的作用。一想到自己已不再是这场惊天悲剧的看客，而是戏中人，刹那间一切惊惧都被吹到了九霄云外；我亢奋激动，至少是打定主意要显得这样。那一刻向我揭示了一个真理：英雄气概，往往不过是荣誉的自尊。马西亚尔那只独眼正望着——全世界的眼睛都在望着我，我必须立得住，配得上这些目光。

“哦！”我怀着一股自豪的冲动，在心里喊道，“我的小姐这会儿要是能看见我就好了！……像个男子汉那样英勇地放炮！”打发两打英国人上西天，还不过是我的最低战果呢。

然而这般豪情的幻景没能持久：马西亚尔年迈体衰，气力渐渐不支；他大口喘着，抹着头上汩汩涌出的血，末了两条胳膊垂落在身侧，阖上眼嚷道：“我不成了；火药冲上我脑袋了。加布里埃尔西托，给我弄点水来。”

===Original===
I ran to obey him, and when I had brought the water he drank it eagerly. This seemed to give him fresh energy; we were just about to load once more when a tremendous shock petrified us as we stood. The mainmast, cut through by repeated shots, fell amidships and across the mizzen; the ship was completely covered with the wreck, and the confusion was appalling.

I happily was so far under shelter that I got no harm but a slight blow on the head which, though it stunned me for a moment, did not prevent my thrusting aside the fragments of rope and timber which had fallen above me. The sailors and marines were struggling to clear away the vast mass of lumber, but from this moment only the lower-deck guns could be used at all. I got clear as best I could and went to look for Marcial but I did not find him, and casting my eyes up at the quarterdeck, I saw that the captain was no longer at his post. He had fallen senseless, badly wounded in the head by a splinter, and two sailors were just about to carry him down to the stateroom. I was running forward to assist when a piece of shell hit me on the shoulder, terrifying me excessively, for I made sure my wound was mortal and that I was at my last gasp. My alarm did not hinder me from going into the cabin; I tottered from loss of blood and for a few minutes lay in a dead faint. I was roused from my short swoon by hearing the rattle of the cannon below and then a voice shouting vehemently:
