所有这些沧桑变故我们都尝遍了，此外还加上人为意志造成的那些。我们在惨败之中尝到了覆舟之祸的滋味；逃脱了一次之后，又被迫再打一仗，这回打赢了；随后，当我们自以为已经脱险、欢欢喜喜望见加的斯的时候，又一次落进了风暴的手心——它奸诈地哄骗了我们，就为把我们一口毁掉。这样接二连三的厄运未免显得离奇——仿佛一位恶毒的神明存心作祟，要把想得出的一切祸害都加到我们这些不幸的凡人头上——可这不过是海上的常理，再加上战争的命运罢了。让这两种可怕的力量凑到一处，除非是傻子，谁也不会为随之而来的灾难感到意外。

===Original===
Another circumstance contributed to my master’s distress of mind, and to mine too, that evening. Since the rescue of the *Santa Ana* Malespina had disappeared. At last, after seeking him everywhere, I discovered him lying in a heap on a sofa in the cabin. I went up to him and saw that he was very pale; I spoke to him but he could not answer. He tried to move but fell back gasping.

“Are you wounded?” I asked. “I will fetch someone to attend to you.”

“It is nothing,” he said. “Can you get me some water?”

I went at once for my master.

“What is the matter—this wound in your hand?” said he, examining the young officer.

“It is more than that,” replied Don Rafael sadly, and he put his hand to his right side close by his sword-belt. And, then, as if the effort of pointing out his wound and speaking those few words had been too much for his weakened frame, he closed his eyes and neither spoke nor moved for some minutes.

===Chinese===
那天傍晚，还有一桩事加重了我主人心里的悲痛，也加重了我的。自*圣安娜号*被夺回之后，马莱斯皮纳就不见了踪影。我到处找他，最后发现他瘫作一团，躺在舱里的一张沙发上。我走到他跟前，见他脸色惨白；我跟他说话，他却答不上来。他想动一动，却一头栽回去，喘个不停。

“你受伤了吗？”我问，“我去叫人来照应你。”

“没什么，”他说，“你能给我弄点水来吗？”

我立刻去找主人。

“怎么回事——你手上这伤是怎么回事？”他一边察看这位年轻军官，一边说。

“不止这个呢，”堂拉斐尔（Don Rafael）凄然答道，一面把手按到右边腰间、佩剑腰带旁边。接着，仿佛指出伤处、说这几句话的力气已叫他虚弱的身体支撑不住，他闭上了眼睛，好几分钟不言不动。

===Original===
“This is serious,” said my master anxiously.

“It is more than serious,” said a surgeon who had come to examine him. Malespina, deeply depressed by finding himself in so evil a plight, and believing himself past all hope, had not even reported himself as wounded, but had crept away to this corner where he had given himself up to his reflections and memories. He believed that he was killed and he would not have the wound touched. The surgeon assured him that though it was dangerous it need not prove mortal, though he owned that if he did not get into port that night so that he might be properly treated on shore, his life, like that of the rest of the wounded, was in the greatest danger. The *Santa Ana* had lost ninety-seven men killed on the 21st, and a hundred and forty wounded; all the resources of the surgery were exhausted and many indispensable articles were altogether wanting. Malespina’s catastrophe was not the only one during the rescue, and it had been the will of Heaven that another man very near and dear to me should share his fate. Marcial had been wounded; though at first his indomitable spirit had kept him up and he hardly felt the pain and depression, before long he submitted to be carried down into the cockpit, confessing that he was very badly hit. My master sent a surgeon to attend to him, but all he would say was that the wound would have been trifling in a man of five-and-twenty—but Marcial was past sixty.

===Chinese===
“这可不轻啊，”主人焦急地说。

“岂止是不轻，”来给他验伤的一位军医说。马莱斯皮纳发觉自己落到这般凶险的境地，心灰意冷，自忖已无生望，竟连受伤都没有呈报，只悄悄爬到这个角落里，由自己沉入回想与追忆之中。他认定自己是死定了，怎么也不肯让人碰那伤口。军医向他保证，伤虽危险，却未必致命；不过军医也承认，倘若他当夜进不了港、上不了岸好好医治，那么他的性命，同其余伤员的一样，处在极大的危险之中。*圣安娜号*二十一日那天阵亡九十七人，伤一百四十人；外科的一切用度都已耗尽，许多必不可少的物件干脆一无所有。夺船之役中遭难的还不止马莱斯皮纳一个，上天的旨意又注定另一个与我至亲至爱的人落得同样的命运。马西亚尔（Marcial）也负了伤；起初他那不服输的精气神还撑得住他，几乎不觉得疼痛和委顿，可没过多久，他也只好由人抬下下层医疗舱去，承认自己伤得很重。主人派了军医去照看他，可军医只说，这伤要落在二十五岁的人身上本算不得什么——无奈马西亚尔已年过六十。

===Original===
Meanwhile the *Rayo* passed to leeward and we hailed her. Álava begged her to enquire of the *Themis* whether the captain thought he could get us into Cádiz, and when he roundly said, “No,” the Admiral asked whether the *Rayo*, which was almost unharmed, expected to get in safely. Her captain thought she might and it was agreed that Gardoqui, who was severely wounded, and several others, should be sent on board her, among them Don Rafael Malespina. Don Alonso obtained that Marcial should also be transferred to her in consideration for his age which greatly aggravated his case, and he sent me, too, in charge of them as page or sick-nurse, desiring me never to lose sight of them for an instant till I saw them safe in the hands of their family, at Cádiz, or even at Vejer. I prepared to obey him, though I tried to persuade my master that he too ought to come on board the *Rayo* for greater safety, but he would not even listen to such a suggestion.

“Fate,” he said, “has brought me on board this ship, and in it I will stay till it shall please God to save us or no. Álava is very bad, most of the officers are more or less hurt, and I may be able to be of some service here. I am not one of those who run away from danger; on the contrary, since the defeat of the 21st I have sought it; I long for the moment when my presence may prove to be of some use. If you reach home before me, as I hope you will, tell Paca that a good sailor is the slave of his country, that I am very glad that I came—that I do not regret it—on the contrary. Tell her that she is to be glad, too, when she sees me, and that my comrades would certainly have thought badly of me if I had not come. How could I have done otherwise? You—do you not think that I did well to come?”

“Of course, certainly,” I replied, anxious to soothe his agitation, “who doubts it?” For his excitement was so great that the absurdity of asking the opinion of a pageboy had not even occurred to him.

===Chinese===
这时*拉约号*从下风处驶过，我们向它喊话。阿拉瓦请它去问*忒弥斯号*，舰长可觉得能把我们弄进加的斯；那人干脆地答了声“不行”，上将便问几乎毫发无损的*拉约号*自己有没有把握安全进港。它的舰长认为不妨一试，于是商定把伤势沉重的加尔多基（Gardoqui）和另外几个人送到它船上去，堂拉斐尔·马莱斯皮纳也在其列。堂阿隆索（Don Alonso）又为马西亚尔求得转去那艘船的恩典——念在他那把年纪，而年纪已大大加重了他的伤情；他还把我也派了去，以小厮或看护的名分照管他们，叮嘱我在把他们平平安安交到家里人手里之前——在加的斯也好，甚至在韦赫尔（Vejer）也好——一刻也不许让他们离开我的视线。我准备服从，但还是劝主人，为稳妥起见，他也该上*拉约号*去，可他连听都不肯听这种话。

“命运把我带上了这条船，”他说，“我就要待在它上面，直到上帝高兴救我们，或者不救。阿拉瓦伤得很重，军官们也多多少少都挂了彩，我留在这儿也许还能派上点用场。我不是那种见了危险就跑的人；恰恰相反，自二十一日吃了败仗起，我一直在寻它；我巴不得有这样一个时刻，我的在场能证明多少有点用处。你要是比我先到家——我盼着你如此——就告诉帕卡：一个好水手是他祖国的奴隶；告诉她，我来了非常高兴——我一点也不后悔——恰恰相反。告诉她，她见了我也要高兴；告诉她，当初我要是不来，同伴们准会把我看扁。我不来又能怎样？你——你不觉得我来对了么？”

“当然，当然，”我急着要抚平他的激动，答道，“这还有疑问吗？”他兴奋得厉害，连向一个小听差征求意见有多可笑，都压根没有想到。

===Original===
“I see you are a reasonable fellow,” he went on, much comforted by my admission. “I see you have a noble and patriotic soul. But Paca never sees anything excepting through her own selfishness, as she has a very odd temper and has taken it into her head that fleets and guns are useless inventions, she cannot understand why I. … In short, I know that she will be furious when she sees me and then—as we have not won the battle, she will say one thing and another—oh! she will drive me mad! However, I will not mind her. You—what do you say? Was I not right to come?”

“Yes, indeed, I think so,” I said once more: “You were very right to come. It shows that you are a brave officer.”

“Well then go—go to Paca, go and tell her so, and you will see what she will say,” he went on more excited than ever. “And tell her that I am safe and sound, and my presence here is indispensable. In point of fact, I was the principal leader in the rescue of the *Santa Ana*. If I had not trained those guns—who knows, who knows? You—what do you think? We may do more yet; if the wind favors us tomorrow morning we may rescue some more ships. Yes sir, for I have a plan in my head. … We shall see, we shall see. And so goodbye, my boy. Be careful of what you say to Paca.”

“I will not forget,” said I. “She shall know that if it had not been for you we should not have recaptured the *Santa Ana*, and that if you are lucky you may still bring a couple of dozen ships into Cádiz.”

===Chinese===
“我看你是个明白事理的人，”他得到我的认可，大为宽慰，接着说，“我看你有一颗高贵而爱国的心。可是帕卡看什么都只从她自己的私心出发，她脾气又古怪，脑子里认准了舰队和大炮都是些没用的劳什子，她就不能明白我为什么……总之，我知道她一见我准要大发雷霆，然后呢——仗没打赢，她这个那个地有得说——噢！她非把我逼疯不可！不过，我不理她就是了。你——你说呢？我来对了，不是吗？”

“是的，真是这样，我看是的，”我又说了一遍，“您来对极了。这说明您是位勇敢的军官。”

“那好，去吧——去见帕卡，去这么告诉她，瞧她会说什么，”他越说越兴奋，“再告诉她，我安然无恙，我在这里不可或缺。说实在的，夺回*圣安娜号*，我正是为首的领头人。要不是我瞄那几门炮——谁知道呢，谁知道？你——你说呢？我们兴许还能再干一场；明儿早上风向要是帮衬，我们没准还能再夺回几条船。是的，先生，因为我脑子里有个算计。……走着瞧，走着瞧。那么再见了，我的孩子。跟帕卡说话可要留神。”

“我忘不了，”我说，“她会知道，要不是您，我们就夺不回*圣安娜号*；她还会知道，只要您走运，兴许还能再带两打船回加的斯呢。”

===Original===
“A couple of dozen!—no man; that is a large number. Two ships, I say—or perhaps three. In short, I am sure I was right to join the fleet. She will be furious and will drive me mad when I get home again; but I was right, I say—I am sure I was right.” With these words he left me and I saw him last sitting in a corner of the cabin. He was praying, but he told his beads with as little display as possible, for he did not choose to be detected at his devotions. My master’s last speech had convinced me that he had lost his wits and, seeing him pray, I understood how his enfeebled spirit had struggled in vain to triumph over the exhaustion of age, and now, beaten in strife, turned to God for support and consolation. Doña Francisca was right; for many years my master had been past all service but prayer.

We left the ship according to orders. Don Rafael and Marcial with the rest of the wounded officers were carefully let down into the boats by the strong-armed sailors. The violence of the sea made this a long and difficult business, but at last it was done and two boat loads were pulled off to the *Rayo*. The passage, though short was really frightful; but at last, though there were moments when it seemed to me that we must be swallowed up by the waves, we got alongside of the *Rayo* and with great difficulty clambered on board.

===Chinese===
“两打！——不，小子；这数目太大了。我说两条船——兴许三条。总之，我敢肯定，参加舰队这一步我是走对了。等我再进家门，她准会大发雷霆，把我逼疯；可我还是来对了，我说——我敢肯定我来对了。”说完这些话，他离开了我；我最后一眼看见他，是坐在舱房的一个角落里。他在祈祷，不过他捻动念珠尽量不叫人瞧见，因为他不愿让人发现自己做祷告。主人最后这番话让我确信他已经神志失常；而眼见他祈祷，我又明白了：他那衰弱的灵魂曾怎样徒劳地挣扎，想压过岁月的衰竭，如今斗败了，只得转向上帝寻求支撑和慰藉。堂娜·弗朗西斯卡说得不错；多年以来，我的主人除了祈祷，早已当不了任何差了。

我们遵令离船。堂拉斐尔和马西亚尔连同其余受伤的军官，由臂力强劲的水手们小心翼翼地吊放到小艇里。海浪凶暴，这桩事办得又久又艰难，但终于办妥了，两条小艇满载着人，划向*拉约号*。这段航程虽短，实在吓人；尽管有几刻工夫我以为我们准要被巨浪吞没，我们终于还是靠上了*拉约号*的船舷，万分吃力地攀上了甲板。

## XV

===Original===
“Out of the frying-pan into the fire,” said Marcial, when they laid him down on deck. “However, when the captain commands the men must obey. *Rayo* is an unlucky name for this cursed ship. They say she will be in Cádiz by midnight, and I say she won’t. We shall see what we shall see.”

“What do you say, Marcial? we shall not get in?” I asked in much alarm.

“You, master Gabrielito, you know nothing about such matters,” said he.

“But when Don Alonso and the officers of the *Santa Ana* say that the *Rayo* will get in tonight.﻿ ﻿… She must get in when they say she will.”

===Chinese===
“刚出煎锅，又进火坑。”人们把他放到甲板上时，马西亚尔说道。“不过，舰长下了令，当兵的就得服从。这条该死的船，顶着*拉约*（Rayo）这么个名字，本就不吉利。他们说半夜她准能进加的斯，我说进不了。咱们走着瞧。”

“你说什么，马西亚尔？我们进不了港？”我大惊失色地问。

“您哪，加布里埃尔西托小少爷，这种事您一窍不通，”他说。

“可是堂阿隆索和*圣安娜号*的军官们都说，*拉约号*今夜进得了港。……他们说她进得去，她就一定进得去。”

===Original===
“Do not you know, you little landlubber, that the gentlemen of the quarterdeck are far more often mistaken than we are in the fo’castle? If not, what was the admiral of the fleet about?﻿—*Mr. Corneta*﻿—devil take him! You see he had not brains enough to work a fleet. Do you suppose that if *Mr. Corneta* had asked my advice we should have lost the battle?”

“And you think we shall not get into Cádiz?”

“I say this old ship is as heavy as lead itself and not to be trusted either. She rides the sea badly and will not answer her helm. Why, she is as lopsided and crippled as I am! If you try to put her to port off she goes to starboard.”

In point of fact the *Rayo* was considered by all as bad a ship as ever sailed. But in spite of that, in spite of her advanced age﻿—for she had been afloat nearly fifty-six years﻿—as she was still sound she did not seem to be in any danger though the gale increased in fury every minute, for we were almost close to port. At any rate, did it not stand to reason that the *Santa Ana* was in greater jeopardy, dismasted and rudderless, in tow of a frigate?

===Chinese===
“你这小旱鸭子，难道不知道后甲板上的老爷们，比我们艏楼里的汉子出错的时候多得多？若不然，舰队司令大人成天干吗吃的？——*科尔内塔先生*——叫魔鬼抓了他去！你瞧，他没那份脑子，驾驭不了一支舰队。你以为*科尔内塔先生*当初若是请教过我，我们还会吃这场败仗？”

“那么你认为我们进不了加的斯？”

“我说呀，这条老船沉得跟铅锭一个样，靠也靠不住。她在海上骑浪骑得难看，舵也不听使唤。嘿，她又歪又瘸，跟我一个德行！你想让她转左舷，她偏朝右舷跑。”

说实在的，*拉约号*在众人眼里，是有史以来下海的船里最糟糕的一条。虽说如此，虽说她年事已高——她下水已近五十六个年头——船身却还坚固，因此尽管狂风一分钟比一分钟暴烈，她似乎并没有什么危险，何况我们离港口已近在咫尺。无论如何，*圣安娜号*失了桅、丢了舵，靠一艘巡防舰拖曳着，难道不是处境更凶险吗？

===Original===
Marcial was carried to the cockpit and Malespina to the captain’s cabin. When we had settled him there, with the rest of the wounded officers, I suddenly heard a voice that was familiar to me though for the moment I could not identify it with anyone I knew. However, on going up to the group whence the stentorian accents proceeded, drowning every other voice, what was my surprise at recognizing Don José María Malespina! I ran to tell him that his son was on board, and the worthy parent at once broke off the string of rodomontade that he was pouring forth and flew to the wounded man. His delight was great at finding him alive; he had come out of Cádiz because he could no longer endure the suspense and he must know what had become of his boy at any cost.

“Why your wound is a mere trifle,” he said, embracing his son. “A mere scratch! But you are not used to wounds; you are quite a mollycoddle, Rafael. Oh, if only you had been old enough to go with me to fight in Rousillon! You would have learned there what wounds are﻿—something like wounds! Do you know a ball hit me in the fleshy part of the arm, ran up to my shoulder and then right round the shoulder blade and out by the belt. A most extraordinary case, that was. But in three days I was all right again and commanding the artillery at Bellegarde.” He went on to give the following account of his presence on board the *Rayo*.

“We knew the issue of the battle at Cádiz, by the evening of the 21st. I tell you, gentlemen﻿—no one would listen to me when I talked of reforming our artillery and you see the consequences. Well, as soon as I knew the worst and had learned that Gravina had come in with a few ships I went to see if the *San Juan Nepomuceno*, on board which you were, was one of them; but they told me she had been captured. You cannot imagine my anxiety; I could hardly doubt that you were dead, particularly when I heard how many had been killed on board your ship. However, I am one of those men who must follow a matter up to the end, and knowing that some of the ships in port were preparing to put out to sea in hope of picking up derelicts and rescuing captured vessels, I determined to set out without a moment’s delay and sail in one of them. I explained my wishes to Solano and then to the Admiral in command, my old friend Escaño, and after some hesitation they allowed me on board. I embarked this morning, and enquired of everyone in the *Rayo* for some news of you and of the *San Juan*, but I could get no comfort; nay, quite the contrary, for I heard that Churruca was killed and that his ship, after a glorious defence, had struck to the enemy. You may fancy my anxiety. How far was I from supposing this morning, when we rescued the *Santa Ana*, that you were on board! If I had but known it for certain I would have redoubled my efforts in the orders I issued﻿—by the kind permission of these gentlemen; Álava’s ship should have been free in two minutes.”

===Chinese===
马西亚尔被抬进了下层医疗舱，马莱斯皮纳则被安置进舰长舱。我们把他同其余伤官一起在那儿安顿妥当，忽然听见一个熟悉的嗓音，一时却又想不起是谁。待我走到那一小群人跟前——那洪亮的调门正是从那里发出，压倒了其余一切声音——我该多么惊讶，认出的竟是堂何塞·马里亚·马莱斯皮纳！我跑去告诉他，他儿子就在这条船上；这位可敬的父亲当即收住了正往外倒的那一串牛皮，扑向伤员。见儿子还活着，他喜出望外；他是专程从加的斯出来的，因为再受不了那份悬心的煎熬，无论如何也要打听到儿子的下落。

“嗨，你这伤不过小事一桩，”他搂住儿子说。“不过擦破点皮！只是你不惯吃枪子儿，你太娇气了，拉斐尔。唉，你要是当年够年纪，能跟我去鲁西永打仗就好了！你在那儿才学得着什么叫伤——那才叫够格的伤！你可知道，一颗炮弹打中我胳膊上肉厚的地方，一路窜上我的肩膀，又绕着肩胛骨转了一圈，从腰带边上钻出去。天底下少有的奇案，就是那一桩。可三天工夫我就好利索了，又在贝尔加德（Bellegarde）指挥起炮兵来。”接着，他讲起自己何以会到*拉约号*上来，原委如下。

“战事的结局，21日傍晚我们在加的斯就知道了。我告诉诸位——当年我谈改革我们的炮兵，没一个人肯听，如今瞧瞧这后果。唉，我一打听到这最坏的消息，听说格拉维纳带着几条船进了港，就去查问*圣胡安·内波穆塞诺号*——你就在那条船上——在不在里头；可人家告诉我，她被俘了。你想不出我那份焦灼；我几乎不能不信你已经死了，尤其在听说你们船上死了那么多人之后。不过，我这号人，事情不追到底不撒手；听说港里有几条船正备帆待发，想去捞漂流的空船、夺回被俘的船，我便打定主意，一刻也不耽搁，搭其中一条出海。我把来意对索拉诺（Solano）讲了，又去找舰队司令、我的老朋友埃斯卡尼奥，他们迟疑了一阵，总算放我上了船。我是今天早晨上的船，在*拉约号*上逢人就打听你和*圣胡安号*的消息，可一点安慰也讨不着；非但如此，恰恰相反——我听说丘鲁卡阵亡了，他的船经过一场光荣的抵抗，向敌人降了旗。你想想我那份焦灼吧。今天早晨我们救援*圣安娜号*的时候，我哪里想得到你就在船上！我若是早确知如此，下起令来准会加倍卖力——自然，还得蒙这几位先生开恩；阿拉瓦那条船，两分钟就该脱了身。”

===Original===
The officers who were standing round us looked at each other with a shrug as they heard Don José’s last audacious falsehood. I could gather from their smiles and winks that he had afforded them much diversion all day with his vainglorious fictions, for the worthy gentleman could put no bridle on his indefatigable tongue, even under the most critical and painful circumstances.

The surgeon now said that his patients ought to be left to rest and that there must be no conversation in their presence, particularly no reference to the recent disaster. Don José María, however, contradicted him flatly, saying that it was good to keep their spirits up by talking to them.

“In the war in Rousillon,” he added, “those who were badly wounded﻿—and I was several times﻿—sent for the soldiers to dance and play the guitar in the infirmary; and I am very certain that this treatment did more to cure us than all your plasters and dosing.”

“Yes, and in the wars with the French Republic,” said an Andalusian officer who wanted to trump Don José’s trick, “it was a regular thing that a corps de ballet should be attached to the ambulance corps, and an opera company as well. It left the surgeons and apothecaries nothing to do, for a few songs, and a short course of pirouettes and capers set them to rights again, as good as new.”

===Chinese===
听到堂何塞这最后一个厚脸皮的谎，围站在我们四周的军官们面面相觑，耸了耸肩。从他们的微笑和挤眉弄眼里我揣度得出，这一天里，他的虚荣瞎话已给他们添了不少消遣，因为这位可敬的老先生那张不知疲倦的嘴，就是在最危急、最沉痛的境地里，也套不上笼头。

这时外科医生发话了：病人们该静养了，在他们跟前不许交谈，尤其不许提起刚过去的那场大祸。堂何塞·马里亚却断然驳他，说同他们说话、给他们提提精神才好。

“鲁西永那场战争里，”他又补一句，“重伤号——我就重伤过好几回——打发人叫士兵到病房里来跳舞、弹吉他；我深信不疑，这个疗法比你们所有的膏药和汤药加起来还管用。”

“可不是，跟法兰西共和国打仗那阵子，”一位安达卢西亚军官说，存心要压过堂何塞这一手，“照例是给伤兵救护队配一个芭蕾舞团，外带一个歌剧班子。外科医生和药剂师全落得无事可做，因为几支歌、一小套旋转蹦跳下来，伤员们就完好如初了。”

===Original===
“Come, come!” cried Malespina, “this is too much. You do not mean to say that music and dancing can heal a wound?”

“You said so.”

“Yes, but that was only once and it is not likely to occur again. Perhaps you think it not unlikely that we may have such another war as that in Rousillon? The most bloody, the best conducted, the most splendidly planned war since the days of Epaminondas! Certainly not. Everything about it was exceptional; and you may believe me when I say it, for I was in the thick of it, from the Introit to the last blessing. It is to my experience there that I owe my knowledge of artillery﻿—did you never hear me spoken of? I am sure you must recognize my name. Well, you must know that I have in my head a magnificent scheme, and if one of these days it is only realized we shall hear of no more disasters like that of the 21st. Yes, gentlemen,” he said, looking round at the three or four officers who were listening, with consummate gravity and conceit: “Something must be done for the country. Something must be devised﻿—something stupendous, to recoup us at once for our losses and secure victory to our fleets forever and ever, Amen.”

“Let us hear, Don José,” said one of the audience. “Explain your scheme to us.”

“Well, I am devoting my mind to the construction of 300-pounders.”

“300-pounders!” cried the officers with shouts of laughter and derision. “Why, the largest we carry is a 36-pounder.”

“Mere toys! Just imagine the ruin that would be dealt by a 300-pound gun fired into the enemy’s fleet,” said Malespina. “But what the devil is that?” he added putting out his hand to keep himself from falling, for the *Rayo* rolled so heavily that it was very difficult for anyone to keep his feet.

“The gale is stiffening and I doubt our getting into Cádiz tonight,” said one of the officers moving away. The worthy man had now but two listeners, but he proceeded with his mendacious harangue all the same.

===Chinese===
“得了，得了！”马莱斯皮纳嚷道，“这可过了头。你总不至于说，音乐和跳舞能治好枪伤吧？”

“这话是你说的。”

“不错，可那只有一回，断不会再有第二回。莫非你以为，我们还能再打一场鲁西永那样的战争？自埃帕米农达斯（Epaminondas）以来最血腥、指挥最得法、筹划最出色的战争！断乎不能。那场战争事事都超凡绝伦；你们信我的话没错，因为我在里头从头到尾都站在最热闹处——从进堂咏一直到最后一声降福。我的炮兵学问，全亏了那番阅历——你没听人说起过我？我敢说，我的名字你们必定听说过。好吧，你们须知，我脑子里如今装着一个宏图，有朝一日只要它实现，21日那样的大祸我们就再也不会听说了。是的，先生们，”他说，环视着听他说话的那三四位军官，带着十二分的郑重与自负：“总得为国家做点事。总得想出个章程——一件惊天动地的大事，立刻补回我们的损失，让我们的舰队千秋万代永操胜券，阿门。”

“讲来听听，堂何塞，”听众中一位说。“把你的宏图给我们说说。”

“是这样，我如今正潜心琢磨铸造300磅炮。”

“300磅炮！”军官们哄笑着、讥诮着喊道，“嗬，咱们船上顶大的才36磅炮呢。”

“那不过是些玩物！你且想想，一门300磅炮打进敌方舰队里，是何等一场天崩地裂，”马莱斯皮纳说。“可这见鬼的是怎么回事？”他补了一句，伸手撑住自己别摔倒，原来*拉约号*横摇得那么厉害，谁也难站住脚。

“风越刮越紧，我看今夜咱们进不了加的斯了，”一位军官说着走开了。这位可敬的老先生眼下只剩两位听众，可他照旧把他那套信口开河的高谈阔论讲下去。

===Original===
“The first thing must be to build a ship from ninety-five to a hundred yards in length.”

“The Devil you will! That would be a snug little craft with a vengeance!” said one of the officers. “A hundred yards! Why the *Trinidad*﻿—God rest her﻿—was but seventy and everybody thought her too long. She did not sail well you know and was very difficult to handle.”

“It does not take much to astonish you I see,” Malespina went on. “What is a hundred yards? Why, much larger ships than that might be built. And you must know that I would build her of iron.”

“Of iron!” and his listeners went into fits of laughter.

“Yes sir, of iron. Perhaps you are not familiar with the science of hydrostatics? There can be no difficulty in building an iron ship of 7,000 tons.”

“And the *Trinidad* was of 4,000! and that was too big. But do you not see that in order to move such a monster you would want such gigantic tackle that no human power could work it?”

“Not a bit of it!﻿—Besides, my good sir, who told you that I was so stupid as to think that I could trust to the wind alone to propel my ship? If you knew﻿—I have an idea.﻿—But I do not care to explain my scheme to you for you would not understand me.”

===Chinese===
“头一件事，得造一条船，船身长九十五到一百码。”

“亏你说得出口！那可真是一艘了不得的「小巧」船！”一位军官说。“一百码！嗬，*圣特立尼达号*——愿上帝让她安息——才七十码，人人还嫌她太长。你是知道的，她航得不佳，操纵起来顶费劲。”

“我看，要叫你们吃惊倒不必费多大的事，”马莱斯皮纳接着说。“一百码算得了什么？嗬，比这大得多的船也造得出来。你们还须知道，我要用铁来造她。”

“用铁造！”他的听众们笑得直不起腰。

“不错，先生，用铁造。诸位兴许对流体静力学这门学问不熟？造一条7,000吨的铁船，毫无难处。”

“*圣特立尼达号*才4,000吨！那就已经嫌大了。可你难道不明白，要让这么大一头巨怪动起来，需要的索具之大，人力根本玩不转？”

“根本没那回事！——再说了，我的好先生，谁告诉你我蠢到那个地步，指望光靠风来推我的船？你要是知道就好了——我有一个主意。——可我不乐意把我的方案讲给你们听，讲了你们也不懂。”

===Original===
At this point of his discourse Don José was so severely shaken that he fell on all fours. But not even this could stop his tongue. Another of his audience walked away, leaving only one who had to listen and to keep up the conversation.

“What a pitching and tossing,” said the old man. “I should not wonder if we were driven on shore.﻿—Well, as I was saying﻿—I should move my monster by an invention of my own﻿—can you guess what?﻿—By steam. To this end I should construct a peculiar kind of machine in which the steam, expanding and contracting alternately inside two cylinders, would put certain wheels in motion; then.﻿ ﻿…”

The officer would listen no longer, and though he had no commission on board the ship nor any fixed duty, being one of the rescued, he went off to assist in working the ship, which was hard enough to do as the tempest increased. Malespina was left alone with me for an audience, and at first I thought he would certainly cease talking, not thinking me capable of sustaining the conversation. But, for my sins, it would seem that he credited me with more merit than I could lay claim to, for he turned to me and went on:

“You understand what I mean? Seven thousand tons, and steam working two wheels, and then.﻿ ﻿…”

“Yes señor, I understand you perfectly,” I replied, to see if he would be silent, for I did not care to hear him, nor did the violent motion of the ship which threatened us with immediate peril at all incline my mind to dissertations on the aggrandizement of the Spanish navy.

“I see,” he continued, “that you know how to appreciate me and value my inventions. You see at once that such a ship as I describe would be invincible, and as available for attack as for defence. With four or five discharges it could rout thirty of the enemy’s ships.”

“Would not their cannon do it some damage?” I asked timidly, and speaking out of civility rather than from any interest I felt in the matter.

“Your observation is a very shrewd one my little gentleman, and proves that you really appreciate my great invention. But to avoid injury from the enemy’s guns I should cover my ship with thick plates of steel. I should put on it a breastplate, in fact, such as warriors wore of old. With this protection it could attack the foe, while their projectiles would have no more effect on its sides than a broadside of bread-pills flung by a child. It is a wonderful idea I can tell you, this notion of mine. Just fancy our navy with two or three ships of this kind! What would become of the English fleet then, in spite of its Nelsons and Collingwoods?”

===Chinese===
讲到这当口，堂何塞被狠狠一颠，扑跌得两手两膝着了地。可就连这一下也堵不住他的嘴。听众里又走掉一位，只剩下一个，只得听着，还得搭腔。

“瞧这又抛又颠的，”老头儿说。“弄不好咱们要被赶到岸上去。——好吧，我方才说到哪儿了——我要用我自个儿的一项发明来驱动我那头巨怪——你猜是什么？——用蒸汽。为这个，我要造一种特别的机器，蒸汽在两个汽缸里一胀一缩，便带动几个轮子转动；然后。……”

那位军官再也不肯听了；他虽在船上没有军职，也没有固定的差事——他是获救上船的人之一——却跑去帮着操船，风暴越来越猛，操船也实在够呛。马莱斯皮纳落得单剩我一个听众；起初我想他总该住口了，不承想把我看成接得住话的人。可是我大概造了孽，他高看了我远超我配领的分量，竟转过脸来，接着往下讲：

“你懂我的意思吧？七千吨，蒸汽推动两个轮子，然后。……”

“懂，先生，您的意思我完全明白，”我答道，想看他肯不肯住口，因为我不爱听他讲，况且船颠簸得那样凶，随时威胁着我们，我的心境实在无法倾向去听人大谈西班牙海军的发扬光大。

“我看得出，”他接着说，“你懂得赏识我、看重我的发明。你一眼就看出，我形容的这样一条船必定无敌，攻可用，守也可用。放它四五轮炮，就能击溃敌人三十条船。”

“他们的炮就伤不了它一点么？”我怯生生地问，一半出于客气，并非对这事真有什么兴致。

“你这一问极有眼力，我的小少爷，足见你是真赏识我这伟大的发明。不过，为免受敌人炮火的损伤，我要给我的船包上厚厚的钢板。说白了就是给它穿一副胸甲，好比古时武士披挂的那种。有这层护身，它尽管攻敌，敌人的炮弹打在它舷侧，也不过像小孩子扔来的一舷面包丸。我这个念头，我告诉你，妙不可言。你设想一下，咱们的海军有两三条这样的船！到那时英国舰队还落个什么下场，纵然它有的是纳尔逊和科林伍德？”

===Original===
“But they might make such ships themselves,” I returned eagerly and feeling the force of this argument. “The English would do the same, and then the conditions of the battle would be equal again.”

Don José was quite dumbfounded by this suggestion and for a minute did not know what to say, but his inexhaustible imagination did not desert him for long and he answered, but somewhat crossly:

“And who said, impertinent boy, that I should be such a fool as to divulge the secret so that the English might learn it? These ships would be constructed in perfect secrecy without a word being whispered even to anyone. Suppose a fresh war were to break out. We should defy the English: ‘Come on, gentlemen,’ we should say, ‘we are ready, quite ready.’ The common ships would put out to sea and begin the action when lo and behold! out come two or three of these iron monsters into the thick of the fight, vomiting steam and smoke and turning here and there without troubling themselves about the wind; they go wherever they are wanted, splintering the wooden sides of the enemy’s ships by the blows of their sharp bows, and then with a broadside or two.﻿ ﻿… It would all be over in a quarter of an hour.”

I did not care to raise any further difficulties for the conviction that our vessel was in the greatest danger quite kept my mind from dwelling on ideas so inappropriate to our critical situation. In fact, I never thought again of the monster ship of the old man’s fancy till thirty years after when we first heard of the application of steam to purposes of navigation; and again when, halfway through the century, our fine frigate the *Numancia* actually realized the extravagant dreams of the braggart of Trafalgar.

Half a century later I remembered Don José María Malespina and I said: “He seemed to us a bombastic liar; but conceptions which are extravagant in one place and time, when born in due season become marvellous realities! And since living to see this particular instance of the fact, I have ceased to think any Utopia impossible, and the greatest visionaries seem to me possible men of genius.”

===Chinese===
“可他们自己也能造这样的船呀，”我急急地回道，只觉这一驳有力。“英国人会照样办理，那一来，交战的条件又拉平了。”

这个诘难把堂何塞问得目瞪口呆，有一分钟说不出话来；可他那永不枯竭的想象力没有抛下他多久，他答上了话，只是带了三分恼：

“谁说了，没规矩的小子，说我蠢到走漏机密、好让英国人学去？这些船要秘密建造，对任何人一个字儿也不透露。假定说，新的战事又起。我们向英国人叫阵：‘来吧，先生们，’我们就说，‘我们准备好了，完全准备好了。’寻常的船出海开战，正打得热闹——瞧啊！两三条这样的铁怪冲出战团最密处，喷着蒸汽和浓烟，忽东忽西，全不把风放在心上；哪里要用到哪里去，用锋利的船头把敌人木船的舷侧撞得粉碎，然后再补上一舷两舷齐射。……不出一刻钟，一切全完。”

我不愿再提什么疑难了，因为深信我们的船正处于极大的危险之中，这个念头把我的心思全占住了，容不得那些与我们危局全不相称的想头。事实上，此后我再没有想起过老头儿幻想中的那头怪物巨船，直到三十年后，我们初次听说蒸汽应用于航海；再到本世纪中叶，我们那艘漂亮的巡防舰*努曼西亚号*（Numancia）当真实现了这位特拉法尔加吹牛家的荒唐大梦。

半个世纪后，我想起堂何塞·马里亚·马莱斯皮纳，对自己说：“当年我们看他是个夸夸其谈的牛皮客；可是在此地此时显得荒唐的构想，生逢其时便会成了不起的现实！自从亲眼见到这一例证，我再也不认为任何乌托邦是不可能实现的了，在我眼里，最了不起的空想家也可能是天才。”

===Original===
I left Don José in the cabin and ascended the companionway, to see what was going forward, and as soon as I was on deck I understood the dangerous situation of the *Rayo*. The gale not only prevented her getting into Cádiz, but was driving her towards the coast where she must inevitably be wrecked on the rocky shore. Melancholy as was the fate of the abandoned *Santa Ana* it could not be more desperate than ours. I looked with dismay into the faces of the officers and crew to see if I could read hope in any one of them, but despair was written in all. I glanced at the sky﻿—it was black and awful; I gazed at the sea﻿—it was raging with fury. God was our only hope﻿—and He had shown us no mercy since the fatal 21st!

The *Rayo* was running northwards. I could understand, from what I heard the men about me saying, that we were driving past the reef of Marajotes﻿—past Hazte Afuera﻿—Juan Bola﻿—Torregorda, and at last past the entrance to Cádiz. In vain was every effort made to put her head round to enter the bay. The old ship, like a frightened horse, refused to obey; the wind and waves carried her on, due north, with irresistible fury and science could do nothing to prevent it.

We flew past the bay, and could make out to our right, Rota, Punta Candor, Punta de Meca, Regla and Chipiona. There was not a doubt that the *Rayo* must be driven on shore, close to the mouth of the Guadalquivir. I need hardly say that the sails were close reefed and that as this proved insufficient in such a furious tempest the topmasts were lowered; at last it was even thought necessary to cut away the masts to prevent her from foundering. In great storms a ship has to humble herself, to shrink from a stately tree to a lowly plant; and as her masts will no more yield than the branches of an oak, she is under the sad necessity of seeing them amputated and losing her limbs to save her life.

The loss of the ship was now inevitable. The main and mizzenmasts were cut through and sent overboard, and our only hope was that we might be able to cast anchor near the coast. The anchors were got ready and the chains and cables strengthened. We were now running right on shore, and two cannon were fired as a signal that we wanted help; for as we could clearly distinguish fires we kept up our hope that there must be someone to come to our rescue. Some were of opinion that a Spanish or English ship had already been wrecked here and that the fires we saw had been lighted by the destitute crew. Our anxiety increased every instant, and as for myself I firmly believed that I was face to face with a cruel death. I paid no attention to what was doing on board, being much too agitated to think of anything but my end, which seemed inevitable. If the vessel ran on a rock, what man could swim through the breakers that still divided us from the coast? The most dangerous spot in a storm is just where the waves are hurled revolving against the shore, as if they were trying to scoop it out and drag away whole tracts of earth into the gulfs below. The blow of a wave as it dashes forward and its gluttonous fury as it rushes back again, is such as no human strength can stand against.

===Chinese===
我把堂何塞留在舱里，顺着舱梯爬上去，看看外头正有什么动静；一上甲板我就明白了*拉约号*处境的凶险。大风不但不容她进加的斯，还把她朝海岸推去，她必将在那礁石嶙峋的岸边撞得粉碎。被弃的*圣安娜号*的命运固然凄惨，却再凄惨不过我们眼下的处境。我沮丧地端详军官和水手们的脸，想从哪一张脸上读出一点希望来，可绝望写满了每一张脸。我望望天——黑得吓人；我看看海——怒得发狂。上帝是我们唯一的指望——而自打那要命的21日以来，他从没怜悯过我们！

*拉约号*正向北狂奔。从身边水手们的谈话里我听明白了，我们正一路刮过马拉霍特斯礁（Marajotes）——刮过哈斯特阿富埃拉（Hazte Afuera）——胡安·波拉（Juan Bola）——托雷戈尔达（Torregorda），最后刮过了加的斯的入口。想让她调转船头进湾，一切努力全是白费。这条老船像一匹受惊的马，死也不肯听命；风和浪挟着她一路正北，狂怒之势不可抵挡，任凭多高的本事也拦不住。

我们飞也似的掠过海湾，右边能辨认出罗塔（Rota）、坎多尔角（Punta Candor）、梅卡角（Punta de Meca）、雷格拉（Regla）和奇皮奥纳（Chipiona）。*拉约号*十有八九要被冲上岸去，就在瓜达尔基维尔河（Guadalquivir）河口近旁。不必说，帆早已缩到最紧，可在这样的狂风暴雨里仍不管用，中桅也放倒了；到后来，甚至觉得非得砍断桅杆，才能免她沉没。大风暴里，船只得自贬身份，从一株堂堂的大树缩成一棵低矮的小草；她的桅杆又像橡树的枝干一样宁折不弯，便只好忍痛看着它们被截去，舍掉肢体来保全性命。

船的损失已在所难免。主桅和后桅被拦腰砍断，抛进了海里；我们唯一的指望，是能在近岸处抛下锚去。锚备好了，锚链和缆索都加固了。我们此刻直朝岸上冲去，放了两炮，作为求救的信号；因为岸上的火堆我们望得真切，我们便存着指望：总会有人来救。有人说，先前已有西班牙或英国的船在这儿失事，我们望见的火，是那一贫如洗的船员们点起来的。我们的焦灼一刻深似一刻；至于我自己，我笃信自己是面对着一场残酷的死亡。船上在忙什么，我一概没心思理会，心里乱得除了那似乎注定的下场，什么也想不了。船若撞上礁石，隔着那一带仍横在我们与海岸之间的拍岸浪，谁游得过去？风暴里最凶险的地方，恰是海浪打着旋砸向海岸的那一带，仿佛要把海岸掏空，整片整片的地皮拖进底下的深渊。浪头扑上来的一击，和它退回去时那贪馋的狂怒，人力全然招架不住。

===Original===
At last, after some hours of mortal anguish, the keel of the *Rayo* came upon a sand bank and there she stuck. The hull and the remaining masts shivered as she struck; she seemed to be trying to cut her way through the obstacle; but it was too much for her; after heaving violently for a few moments, her stern went slowly down with fearful creaks and groans, and she remained steady. All was over now, nothing remained to be done but to save ourselves by getting across the tract of sea which separated us from the land. This seemed almost impossible in the boats we had on board; our best hope was that they might send us help from the shore, for it was evident that the crew of a lately wrecked vessel was encamped there, and one of the government cutters, which had been placed on the coast by the naval authorities for service in such cases, must surely be in the neighborhood. The *Rayo* fired again and again, and we watched with desperate impatience, for if some succour did not reach us soon we must all go down in the ship. The hapless crippled mass whose timbers had parted as she struck seemed likely to hasten her end by the violence of her throes, and the moment could not be far off when her ribs must fall asunder and we should be left at the mercy of the waves with nothing to cling to but the floating wreck.

Those on shore could do nothing for us, but by God’s mercy our signal guns were heard by a sloop which had put to sea at Chipiona and which now approached us, keeping, however, at a respectful distance. As soon as her broad mainsail came in view we knew that we were saved, and the captain of the *Rayo* gave orders to insure our all getting on board without confusion in such imminent peril. My first idea, when I saw the boats being got out, was to run to the two men who most interested me on board: Marcial and young Malespina, both wounded, though Marcial’s was not a serious case. I found the young officer in a very bad way and saying to the men around him: “I will not be moved﻿—leave me to die here.”

Marcial had crawled up on deck and was lying on the planks so utterly prostrate and indifferent that I was really terrified at his appearance. He looked up as I went near him and taking my hand said in piteous tones: “Gabrielillo, do not forsake me!”

===Chinese===
终于，熬过几个时辰濒死的苦楚，*拉约号*的龙骨触上了一道沙洲，卡在那里。船身和残余的桅杆撞得发颤；她像还想劈开障碍闯过去；可这担子太重了；她猛烈地挣了几下，船尾伴着可怕的吱嘎呻吟缓缓沉了下去，随即稳住不动了。至此一切完事，剩下的只有自救一途：渡过隔开我们与陆地的那片海。靠船上现有的小艇，这几乎办不到；我们最好的指望是岸上会派人来援，因为岸上分明扎营驻着一条新失事船上的船员，而海军当局为办这类差事布防在海岸上的政府缉私快船（cutter），必定就在左近。*拉约号*一炮接一炮地放，我们望眼欲穿地等着，因为救援若不快到，我们全得随船沉没。这不幸的残躯，船木已在触底时离散，看她挣扎得那样狂暴，似乎还要亲手催促自己的末日；她的船肋散架的时刻已不在远，到那时我们将被抛给海浪摆布，除了漂着的残骸，再没有可抓的东西。

岸上的人帮不上我们的忙；可托上帝的慈悲，我们的信号炮被一条从奇皮奥纳出海的单桅小帆船（sloop）听见了，她此刻朝我们驶来，只是保持着一段敬而远之的距离。她那宽大的主帆一映入眼帘，我们就知道有救了；*拉约号*的舰长下令，务必让大家在这燃眉之危里登船不乱。见小艇放下来，我头一个念头，就是奔向船上我最挂念的两个人：马西亚尔和小马莱斯皮纳，两人都带着伤，虽然马西亚尔伤得不重。我见那年轻军官情形很坏，正对身边的人说：“我不挪——把我留在这儿死好了。”

马西亚尔已爬上甲板，直挺挺躺在船板上，那副全然颓败、万念俱灰的样子，真把我吓着了。我走近时他抬起眼，拉住我的手，声调凄楚：“加布里埃尔西托，别丢下我！”

===Original===
“To land!” I cried trying to encourage him, “we are all going on shore.” But he only shook his head sadly as if he foresaw some immediate disaster.

I tried to help him up, but after the first effort he let himself drop as if he were dead. “I cannot,” he said at length. The bandages had come off his wound and in the confusion of our desperate situation no one had thought of applying fresh ones. I dressed it as well as I could, comforting him all the time with hopeful words; I even went so far as to laugh at his appearance to see if that would rouse him. But the poor old man could not smile; he let his head droop gloomily on his breast, as insensible to a jest as he was to consolation. Thinking only of him, I did not observe that the boats were putting off. Among the first to be put on board were Don José Malespina and his son; my first impulse had been to follow them in obedience to my master’s orders, but the sight of the wounded sailor was too much for me. Malespina could not need me, while Marcial was almost a dead man and still clung to my hand with his cold fingers, saying again and again: “Gabrielillo, do not leave me.”

The boats labored hard through the breakers, but notwithstanding, when once the wounded had been moved the embarkation went forward rapidly, the sailors flinging themselves in by a rope or taking a flying leap. Several jumped into the water and saved themselves by swimming. It flashed through my mind as a terrible problem, by which of these means I could escape with my life, and there was no time to lose for the *Rayo* was breaking up; the after-part was all under water and the cracking of the beams and timbers, which were in many places half rotten, warned me that the huge hulk would soon cease to exist. Everyone was rushing to the boats, and the sloop, which kept at a safe distance, very skilfully handled so as to avoid shipping water, took them all on board. The empty boats came back at once and were filled again in no time.

Seeing the helpless state in which Marcial was lying I turned, half-choked with tears, to some sailors and implored them to pick him up and carry him to a boat; but it was as much as they could do to save themselves. In my desperation I tried to lift him and drag him to the ship’s side, but my small strength was hardly enough to raise his helpless arms. I ran about the deck, seeking some charitable soul; and some seemed on the point of yielding to my entreaties, but their own pressing danger choked their kind impulses. To understand such cold-blooded cruelty you must have gone through such a scene of horror; every feeling of humanity vanishes before the stronger instinct of self-preservation which becomes a perfect possession, and sometimes reduces man to the level of a wild beast.

“Oh the wretches! they will do nothing to save you, Marcial,” I cried in bitter anguish.

“Let them be,” he said. “They are the same at sea as on shore. But you child, be off, run, or they will leave you behind.” I do not know which seemed to me the most horrible alternative﻿—to remain on board with the certainty of death, or to go and leave the miserable man alone. At length, however, natural instinct proved the stronger and I took a few steps towards the ship’s side; but I turned back to embrace the poor old man once more and then I ran as fast as I could to the spot where the last men were getting into the boat. There were but four, and when I reached the spot I saw that all four had jumped into the sea and were swimming to meet the boat which was still a few yards distant.

===Chinese===
“上岸去！”我喊道，想给他鼓劲，“我们全都要上岸了。”可他只哀哀地摇头，仿佛预感到大祸就在眼前。

我想扶他起来，可他挣了一下，便像死人似的瘫了回去。“我不成了，”他末了说。伤口上的绷带早已脱落，乱成一片的绝境里，谁也没顾上给他重新裹上。我尽自己所能替他包扎，一路拿有指望的话宽慰他；我甚至取笑他这副模样，看能不能逗起他的精神。可这可怜的老人笑不出来了；他阴郁地把头垂在胸前，玩笑听不进，安慰也听不进。我心里只有他，竟没留意小艇正在离船。头一批送上去的就有堂何塞·马莱斯皮纳和他儿子；我的第一个冲动是遵照主人的吩咐跟了去，可眼前这伤者的景象叫我割舍不下。马莱斯皮纳用不着我，马西亚尔却已是个半死的人，还用冰冷的手指攥着我的手，一遍又一遍地说：“加布里埃尔西托，别离开我。”

小艇在拍岸浪里艰难地挣命；尽管如此，伤员一转移完，登船便快了起来，水手们抓着缆绳荡上去，或者飞身一跃跳进去。有几个跳进海里，靠游泳逃了命。一个可怕的难题在我脑子里一闪：这几条路子，我能靠哪一条逃出性命？而且刻不容缓，*拉约号*正在解体；船尾已全泡在水里，船梁和船木——好些地方已半朽——咯咯作响，警告我这条庞然大物很快就要不复存在。人人都在往小艇奔；那条单桅小帆船泊在安全距离外，操驾得极巧，免得进水，把人一个个全收上了船。空下来的小艇立刻划回，一眨眼又装满了。

眼见马西亚尔躺得那样无助，我噙着满眶泪水，转向几个水手，哀求他们把他抱起来抬到小艇上去；可他们自己逃命还来不及。绝望之下，我想把他抱起来拖到船舷边，可我这点微力，连他那双无力的胳膊都抬不动。我在甲板上跑来跑去，寻一个慈悲的人；有几个眼看要被我的哀求打动了，可自身迫在眉睫的危险又噎住了他们的善念。要懂得这般冷血的狠心，你得亲身经历过那样骇人的场面；求生这个更强的本能一到，人道的情感便荡然无存——那本能把人整个占住，有时竟把人降到野兽的地步。

“噢，这些狠心鬼！他们不肯救你了，马西亚尔，”我惨痛地喊道。

“由他们去，”他说。“海上的人跟岸上的人一个样。你呢，孩子，快走，跑起来，不然他们要把你撂下了。”留在船上坐以待毙，还是脱身而去、撇下这可怜的人独自一个，这两条路哪一条更可怕，我说不上来。末了，还是天生的本能占了上风，我朝船舷挪了几步；可我又回身，再一次抱了抱这可怜的老人，然后撒腿飞奔，奔向最后几个人上艇的地方。那边只剩四个人，我跑到时，看见四个全跳进了海，朝着还在几码开外的小艇游去。

===Original===
“Take me!” I shrieked, seeing that they were leaving me behind. “I am coming too!﻿—Take me too!”

I shouted with all my strength but they either did not hear or did not heed me. Dark as it was, I could make out the boat and even knew when they were getting into it, though I could hardly say that I saw them. I was on the point of flinging myself overboard to take my chance of reaching the boat when, at that very moment, it had vanished﻿—there was nothing to be seen but the black waste of waters. Every hope of escape had vanished with it. I looked round in despair﻿—nothing was visible but the waves preying on what was left of the ship; not a star in the sky, not a spark on shore﻿—the sloop had sailed away.

===Chinese===
“带上我！”我尖声叫道，眼见他们要把我撂下。“我也要来！——也带上我！”

我使足力气喊，可他们不是没听见，就是没理会。天那样黑，我还认得出那条小艇，甚至知道他们何时爬了进去，虽说算不上真看见了他们。我正要纵身跳海，碰碰运气追上小艇，恰在那一刻，它没了踪影——眼前只剩下黑茫茫一片汪洋。一切逃生之望随它一同消失。我绝望地四下张望——除了撕咬船骸的浪，什么也看不见；天上一颗星也没有，岸上一点火星也没有——那条单桅小帆船已经开走了。

===Original===
Beneath my feet, which I stamped with rage and anguish, the hull of the *Rayo* was going to pieces, nothing remained indeed but the bows, and the deck was covered with wreck; I was actually standing on a sort of raft which threatened every moment to float away at the mercy of the waves.

I flew back to Marcial. “They have left me, they have left us!” I cried. The old man sat up with great difficulty, leaning on one hand and his dim eyes scanned the scene and the darkness around us.

“Nothing.﻿ ﻿…” he said. “Nothing to be seen; no boats, no land, no lights, no beach.﻿—They are not coming back!”

As he spoke a tremendous crash was heard beneath our feet in the depths of the hold under the bows, long since full of water; the deck gave a great lurch and we were obliged to clutch at a capstan to save ourselves from falling into the sea. We could not stand up; the last remains of the *Rayo* were on the point of being engulfed. Still, hope never forsakes us; and I, at any rate, consoled myself with the belief that things might remain as they were now till daybreak and with observing that the foremast had not yet gone overboard. I looked up at the tall mast, round which some tatters of sails and ends of ropes still flapped in the wind, and which stood like a dishevelled giant pointing heavenward and imploring mercy with the persistency of despair; and I fully determined that if the rest of the hull sank under water I would climb it for a chance of life.

===Chinese===
我暴怒哀痛地跺着脚，脚底下，*拉约号*的船身正一块块散开，实在只剩下一个船头，甲板上堆满残骸；我竟是站在一种木筏似的东西上，它时时刻刻都要散开，任凭海浪摆布着漂走。

我飞奔回马西亚尔身边。“他们把我撂下了，把我们撂下了！”我喊道。老人十分吃力地撑起半坐着，一只手拄着地，昏花的眼扫视四下，扫视我们周围的黑暗。

“什么都没有。……”他说。“什么也看不见；没有艇，没有岸，没有灯火，没有滩。——他们不回来了！”

话音未落，我们脚下、船头底下早已灌满水的船舱深处，传来一声天崩地裂的巨响；甲板猛地一歪，我们慌忙死死抱住绞盘，才没栽进海里。我们站也站不住；*拉约号*最后这点残躯眼看就要被吞没。可希望从不抛下人；至少我还宽慰自己，想着眼下这个样子兴许能撑到天亮；我又留意到前桅还没倒进海里。我抬头望那高高的桅杆，上面还挂着几片破帆和断绳头，在风里噼啪地飘；它立在那儿，像一个披头散发的巨人，直指上天，以绝望的执拗哀求着慈悲；我拿定主意：船身其余部分若是沉下水去，我就爬上桅杆，挣一条活路。

===Original===
Marcial laid himself down on the deck.

“There is no hope, Gabrielillo,” he said. “They have no idea of coming back, nor could they if they tried in such a sea. Well, since it is God’s will, we must both die where we are. For me, it matters not; I am an old man, and of no use for any earthly thing.﻿—But you, you are a mere child and you.﻿ ﻿…” But here his voice broke with emotion. “You,” he went on, “have no sins to answer for, you are but a child. But I.﻿ ﻿… Still, when a man dies like this﻿—what shall I say﻿—like a dog or a cat﻿—there is no need, I have heard, for the priest to give him absolution﻿—all that is needed is that he should make his peace himself with God. Have you not heard that said?”

I do not know what answer I made; I believe I said nothing, but only cried miserably.

“Keep your heart up, Gabrielillo,” he went on. “A man must be a man, and it is at a time like this that you get to know the stuff you are made of. You have no sins to answer for, but I have. They say that when a man is dying and there is no priest for him to confess to, he ought to tell whatever he has on his conscience to anyone who will listen to him. Well, I will confess to you Gabrielillo; I will tell you all my sins, and I expect God will hear me through you and then he will forgive me.”

===Chinese===
马西亚尔在甲板上躺了下来。

“没指望了，加布里埃尔西托，”他说。“他们压根儿没打算回来，这样的海，就是想回也回不来。唉，既然是上帝的旨意，咱们俩就死在这儿吧。我呢，无所谓；我是个老头子了，世上什么事也当不了用了。——可你呢，你还只是个孩子，你……”说到这里，他的嗓音哽住了。“你，”他接着说，“没有罪要交代，你不过是个孩子。可我。……不过，一个人这样死法——怎么说呢——像条狗、像只猫似的死了——听说就用不着神父来赦罪——只消他自己同上帝讲和就是了。这话你没听说过吗？”

我不知自己答了什么；我想我什么也没说，只是凄凄地哭。

“把心提起来，加布里埃尔西托，”他接着说。“是男人就得像个男人，恰恰在这样的时刻，你才认得出自己是什么料子做的。你没有罪要交代，我有。人们说，一个人快死的时候，身边没有神父可办告解，就该把他良心上的事，讲给任何肯听的人。好吧，我就向你告解，加布里埃尔西托；我要把我的罪全告诉你，我想上帝会借你的耳朵听到我，然后宽恕我。”

===Original===
Dumb with terror and awe at the solemnity of his address, I threw my arms round the old man who went on speaking.

“Well, I say, I have always been a Christian, a Catholic, Apostolic Roman; and that I always was and still am devoted to the Holy Virgin del Cármen, to whom I pray for help at this very minute; and I say too that though for twenty years I have never been to confession nor received the sacrament, it has not been my fault, but that of this cursed service, and because one always puts it off from one Sunday to the next. But it is a trouble to me now that I failed to do it, and I declare and swear that I pray God and the Virgin and all the Saints to punish me if it was my fault; for this year, if I have never been to confession or communion, it was all because of those cursed English that forced me to go to sea again just when I really meant to make it up with the Church. I never stole so much as a pin’s head, and I never told a lie, except for the fun of it now and then. I repent of the thrashings I gave my wife thirty years ago﻿—though I think she rightly deserved them, for her temper was more venomous than a scorpion’s sting. I never failed to obey the captain’s order in the least thing; I hate no one on earth but the Greatcoats, and I should have liked to see them made mincemeat of. However, they say we are all the children of the same God, so I forgive them, and I forgive the French who brought us into this war. I will say no more, for I believe I am going﻿—full sail. I love God and my mind is easy. Gabriel hold me tight and stick close to me; you have no sins to answer for, you will go straight away to Heaven to pipe tunes with the angels. Ah well, it is better to die so, at your age, than to stay below in this wicked world. Keep up your courage, boy, till the end. The sea is rising and the *Rayo* will soon be gone. Death by drowning is an easy one; do not be frightened﻿—stick close to me. In less than no time we shall be out of it all; I answering to God for all my shortcomings, and you as happy as a fairy, dancing through the star-paved heavens﻿—and they tell us happiness never comes to an end up there because it is eternal, or, as they say, tomorrow and tomorrow and tomorrow, world without end.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===
他那话语的庄重把我惊得哑然，又惧又敬；我张开胳膊搂住老人，他继续说着。

“好吧，我说：我始终是个基督徒，罗马公教、宗徒传下来的正统教徒；我从来到如今都虔奉卡门圣母，这会儿正求她救助的就是我；我还说，虽说我二十年没办告解、没领圣事，那不是我的错，是这该死的当差害的，再加上人总爱把这个礼拜天推到下个礼拜天。可如今我懊恼当初没办；我声明并发誓，若是我的错，求上帝、圣母和诸位圣徒罚我；因为今年我要是一直没去告解、没去领圣体，全怪那些该死的英国人，偏偏在我真心想同教会和好的当口，逼得我又下了海。我连个针尖大的东西也没偷过，也从没说过谎——偶尔说说也只是逗个乐。我悔三十年前揍我老婆那几顿——虽说我想她挨得不冤，她的性子比蝎子螫的还毒。舰长的命令，顶小的事我也没误过；这世上我谁也不恨，只恨大氅佬，我本想看他们被剁成肉酱的。不过，人家说我们都是同一位上帝的儿女，那我就饶了他们，也饶了把我们拖进这场战争的法国人。我不多说了，我想我要去了——满帆而去。我爱上帝，我心里安稳。加布里埃尔，抱紧我，贴紧我；你没有罪要交代，你会径直升上天堂，跟天使们吹哨子奏曲儿。啊，像你这个年纪这样死，比赖在这个邪恶的尘世下界强。孩子，鼓起勇气撑到底。海在涨，*拉约号*快没了。淹死是种容易的死法；别害怕——贴紧我。转眼之间，这一切就都过去了；我向上帝交代我的一切欠缺，你呢，快活得像个小仙子，在铺满星斗的天空里跳舞——人家告诉我们，那上头的幸福永无穷尽，因为那是永恒的，或者像他们说的，明天，明天，又明天，直到永永远远。……”

===Original===
He could say no more. I clung passionately to the poor mutilated body. A tremendous sea swept over the bows and I felt the water dash against my shoulder. I shut my eyes and fixed my thoughts on God. Then I lost consciousness and knew no more.

===Chinese===
他再也说不下去了。我死死抱住那可怜的残缺身躯。一个巨浪扫过船头，我觉得海水砸在自己肩上。我闭上眼睛，把心思凝在上帝身上。随后我便失去了知觉，什么也不知道了。

## XVI

===Original===
When, I know not how long after, the idea of life dawned once more on my darkened spirit, I was conscious only of being miserably cold; indeed, this was the only fact that made me aware of my own existence, for I remembered nothing whatever of all that had happened and had not the slightest idea of where I was. When my mind began to get clearer and my senses recovered their functions I found that I was lying on the beach; some men were standing round me and watching me with interest. The first thing I heard was: “Poor little fellow!—he is coming round.”

By degrees I recovered my wits and, with them, my recollection of past events. My first thought was for Marcial, and I believe that the first words I spoke were an enquiry for him. But no one could tell me anything about him; I recognized some of the crew of the *Rayo* among the men on the beach and asked them where he was; they were all agreed that he must have perished. Then I wanted to know how I had been saved, but they would tell me nothing about that either. They gave me some liquor to drink, I know not what, and carried me to a neighboring hut, where, warmed by a good fire and cared for by an old woman, I soon felt quite well, though still rather weak. Meanwhile I learned that another cutter had put out to reconnoitre the wreck of the *Rayo* and that of a French ship which had met with the same fate, and that they had picked me up still clinging to Marcial; they found that I could be saved but my companion was dead. I learned too that a number of poor wretches had been drowned in trying to reach the coast. Then I wanted to know what had become of Malespina, but no one knew anything either of him or of his father. I enquired about the *Santa Ana* which, it appeared, had reached Cádiz in safety, so I determined to set out forthwith to join my master. We were at some distance from Cádiz, on the coast to the north of the Guadalquivir, I wanted therefore to start at once to make so long a journey. I took two days’ rest to recover my strength, and then set out for Sanlúcar, in the company of a sailor who was going the same way. We crossed the river on the morning of the 27th and then continued our walk, keeping along the coast. As my companion was a jolly, friendly fellow the journey was as pleasant as I could expect in the frame of mind I was in, grieved at Marcial’s death and depressed by the scenes I had so lately witnessed. As we walked on we discussed the battle and the shipwrecks that had ensued.

===Chinese===
不知过了多久，生命的意念才重新在我昏暗的心灵里破晓，这时我只觉得自己冷得可怜；说实话，正是这份寒意叫我意识到自己还存在，因为此前的种种我一点也记不得，对身在何处也没有半点头绪。等我脑子渐渐清楚、感官恢复了功用，我才发现自己躺在海滩上；有几个人围着我站着，关切地打量我。我听到的头一句话是：“可怜的小家伙！——他缓过来了。”

我渐渐恢复了神志，往事也随之回到记忆里。我头一个想到的是马西亚尔，我相信我开口说的头一句话便是打听他。可谁也说不清他的下落；我在海滩上的人群里认出几个*拉约号*的水手，便问他们人在哪里，众人都说他准是没命了。接着我又想知道自己是怎么得救的，可这件事他们同样说不出什么。他们给我喝了点酒——不知是什么酒——把我抬到邻近一间茅屋里；炉火烤得暖暖的，又有一位老大娘照看，我不多时便觉得大体安适，只是还有些虚弱。其间我听说，另有一条单桅快艇出海查看*拉约号*和一艘遭了同样命运的法国船的残骸，把我捞起来的时候，我还紧紧抱着马西亚尔；他们看得出我还有救，而我的同伴已经死了。我还听说，不少可怜人在拼力抢登岸时淹死了。随后我想打听马莱斯皮纳的下落，可是不论他还是他父亲，都没有人知道半点音讯。我问起*圣安娜号*，看来它已平安抵达加的斯，于是我决意立刻动身去与主人会合。我们离加的斯还有相当一段路，在瓜达尔基维尔河（Guadalquivir）以北的海岸上，因此我想即刻启程，好赶完这么远的路。我歇了两天恢复体力，便同一名顺路的水手结伴，动身往桑卢卡尔（Sanlúcar）去。二十七日早晨我们渡过河，随后沿着海岸继续步行。同伴是个乐呵呵的和气人，只是我痛惜马西亚尔之死，又为新近目睹的惨象所压，以那样的心境，这一路能有几分惬意已属难得。我们一路走着，谈论那场海战和接踵而至的沉船之祸。

===Original===
“A very good sailor was that old cripple,” said my companion. “But what possessed him to go to sea again with more than sixty years on his shoulders? It served him right to come to a bad end.”

“He was a brave seaman,” said I, “and had such a passion for fighting that even his infirmities could not keep him quiet when he had made up his mind to join the fleet.”

“Well, I have had enough of it for my part,” said the sailor. “I do not want to see any more fighting at sea. The King pays us badly, and then, if you are maimed or crippled—goodbye to you—I know nothing about you—I never set eyes on you in my life.—Perhaps you don’t believe me when I tell you the King pays his men so badly? But I can tell you this: most of the officers in command of the ships that went into action on the 21st had seen no pay for months. Only last year there was a navy captain at Cádiz who went as waiter in an inn because he had no other way of keeping himself or his children. His friends found him out though he tried to conceal his misery, and they succeeded at last in getting him out of his degrading position. Such things do not happen in any other country in the world; and then we are horrified at finding ourselves beaten by the English! As to the arsenals, I will say nothing about them; they are empty and it is of no use to hope for money from Madrid—not a *cuarto* comes this way. All the King’s revenues are spent in paying the court officials, and chief among them the Prince of Peace; who gets 40,000 dollars as Counsellor of the Realm, Secretary of State, Captain-General, and Sergeant-Major of the Guards.—No, say I, I have had enough of serving the King. I am going home to my wife and children, for I have served my time and in a few days they must give me my papers.”

===Chinese===
“那个老瘸子倒真是条好水手，”我的同伴说，“可是背上都压着六十多年了，是什么鬼迷了心窍，还要再下海？落得个坏下场，也是他活该。”

“他是个勇敢的水手，”我说，“他嗜战成癖，一旦打定主意要投舰队，连一身伤残都拴他不住。”

“唉，要说我，可是受够了，”水手说，“海上打仗，我再也不想见了。国王给我们的饷本来就菲薄，再说，你要是缺了胳膊少了腿——那就再会吧——我不认得你——我这辈子没见过你这号人。——我跟你讲国王给手下人的饷多么菲薄，你兴许不信？可有一样我能告诉你：二十一日临阵的各条军舰，统兵的军官大半有好几个月没见着饷银了。就在去年，加的斯有个海军上校，因为再没有别的法子养活自己和儿女，进客店当跑堂去了。朋友们虽然他百般遮掩窘况，还是识破了，末了总算把他从那丢人的差事里搭救出来。这种事在世界上别的哪一国都不会有；到头来我们叫英国人打败了，还大惊小怪！至于各处军械库，我干脆不提也罢；都是空的，指望马德里拨钱也是白指望——连一个*夸尔托*也到不了这边。国王的全部进项都拿去支付宫廷官员的薪俸了，头一份就是那位和平亲王；他兼着王国枢密官、国务大臣、大将军和近卫军军士长，一年拿四万银元。——不，我说，替国王当差我算当够了。我要回家守着老婆孩子去，我的兵役已经服满，过不了几天，他们就得把退役文书发给我。”

===Original===
“But you have nothing to complain of friend,” said I, “since you were on board the *Rayo* which hardly did any fighting.”

“I was not in the *Rayo* but in the *Bahama*, one of the ships that fought hardest and longest.”

“She was taken and her captain killed, if I remember rightly.”

“Aye, so it was,” he said. “I could cry over it when I think of him—Don Dionisio Alcalá Galiano, the bravest seaman in the fleet. Well, he was a stern commander; he never overlooked the smallest fault, and yet his very severity made us love him all the more, for a captain who is feared for his severity—if his severity is unfailingly just—inspires respect and wins the affection of his men. I can honestly say that a more noble and generous gentleman than Don Dionisio Alcalá Galiano was never born. And when he wanted to do a civility to his friends he did not do it by halves; once, out in Havana he spent ten thousand dollars on a supper he gave on board ship.”

===Chinese===
“可你没什么好抱怨的呀，朋友，”我说，“你不是在*拉约号*上吗，那条船几乎没怎么打。”

“我不在*拉约号*，我在*巴哈马号*，那是打得最凶、撑得最久的船之一。”

“它叫人家俘了，舰长也阵亡了，要是我没记错的话。”

“是这么回事，”他说，“一想起他，我就想哭——堂迪奥尼西奥·阿尔卡拉·加利亚诺（Don Dionisio Alcalá Galiano），全舰队最勇敢的水手。唉，他是个严厉的长官；再小的过失也逃不过他；可正是那份严厉，反倒叫我们越发爱戴他；因为一位因严厉而叫人惧怕的舰长——倘若他的严厉永远公正无偏——自会赢得敬重，博得部下的爱戴。我可以凭良心说，比堂迪奥尼西奥·阿尔卡拉·加利亚诺更高贵、更慷慨的绅士，世上还不曾生出来过。他要向朋友们表示情谊，也从不做半吊子；有一回在哈瓦那，他在舰上设一席晚宴，一挥就是一万银元。”

===Original===
“He was a first-rate seaman too, I heard.”

“Ah, that he was. And he was more learned than Merlin and all the Fathers of the Church. He made no end of maps, and discovered Lord knows how many countries out there, where it is as hot as hell itself! And then they send men like these out to fight and to be killed like a parcel of cabin-boys. I will just tell you what happened on board the *Bahama*. As soon as the fighting began Don Dionisio Alcalá Galiano knew we must be beaten on account of that infernal trick of turning the ships round—we were in the reserve and had been in the rear. Nelson, who was certainly no fool, looked along our line, and he said: ‘If we cut them through at two separate points, and keep them between two fires, hardly a ship will escape me.’ And so he did, blast him; and as our line was so long the head could never help the tail. He fought us in detachments, attacking us in two wedge-shaped columns which, as I have heard say, were the tactics adopted by the great Moorish general, Alexander the Great, and now used by Napoleon. It is very certain, at any rate, that they got round us and cut us in three, and fought us ship to ship in such a manner that we could not support or help each other; every Spaniard had to deal with three or four Englishmen.

“Well, so you see the *Bahama* was one of the first to be under fire. Galiano reviewed the crew at noon, went round the gun-decks, and made us a speech in which he said: ‘Gentlemen, you all know that our flag is nailed to the mast.’ Yes, we all knew the sort of man our Captain was, and we were not at all surprised to hear it. Then he turned to the captain of the marines, Don Alonso Butron, ‘I charge you to defend it,’ he said. ‘No Galiano ever surrenders and no Butron should either.’”

===Chinese===
“我还听说，他也是个头一等的水手。”

“啊，可不是嘛。他比梅林（Merlin）和教会里所有的教父都博学。他绘的地图没有个数，在天边那些热得跟地狱没两样的地方，发现的天知道多少国土！可是到头来，却派这样的人出去打仗，像一小队见习水手似的叫人打死。我就把*巴哈马号*上的事讲给你听吧。仗一开打，堂迪奥尼西奥·阿尔卡拉·加利亚诺就知道我们必败无疑，全坏在那套把军舰掉头转向的鬼名堂上——我们原编在后备，一直落在队尾。纳尔逊那家伙可绝不是傻子，他顺着我们的战列一打量，说道：‘要是从两处把他们拦腰截断，叫他们两头挨打，几乎没有一条船能从我手里逃掉。’他就真这么干了，这挨千刀的；我们的战列又拉得太长，首尾根本照应不上。他分股打我们，用两支楔形纵队来攻；据我听人说，那正是伟大的摩尔人将军亚历山大大帝用过的战术，如今又教拿破仑学了去。无论如何，有一点确凿无疑：他们绕到我们外围，把我们切成三段，一条对一条地打，打得我们彼此谁也支援不上、救护不了；每个西班牙人得当三四个英国人的对手。

“唉，你瞧，*巴哈马号*就是最先挨炮火的几条之一。正午时分，加利亚诺检阅了全舰人员，走遍各层炮甲板，对我们训话道：‘诸位，你们都知道，咱们的舰旗钉死在桅杆上了。’是的，我们都知道我们舰长是何等样人，听了这话毫不意外。随后他转向海军陆战队队长堂阿隆索·布特龙（Don Alonso Butron）：‘我把卫旗之责托付给你，’他说，‘加利亚诺家的人从不投降，布特龙家的人也不该投降。’”

===Original===
“‘What a pity it is,’ said I, ‘that such men should not have had a leader worthy of such courage, since they could not themselves conduct the fleet.’”

“Aye, it is a pity, and you shall hear what happened. The battle began, and you know something of what it was like if you were on board the *Trinidad*. The ships riddled us with broadsides to port and starboard. The wounded fell like flies from the very first, and the captain first had a bad bruise on his foot and then a splinter struck his head and hurt him badly. But do you think he would give in, or submit to be plastered with ointment? Not a bit of it; he stayed on the quarterdeck, just as if nothing had happened, though many a man he loved truly fell close to him never to stand up again. Alcalá Galiano gave his orders and directed his guns as if we had been firing a salute at a review. A spent ball knocked his telescope out of his hand and that made him laugh. I fancy I can see him now; the blood from his wound stained his uniform and his hands and he cared no more than if it had been drops of saltwater splashed up from the sea. He was a man of great spirit and a hasty temper; he shouted out his orders so positively that if we had not obeyed them because it was our duty, we should have done so out of sheer alarm.—But suddenly it was all over with him.—He was struck in the head by a shot and instantly killed.

“The fight was not at an end, but all our heart in it was gone. When our beloved captain fell the officers covered his body that we men might not see it, but we all knew at once what had happened, and after a short and desperate struggle for the honor of our flag, the *Bahama* surrendered to the English who carried her off to Gibraltar if she did not go to the bottom on the way, as I rather suspect she did.”

===Chinese===
“‘真可惜啊，’我说，‘这样的人既然自己统领不了舰队，竟就没有一位配得上这份忠勇的统帅。’”

“是啊，可惜；你听我说后来的事吧。仗打起来了，你要是在*圣特立尼达号*上待过，那滋味也略知一二。各条敌舰左舷右舷轮番齐射，把我们轰得千疮百孔。伤员从一开头就像苍蝇似的往下倒；舰长先是脚上重重挨了一下，青肿了一大块，随后一块破片又打中他的头，伤得不轻。可你以为他肯让步，肯服服帖帖让人给他涂药膏吗？一点也不；他照旧站在后甲板上，好像什么事都没有似的，尽管他真心疼爱的不少人就在近旁倒下，再没有起来。阿尔卡拉·加利亚诺发号施令、指挥开炮，从容得仿佛我们在阅兵式上鸣放礼炮。一发泄了劲的实心弹把他手里的望远镜打飞，反倒把他逗笑了。如今我仿佛还能看见他；伤口的血染了他的军服和双手，他毫不在意，只当是海上溅起来的几滴咸水。他是个心气极高、火性极烈的人；口令喊得斩钉截铁，我们若不是出于本分服从，单凭那一声声的威势也得照办。——可是突然之间，他一切都完了。——一发炮弹打中他的头，登时殒命。

“仗还没有打完，可我们在仗里的那颗心已经死了。我们敬爱的舰长倒下时，军官们拿东西遮住他的遗体，不叫我们这些人看见，可大家立刻就明白出了什么事；为着舰旗的荣誉又拼了一场短促而殊死的挣扎之后，*巴哈马号*终于向英国人投降，被他们掳往直布罗陀——我倒相当疑心，它没等到那儿，半路上就沉了底。”

===Original===
After giving this history and telling me how he had been transferred from the *Bahama* to the *Santa Ana*, my companion sighed deeply and was silent for some time. However, as the way was long and dull I tried to reopen the conversation and I began telling him what I myself had seen, and how I had at last been put on board the *Rayo* with young Malespina.

“Ah!” said he. “Was he a young artillery officer who was transferred to the sloop to be taken to shore on the night of the 23rd?”

“The very same,” said I. “But no one has been able to tell me for certain what became of him.”

“He was one of a party in the second boat which could not get to shore; some of those who were whole and strong contrived to escape, and among them that young officer’s father; but all the wounded were drowned, as you may easily suppose, as the poor souls of course could not swim to land.”

===Chinese===
他讲完这段来历，又告诉我他后来如何从*巴哈马号*转到*圣安娜号*，随后深深叹了口气，半晌无言。可是路长人闷，我设法重新挑起话头，对他讲起我自己的见闻，讲我最后怎样同年轻的马莱斯皮纳一道被送上了*拉约号*。

“啊！”他说，“你说的是不是那位年轻炮兵军官，二十三日夜里被转到单桅小帆船上、要送上岸去的？”

“正是他，”我说，“可是谁也没法确切告诉我他的下场。”

“他就在第二条小艇的那一伙人里，那条艇没能靠上岸；肢体完好、气力壮实的几个脱了身，那年轻军官的父亲也在其中；可伤员全都淹死了，这你可想而知，那些可怜人当然没法游到岸上。”

===Original===
I was shocked to hear of Don Rafael’s death, and the thought of the grief it would be to my hapless and adored little mistress quite overcame me, choking every mean and jealous feeling.

“What a dreadful thing!” I exclaimed. “And is it my misfortune to have to carry the news to his sorrowing friends? But, tell me, are you certain of the facts?”

“I saw his father with my own eyes, lamenting bitterly and telling all the details of the catastrophe with such distress it was enough to break your heart. From what he said he seemed to have saved everybody on board the boat, and he declared that if he had saved his son it would have been at the cost of the lives of all the others, so he chose, on the whole, to preserve the lives of the greatest number, even in sacrificing that of his son, and he did so. He must be a singularly humane man, and wonderfully brave and dexterous.” But I was so deeply distressed that I could not discuss the subject. Marcial dead, Malespina dead! What terrible news to take home to my master’s house. For a moment my mind was almost made up not to return to Cádiz; I would leave it to chance or to public rumor to carry the report to the sad hearts that were waiting in such painful suspense. However, I was bound to present myself before Don Alonso and give him some account of my proceedings.

===Chinese===
听得堂拉斐尔的死讯，我大为震动；一想到这将给我那命薄而受我仰慕的小女主人带来何等的悲痛，我便心如刀绞，一切褊狭嫉妒的念头全给堵了回去。

“多可怕的事啊！”我叫道，“难道偏偏轮到我来把这消息带给他悲伤的亲友？可是告诉我，你说的千真万确吗？”

“我亲眼见过他父亲，恸哭不已，一件件讲着那场大祸的细节，那份悲恸叫人心都要碎了。照他的话说，艇上的人仿佛全是他救的；他声言，倘若要救他的儿子，就得拿艇上其余人的性命作抵，于是他权衡再三，选择保全最大多数的性命，宁可牺牲自己儿子的性命，他也果真这么做了。这人必是个异乎寻常的仁厚之人，而且勇敢和干练得出奇。”我心里悲苦至极，实在没法再谈这个话题。马西亚尔死了，马莱斯皮纳也死了！要把这样的消息带回主人家去，多可怕啊。有一瞬间，我几乎拿定主意不回加的斯了；就任凭机缘或街巷风声，把这凶信带给那些在痛苦悬望中守候着的伤心人吧。然而我总得到堂阿隆索跟前报到，把自己的种种经历交代一番。

===Original===
At length we reached Rota and there embarked for Cádiz. It is impossible to describe the commotion produced by the report of the disaster to our fleet. News of the details had come in by degrees, and by this time the fate of most of our ships was known, though what had become of many men and even whole crews had not been ascertained. The streets were full of distressing scenes at every turn, where someone who had come off scot-free stood telling off the deaths he knew of, and the names of those who would be seen no more. The populace crowded down to the quays to see the wounded as they came on shore, hoping to recognize a father, husband, son or brother. There were episodes of frantic joy mingled with shrieks of dismay and bitter cries of disappointment. Too often were hopes deceived and fears confirmed, and the losers in this fearful lottery were far more numerous than the winners. The bodies thrown up on the shore put an end to the suspense of many families, while others still hoped to find those they had lost among the prisoners taken to Gibraltar.

To the honor of Cádiz be it said never did a community devote itself with greater willingness to the care of the wounded, making no distinctions between friends and foes but hoisting the standard, as it were, of universal and comprehensive charity. Collingwood, in his narrative, does justice to this generosity on the part of my fellow-countrymen. The magnitude of the disaster had deadened all resentment, but is it not sad to reflect that it is only in misfortune that men are truly brothers?

In Cádiz I saw collected in the harbor the whole results of the conflict which previously, as an actor in it, I had only partially understood, since the length of the line and the manœuvring of the vessels would not allow me to see everything that happened. As I now learned—besides the *Trinidad*—the *Argonauta*, 92 guns, Captain Don Antonio Pareja, and the *San Augustín*, 80 guns, Don Felipe Cagigal, had been sunk. Gravina had got back into Cádiz with the *Príncipe de Astúrias*, as well as the *Montañes*, 80 guns, commanded by Alcedo, who with his second officer Castaños, had been killed; the *San Justo*, 76 guns, Captain Don Miguel Gastón; the *San Leandro*, 74, Captain Don José Quevedo; the *San Francisco*, 74, Don Luis Flores; and the *Rayo*, 100, commanded by Macdonell. Four of these had gone out again on the 23rd to recapture the vessels making for Gibraltar; and of these, two, the *San Francisco* and the *Rayo* were wrecked on the coast. So, too, was the *Monarca*, 74 guns, under Argumosa, and the *Neptuno*, 80 guns; and her heroic commander, Don Cayetano Valdés, who had previously distinguished himself at Cape St. Vincent, narrowly escaped with his life. The *Bahama* had surrendered but went to pieces before she could be got into Gibraltar; the *San Ildefonso*, 74 guns, Captain Vargas, was taken to England, while the *San Juan Nepomuceno* was left for many years at Gibraltar, where she was regarded as an object of veneration and curiosity. The *Santa Ana* had come safely into Cádiz the very night we were taken off her.

===Chinese===
我们终于到了罗塔（Rota），从那里搭船去加的斯。舰队遭难的噩耗在城里激起的骚动，实在无从描摹。详情陆续传来，到这时，我们大部分军舰的下落已见分晓，只是许多官兵乃至整队水手的存亡仍旧无从查考。大街上触处皆是伤心惨目的场面：总有那么一个全须全尾逃回来的人，站在那里一件件数说他知道的死讯，报出那些再也见不着的人的名字。百姓成群涌到码头上，看伤员们上岸，指望认出一位父亲、丈夫、儿子或兄弟。颠狂的欢喜同惊怖的尖号、失望的哀哭交织在一起。希望落空、惧恐坐实的情形比比皆是，在这场可怕的抽签里，输家远远多过赢家。海浪抛上岸来的尸体，了断了许多家庭的悬念；另一些人家，则还指望在被押往直布罗陀的俘虏中间，寻回自己失落的人。

应当为加的斯说句公道话：再没有一个地方的人，比它更甘心情愿地献身于救护伤员，不分敌友，一视同仁，仿佛高高竖起了一面无所不包的博爱旗帜。科林伍德在他的记述里，对我的同胞这份慷慨侠肠给了公道的评价。灾祸之惨重麻钝了一切怨愤；然而细想起来岂不可悲：唯有在不幸之中，人才真正是弟兄？

在加的斯，我在港湾里看到了这场大战结出的全部果实；先前我身为局中之人，只因战列绵长、舰船调动不歇，所能明白的只是零碎几处。我这才知道：除了*圣特立尼达号*，*阿尔戈纳塔号*——九十二门炮，舰长堂安东尼奥·帕雷哈（Don Antonio Pareja）——和*圣阿古斯丁号*——八十门炮，堂费利佩·卡希加尔（Don Felipe Cagigal）——也沉没了。格拉维纳带着*阿斯图里亚斯亲王号*回到了加的斯，随行的还有*蒙塔涅斯号*，八十门炮，由阿尔塞多指挥，他同他的副官卡斯塔尼奥斯（Castaños）一同阵亡；*圣胡斯托号*，七十六门炮，舰长堂米格尔·加斯通（Don Miguel Gastón）；*圣莱安德罗号*，七十四门，舰长堂何塞·克维多（Don José Quevedo）；*圣弗朗西斯科号*（San Francisco），七十四门，堂路易斯·弗洛雷斯（Don Luis Flores）；还有*拉约号*，一百门，由麦克唐内尔（Macdonell）指挥。这几条船里有四条在二十三日再度出海，去夺回那些正驶往直布罗陀的舰只；其中*圣弗朗西斯科号*和*拉约号*两条在海岸上失事。同样失事的，还有阿尔古莫萨（Argumosa）指挥的*蒙纳尔卡号*（Monarca），七十四门炮，以及*内普图诺号*，八十门炮；后者的英勇舰长堂卡耶塔诺·巴尔德斯（Don Cayetano Valdés）——他先前已在圣文森特角大显身手——险些送了命，总算逃得性命。*巴哈马号*投降了，可还没等弄进直布罗陀就碎裂了；*圣伊尔德丰索号*，七十四门炮，舰长巴尔加斯（Vargas），被掳往英国；*圣胡安·内波穆塞诺号*则一连多年留驻直布罗陀，被人当作一件可敬又可奇之物。*圣安娜号*在我们离船的那天夜里，就安然驶进了加的斯。

===Original===
The English too lost some fine ships, and not a few of their gallant officers shared Nelson’s glorious fate.

With regard to the French it need not be said that they had suffered as severely as we had. With the exception of the four ships that withdrew under Dumanoir without showing fight—a stain which the Imperial navy could not for a long time wipe out—our allies behaved splendidly. Villeneuve, only caring to efface in one day the remembrance of all his mistakes, fought desperately to the last and was carried off a prisoner to Gibraltar. Many of their officers were taken with him, and very many were killed. Their vessels shared all our risks and dangers; some got off with Gravina, some were taken and several were wrecked on the coast. The *Achille* blew up, as I have said, in the midst of the action.

But in spite of all these disasters, Spain had paid dearer for the war than her haughty ally. France had lost the flower of her navy indeed, but at that very time Napoleon had won a glorious victory on land. His army had marched with wonderful rapidity from the shores of the English Channel across Europe, and was carrying out his colossal schemes in the campaign against Austria. It was on the 20th of October, the day before Trafalgar, that Napoleon, at the camp at Ulm, looked on as the Austrian troops marched past, while their officers delivered up their swords; only two months later, on the 2nd of December, he won, on the field of Austerlitz, the greatest of his many victories.

These triumphs consoled France for the defeat of Trafalgar; Napoleon silenced the newspapers, forbidding them to discuss the matter; and when the victory of his implacable enemies, the English, was reported to him he simply shrugged his shoulders and said: “I cannot be everywhere at once.”

===Chinese===
英国人也损失了几条好船，他们不少英勇的军官，也落得纳尔逊那般光荣的下场。

至于法国人，不必说，他们受的创痛同我们一样深重。除了迪马努瓦麾下不发一炮便撤走的那四条船——帝国海军久久洗刷不掉的一桩污点——我们的盟友打得极其出色。维尔纳夫一心只想在一天之内抹尽平生过失的记忆，殊死战斗到最后，被当作俘虏押往直布罗陀。他们的许多军官同他一道被俘，更有许许多多人阵亡。他们的军舰分担了我们全部的风险与危难：有的随格拉维纳脱身，有的被俘，有几条在海岸失事。*阿希尔号*呢，如我所说，在海战正酣之际炸毁了。

然而，尽管遭了这一切灾殃，西班牙为这场战争付的代价，还是比较那骄横的盟友更为惨重。法国固然损失了它的海军精华，可恰在此时，拿破仑在陆上赢了一场辉煌的大捷。他的大军以惊人的神速从英吉利海峡边横贯欧洲而来，正在对奥地利的战事中推行他那宏大的方略。就在十月二十日、特拉法尔加的前一天，拿破仑在乌尔姆（Ulm）军营里，眼看奥地利军队列队走过，他们的军官纷纷呈上佩剑；仅仅两个月之后，十二月二日，他又在奥斯特利茨（Austerlitz）战场上，赢了他无数大捷中最伟大的一仗。

这些凯旋安慰了法国在特拉法尔加的失败；拿破仑封了各报的口，不许他们议论这件事；当他那不共戴天的仇敌英国人得胜的消息报了上来，他只是耸了耸肩，说：“我不可能同时处处都在。”

## XVII

===Original===
I postponed the fatal hour when I must face my master as long as possible, but at last my destitute condition, without money and without a home, brought me to the point. As I went to the house of Doña Flora my heart beat so violently that I had to stop for breath at every step. The terrible shock I was about to give the family by announcing young Malespina’s death weighed so terribly on my soul that I could not have felt more crushed and guilty if I had myself been the occasion of it. At last however, I went in. My presence in the courtyard caused an immense sensation. I heard heavy steps hurrying along the upper galleries and I had not been able to speak a word before I felt myself in a close embrace. I at once recognized Doña Flora, with more paint on her face than if it had been a picture, but seriously discomposed in effect by the good old soul’s delight at seeing me once more. But all the fond names she lavished on me﻿—her dear boy, her pet, her little angel﻿—could not make me smile. I went upstairs, everyone was in a bustle of excitement. I heard my master exclaim: “Oh! thank God! he is safe.” I went into the drawing-room, and there it was Doña Francisca who came forward, asking with mortal anxiety﻿—“And Don Rafael?﻿—Where is Don Rafael?”

But for some minutes I could not speak; my voice failed me, I had not courage to tell the fatal news. They questioned me eagerly and I saw Doña Rosita come in from an adjoining room, pale, heavy-eyed, and altered by the anguish she had gone through. At the sight of my young mistress I burst into tears, and there was then no need for words. Rosita gave a terrible cry and fell senseless; her father and mother flew to her side, smothering their own grief, while Doña Flora melted into tears and took me aside to assure herself that I, at any rate, had returned whole in every part.

===Chinese===
我把必须去面对主人的那个要命的时刻，能拖多久便拖了多久，但到头来，我身无分文、无家可归的窘境还是把我逼到了那一步。往堂娜·弗洛拉家去的路上，我的心跳得那样剧烈，每走一步都得停下来喘口气。我将要用小马莱斯皮纳的死讯给这家人带来可怕的打击，这份沉重压在我心上，压得那样厉害，仿佛那祸事当真是我亲手酿成的，我也不至于觉得更沉重、更愧疚了。然而我终于还是走了进去。我一出现在院子里，便引起了一阵大大的骚动。我听见沉重的脚步沿着楼上的回廊奔来，我还没能说出一句话，就发觉自己已被紧紧搂进了一个怀抱。我立刻认出了堂娜·弗洛拉——她脸上的脂粉涂得比一幅画像还厚——可这位好心老太太重逢的欢喜，也着实把她搅得仪态尽失。但她朝我倾泻的那一串亲昵称呼——她的心肝、她的宝贝、她的小天使——都不能使我露出一丝笑容。我上了楼，人人都在激动中忙作一团。我听见我的主人高声喊道：“噢！感谢上帝！他平安无事。”我走进客厅，迎面走来的是堂娜·弗朗西斯卡，她怀着揪心的焦虑问——“堂拉斐尔呢？——堂拉斐尔在哪儿？”

可是有好几分钟我说不出话来；嗓子不听使唤，我没有勇气说出那个致命的消息。他们急切地追问，我看见堂娜·罗西塔从隔壁房间走进来，脸色苍白，眼皮沉重，被熬过的苦楚折磨得脱了形。一看见我年轻的女主人，我的眼泪便夺眶而出，这时已无需言语了。罗西塔发出一声可怕的惨叫，昏死过去；她的父亲和母亲飞扑到她身边，强压着各自的悲痛，堂娜·弗洛拉则泪如雨下，把我拉到一旁，要确认至少我自己是全须全尾地回来了。

===Original===
“Tell me,” she said, “how did he come by his death? I felt sure of it﻿—I told Paca so; but she would only say her prayers and believed that so she could save him. As if God could be troubled with such matters.﻿—And you are safe and sound﻿—what a comfort! No damage anywhere?”

It is impossible to describe the consternation of the whole household. For a quarter of an hour nothing was to be heard but crying, lamentation, and sobbing; for Malespina’s mother had come to Cádiz and was also in the house. But how mysterious are the ways of Providence in working out its ends! About a quarter of an hour, as I say, had elapsed since I had told them the news when a loud assertive voice fell on my ear. It was that of Don José María, shouting in the courtyard, calling his wife, Don Alonso, and Rosita. That which first struck me was that his tones seemed just as strident and cheerful as ever, which I thought very indecorous after the misfortune that had happened. We all ran to meet him, and I stared to see him radiant and smiling.

===Chinese===
“告诉我，”她说，“他是怎么死的？我早料到了——我跟帕卡这么说过；可她只会念她的祷告，以为这样就能救得了他。好像上帝会为这种事操心似的。——倒是你平平安安——真叫人宽心！哪儿都没伤着吧？”

这全家上下的悲恸惶乱，实在无法描画。一刻钟里，除了哭喊、哀叹和抽泣，什么也听不见；因为小马莱斯皮纳的母亲已来到加的斯，也在那座宅子里。可是天意成全其安排，途径何等神秘！我说过，自我报出凶讯后过去了约莫一刻钟，一个洪亮自负的声音便落进我的耳朵。那是堂何塞·马里亚的嗓音，他在院子里大声喊叫，唤着他的妻子、堂阿隆索和罗西塔。头一样叫我吃惊的是，他的声调似乎一如既往地又尖利又欢快，出了这样的大祸，我觉得实在不成体统。我们都跑出去迎他，却看得目瞪口呆：他红光满面，笑吟吟的。

===Original===
“But poor Don Rafael.﻿ …” said my master.

“Safe and sound,” replied Don José. “That is to say not exactly sound, but out of danger, for his wound is nothing to be anxious about. The fool of a surgeon said he would die, but I knew better. What do I care for surgeons! I cured him, gentlemen﻿—I, I myself, by a new treatment which no one knows of but myself.”

These words, which so suddenly and completely altered the aspect of affairs, astounded the audience. The greatest joy took the place of grief and dismay; and to wind up, as soon as their agitation allowed them to think of the delusion they had suffered under, they scolded me soundly for the fright I had given them. I excused myself by saying that I had only repeated the tale as it was told to me, and Don José flew into a great rage, calling me a rascal, an imposter, and a busybody.

===Chinese===
“可是可怜的堂拉斐尔……”我的主人说。

“安然无恙，”堂何塞答道，“也就是说，‘无恙’谈不上，但已脱离危险，因为他的伤没有什么可焦虑的。那个蠢货外科医生说他必死，可我心里比他明白。外科医生算什么东西！是我治好了他，诸位——是我，我亲自治的，用的是一种除我之外无人知晓的新疗法。”

这几句话如此突然而又彻底地改变了事情的面貌，把满屋子的人都惊呆了。极大的欢喜取代了悲痛与沮丧；而到了末了，等到他们的激动平息下来，想起了自己方才受的那场蒙骗，便把我狠狠数落了一顿，怪我吓了他们一大跳。我替自己分辩，说我不过是把听来的话照原样复述了一遍，堂何塞顿时勃然大怒，骂我是恶棍、骗子、多管闲事的家伙。

===Original===
It was happily true that Don Rafael was alive and out of danger; he had remained with some friends at Sanlúcar while his father had come to Cádiz to fetch his mother to see him. My readers will hardly believe in the origin of the mistake which had led me to announce the young man’s death in such perfect good faith; though a few may have been led to suspect that some tremendous fib of the old man’s must have given rise to the report that reached me. And so it was, neither more nor less. I heard all about it at Sanlúcar whither I went with the family. Don José María had invented a whole romance of devotion and skill on his own part, and had related more than once the history of his son’s death, inventing so many dramatic details that for a few days he figured as a hero, and had been the object of universal admiration for his humanity and courage. His story was that the boat had upset, and that as the choice lay between rescuing his son and saving all the others he had chosen the latter alternative as the most magnanimous and philanthropical. This romance he dressed up in so many interesting, and at the same time probable circumstances that it could not fail to be believed. The falsehood was of course very soon found out, and his success was of brief duration, but not before the story had come to my ears and put me under the necessity of reporting it to the family. Though I knew very well how absolutely mendacious old Malespina could be, I had never dreamed of his lying about so serious a matter.

===Chinese===
堂拉斐尔还活着、已脱险，这是千真万确的喜事；他留在桑卢卡尔（Sanlúcar）几位朋友那里，他的父亲则来加的斯接他母亲去探望他。我的读者们恐怕很难相信，那个使我如此诚心诚意地宣布年轻人死讯的误会，究竟源出何处；尽管少数人也许已经疑心，必是老头子的一通弥天大谎，才引出了传到我耳中的那番说法。事情正是如此，不多不少，分毫不差。我随这一家人去了桑卢卡尔，在那里听得了全部原委。堂何塞·马里亚编出了一整篇标榜自己赤诚与才干的传奇，不止一次地讲述他儿子罹难的经过，杜撰出那么多的戏剧性细节，以致有那么几天他被当成了英雄，他的人道与勇气成了众口交誉的对象。他讲的故事是：小艇翻了，在救自己的儿子和救其余所有人之间只能二者择一，他选择了后者，视之为最宽宏大量、最仁慈为怀的一途。这篇传奇被他敷上了那么多既有趣味、又近情理的情节，不由得人不信。谎言当然很快便被戳穿，他的风光为期甚短，但在戳穿之前，那故事已传进我的耳朵，害得我不得不向这家人报了凶讯。我虽然深知老马莱斯皮纳撒起谎来是何等彻底，却做梦也想不到，他会在这么严重的事情上撒谎。

---

===Original===
When all this excitement was over my master sank into deep melancholy; he would scarcely speak and seemed as though his soul, having no illusions left, had closed accounts with the world and was only waiting to take its departure. The absence of Marcial was to him the loss of the only companion of his childish old age; he had no one now to fight mimic battles with, and he gave himself up to dull sorrow. Nor did Doña Francisca spare him any drop of mortification, seeing him in this crestfallen state. I heard her the same day saying spitefully:

“A pretty mess you have made of it! What do you think of yourself now? Now are you satisfied? Go, oh go by all means and join the fleet! Was I right or was I wrong? If you would only have listened to me. But you have had a lesson I hope; you see now how God has punished you.”

===Chinese===
等这场骚动全部过去，我的主人便陷入了深深的忧郁；他几乎不言不语，那模样仿佛他的灵魂既然再无幻念，索性同这个世界清了账，只待动身离去。马西亚尔的缺席，对他说来就是失去了他孩童一般的晚年里唯一的伴侣；如今再没有人陪他演练那些模拟海战了，他便把自己整个交给了沉沉的哀伤。而堂娜·弗朗西斯卡眼见他这副垂头丧气的光景，也不肯少让他受一分难堪。就在当天，我听见她刻薄地说：

“瞧你把事情搅成了什么样子！如今你把自己看作什么了？这下你可满意了？去呀，噢，千万再去参加舰队呀！我当初是对还是错？你要是肯听我一言就好了。不过这一回你总算得了个教训，但愿如此；你瞧瞧，上帝是怎么罚你的。”

===Original===
“Woman, leave me in peace,” said my master sadly.

“And now we are left without any fleet at all, and without sailors, and we shall soon find ourselves ruined out of hand if we keep up our alliance with the French.﻿—Please God those gentry may not pay us out for their misfortunes. Señor Villeneuve!﻿—he has covered himself with glory indeed! And Gravina again! If he had opposed the scheme of taking the fleet out, as Churruca and Alcalá Galiano did, he might have prevented this heartbreaking catastrophe.”

“Woman, woman﻿—what do you know about it? Do not annoy me,” said Don Alonso quite vexed.

===Chinese===
“婆娘，让我清静清静，”我的主人悲哀地说。

“如今咱们是连一支舰队也没有了，水手也没有了，要是再同法国人结盟下去，用不了多久就得一败涂地。——求上帝保佑，别让那帮老爷拿咱们替他们的厄运出气。维尔纳夫先生！——他可真是荣光满身呐！还有格拉维纳！当初他要是像丘鲁卡和阿尔卡拉·加利亚诺那样反对出海的方案，本可以拦住这场令人心碎的大劫难。”

“婆娘，婆娘——这种事你懂什么？别来烦我，”堂阿隆索相当恼火地说。

===Original===
“What do I know about it? More than you do. Yes﻿—I repeat it: Gravina may be a worthy gentleman and as brave as you please; but in this case, much good he has done!”

“He did his duty. You would have liked us all to be set down as cowards, I suppose?”

“Cowards, no﻿—but prudent. It is as I say and repeat: the fleet ought never to have gone out of Cádiz just to humor the whims and conceit of Villeneuve.

===Chinese===
“我懂什么？比你知道的多。是的——我再说一遍：格拉维纳也许是个可敬的君子，要多勇敢有多勇敢；可在这件事上，他可真是帮了大忙啊！”

“他尽了他的本分。我猜，你是巴不得咱们全被说成懦夫，是不是？”

“懦夫，倒也不是——是谨慎的人。我还是那句话，再重复一遍：舰队根本就不该驶出加的斯，只为着迎合维尔纳夫的任性与自负。

===Original===
Everyone here knew that Gravina, like the others, was of opinion that it ought to stop in the bay. But Villeneuve had made up his mind to it, intending to hit a blow that might restore him to his master’s favor, and he worked on our Spanish pride. It seems that one of the reasons Gravina gave was the badness of the weather, and that he said, looking at the barometer in the cabin: ‘Do you not see that the barometer foretells foul weather? Do you not see how it has gone down?’ And then Villeneuve said drily: ‘What is gone down here is courage!’ At such an insult Gravina stood up, blind with rage, and threw the French Admiral’s own conduct at Finisterre in his teeth.
